# vego roadmap

What is planned and not built, and the decisions behind what is built. `docs/verification.md` describes what exists today for someone using it, and the README gives a short overview. This file is for someone changing the verifier.

## Target

vego is meant for use on real projects, not only textbook algorithms. The first target is the code found in services, command-line tools, infrastructure, parsers, and data structures: logic over integers, strings used as tokens, slices, structs, and errors, calling into the standard library and third-party modules.

Not a target: numerical, scientific, graphics, and machine-learning code, where floating point is the core logic, and code whose point is formatting or parsing text exactly.

Design choices should be judged against the first group. A feature that only helps the second can wait.

## Go that stops a function from being verified

Each of these makes vego report the function that uses it as `cannot verify`, or keeps a true contract from being proved. They are listed in the order they are meant to be built. Since the trial on real code (F16, 2026-11-20; see Before it is called ready) the order rests on what blocked the trial's 152 functions, and each entry carries its numbers there: functions blocked, of which the entry was the first message, and of which it was the only blocker, with the packages and the Target kinds (parser, data structure, command, service) it appeared in. A count of 0 alone means removing the entry completes nothing by itself. `/home/spachava/dev/vego-trial/measure.sh` reruns the trial and prints the functions proved per package, and the functions that changed against the trial's classification; it is the measure of progress after each feature.

The origin of errors decided along the path, placed before the first of them by the orchestrator after F19, is built (F20, 2026-11-20; see Errors, Origin is decided along the path): `err` can be reused for a call whose errors nothing vouches for after it is wrapped, as `guide/files.go` WriteReport shows. The trial rerun gave 60 of 152, as before, with every diagnostic of the trial the same at 9c39fbb and after: no function of the trial met the rule over every assignment once the shipped entries were in, and memory.loadGraph is stopped by `json.Unmarshal` (see Before it is called ready, After the origin of errors along the path).

Maps, item 1 of this list until F23 (2026-11-21), are built (see Maps): a range over a map walks one unknown order of its keys, a `Visited` annotation names the keys a loop has visited, `Forall k in m` and `Exists k in m` range over the keys of a map, string keys included, a loop may write keys and delete keys of the map it ranges over, `Modifies m[k]` names one entry, the number of keys is bounded by memory and by the key type, keys may be booleans, and the shipped entries of `maps` say which keys the maps have. Pointer keys were cut and are listed under Maps, Not built yet. The trial rerun gave 68 of 152 from 62, with speculation on, and the trial's commit d6dcd8f adds the annotations the new forms allow. What now stops each of the 15 functions maps stopped:
  - verify as written with a full contract: digraph's `nodeset.sort` (the always-true guard undone, `|s| <= 1000000` dropped, `i = |done|`) and `nodeset.addAll` (s may be x; it says which keys s gets);
  - verify with a partial contract: `graph.addEdges` (with `addNode` keeping every other key by a quantifier), `graph.nodelist` (Ensures True), `TaskStore.GetAllTasks` (`|allTasks| = |ts.tasks|`; which tasks needs equality of whole structs), and `GetTasksByTag` and `GetTasksByDueDate` (under `|ts.tasks| <= 1000000`, which no caller establishes, since an append that may move needs twice the length);
  - `graph.transpose`: `addEdges may modify edges while the loop ranges over it`, since nothing says that a set the function made is not one of `g`'s (what memory held on entry refers only to what existed on entry; see Maps, Not built yet), with the frame of `rev[succ]` behind it;
  - `reachableFrom` and `sccs`: a function literal used as a value with no contract (item 10 and Function values);
  - `allpaths` and `digraph`: `stdout` and `stdin` (item 3);
  - `summarizeJob`: `os.ReadDir` (Trusted contracts for unproved code, Not built yet);
  - the three handlers: the invariant of the task store behind `ts.store` (item 1) and `json.Marshal` (item 2), and `dueHandler` also `http.Error`.

Calls of the standard library's generic functions under contracts, item 1 of this list until F21 (2026-11-20), are built (see Trusted contracts for unproved code, Generic functions under entries): a call of a generic function is checked against a contract of its own instantiation, built from the entry written under the generic name, and 21 entries ship for `slices`, `maps`, and `cmp`, each tested against the real function at every class of type argument its constraint admits. The trial rerun gave 62 of 152 from 60: hex.AppendEncode (`slices.Grow`, then a write inside the capacity) and hex.AppendDecode moved from blocked to verified. Of the other ten of the twelve, none is stopped by a generic call any longer. The memory server's createEntities, createRelations, and addObservations are stopped by their own `append` to `graph.Entities`, `graph.Relations`, and an entity's `Observations`, which come from `loadGraph` without `fresh`, by the bounds of those appends, and by the requires of `saveGraph`, and addObservations also by the formatting bound of its `fmt.Errorf` of `obs.EntityName`; deleteEntities, deleteObservations, and deleteRelations by `DeleteFunc` writing those same slices without a `Modifies` entry (they are not the function's own memory, since `loadGraph` does not say they are new) and by the requires of `saveGraph`. `loadGraph` itself is stopped by `json.Unmarshal` (item 2), and `saveGraph` by `json.Marshal`. memory.main is stopped by `flag.Parse`, laascmd.main by `os.Args` (item 3), evalresults.summarize by `(*json.Encoder).Encode` (item 2), and sortedKeys is a generic function of the project (item 10). Left out and listed: equality of whole structs in formulas, clauses limited to some type arguments, `slices.SortFunc` and `BinarySearchFunc`, and the rest of item 10.

Writing a `bytes.Buffer` and a `strings.Builder`, from item 1 as the orchestrator split it after part 1, is built (F26, 2026-11-23; see Buffers and builders): `|b|` is the length `Len` returns, the writing and reading methods ship bounded by it, a struct that holds one by value owns what it owns, `fmt.Fprint*` write into a buffer or a `*bufio.Writer` through its own `Write`, and two struct values of one type are known to be one or apart. The trial rerun gave 68 of 152, as before; what now stops `toDot` and hex's `Dump` is under Before it is called ready, After buffers and builders.

Ownership in source, the second part of item 1 as the orchestrator split it, is built (F27, 2026-11-23; see Ownership in source): `@Owns` on a struct of the project, for a slice, a map, a pointer to a struct, and an interface value; growth checked at every store in place of `fresh(...) Or within(...)`; invariants through owned pointer fields across packages, with `valid(x)`; the invariant HI kept by refusing what would break it, with summaries of what each function keeps; the separation C3 assumed of what a listed owner owns and E proved at every verified call; and owners converted to interfaces whose methods write behind their receiver, with rows that follow later stores. The trial rerun gave 69 of 152 from 68: `deleteAllTasksHandler` verifies (see Before it is called ready, After ownership in source).

JSON, item 2 of this list, is built (F29, 2026-11-23; see JSON): `json.Marshal` and `json.MarshalIndent` of values of the project's own types, with the bound of what they build proved from a bound on each length the value holds, `json.NewEncoder(w).Encode(v)` and `json.NewDecoder(io.LimitReader(r, n)).Decode(&x)` written as one expression, and `json.Unmarshal(data, &x)` into a value whose slices, maps, and pointers are nil; every entry holds of Go 1.26 and 1.27, each with and without `GOEXPERIMENT=jsonv2`. The trial rerun gave 70 of 152, as before: none of the eleven functions it waited for is stopped by JSON now, and each is stopped by something else (see Before it is called ready, After JSON).

Invariants kept by frame (F30, 2026-11-23; see Invariants kept by frame), what the four handlers JSON left waited for, are built: a goal that the invariants of a value hold, or a loop invariant, is proved by showing that nothing it reads changed since a point where it held, and a call that writes no footprint grows no old row but through a cell it writes (GR). The trial rerun gave 74 of 152, from 70: `getTaskHandler`, `getAllTasksHandler`, `tagHandler`, and `dueHandler`.

Handlers and ghost states, the next part of item 1 as the orchestrator ordered it, are built (F28, 2026-11-23; see Footprints, The writer of a handler, What a handler reads of its request, Ghost states of values the project converts, and `behind(r)`, The body of a response leaves the response alone, and Converting between interfaces by contract; and Variadic calls and formatting, The text of an error): a handler writes its response through the shipped entries of `ResponseWriter`, `http.Error`, `http.NotFound`, `StatusText`, and `Header.Del`, reads its request (`Query().Get`, `PathValue`, `Header.Get`, fields), formats into the writer with `fmt.Fprintf`, and answers with `http.Error(w, err.Error(), code)`; a `ResponseWriter` is never kept; `Request.Trailer` and `Response.Trailer` are hidden; a value of the project converts to an interface whose contracts speak of a state, with ghost states; and two shipped entries were repaired: `(io.Closer).Close`, which now writes behind its receiver (`behind(c)`), and the separation of a handler's writer from `req.Trailer`. The trial rerun gave 70 of 152 from 69: `deleteTaskHandler` verifies (see Before it is called ready, After handlers and ghost states).

1. **Interfaces whose methods write behind the receiver** (interfaces, third part; part 1 built as F25, 2026-11-22: footprints of interface values, the separation proof, shipped owners, `io.Writer` and `io.Reader` writing behind their receivers, and conversions of values of the project, see Footprints; ownership in source built as F27, 2026-11-23, see Ownership in source, which carries the invariant of a struct reached through an owned pointer field across packages and converts owners of the project; what is left of the item is integer states (`sort`, `heap`) and implementations that rely on their receiver's invariant, ghost states and the `net/http` entries being built as F28; it moved no function of the trial, which waits for what part 1 does not build, as recorded under Before it is called ready, Use it on a real package, After footprints, part 1; what follows is the item as it was counted; 19 functions, first in 8, alone in 1; 4 packages, the only entry in all four kinds: `w.Header().Set(...)` and `http.Error(w, ...)` in every handler, a `*strings.Builder` or `*bytes.Buffer` as an `io.Writer` (writing them, and formatting into them with `fmt.Fprintf`, are built since F26, see Buffers and builders; the conversion stays refused), hex's `Dumper` as an `io.WriteCloser`, `heap.Interface` with `Swap`, whose `Less` also needs an integer state, `Len`, of the receiver). With it comes the invariant of a struct reached through a pointer field and across packages (4 functions, first in 1, alone in 1: 4 of the 9 handlers of eliben's REST server call a method of the task store they hold in `ts.store`, whose invariant names fields package main cannot name; F12 of the review of F16). That is a decided limitation today (Struct invariants, Only at boundaries), and the trial reopens it. It is footprints, the memory a value owns, which a call through an interface forgets and from which callers are kept separate, and converting a value whose method writes behind its receiver, or a value of the project to an interface whose contracts speak of a state (a closer of the project to `io.Closer`, refused since state of interface values), as `*bytes.Buffer` or a writer of the project to `io.Writer`, a `Store` with `Put`, `sort.Interface` and `sort.Sort(byAge(people))`. It also lifts the cost of interfaces recorded in Interfaces: an implementation whose receiver has an invariant, or whose `Requires` reads receiver memory, cannot be converted today. And it is where a struct invariant can say that a struct owns the array of its slice, absorbing `fresh(s.items) Or within(...)`, and cover what a pointer field points to. Of trusted functions that keep a value it unblocks `bw := bufio.NewWriter(w)` followed by a write, the form everybody writes first, and `bufio.NewReader` the same; `(*bufio.Scanner).Bytes`, `ReadLine`, and `ReadSlice`, which return a buffer the next call overwrites; and a wrapper that changes a state of what it keeps, such as `http.MaxBytesReader` (see Trusted contracts for unproved code, Not built yet). Calls through `io.Writer` and the other interfaces of other modules are built (see Interfaces, second part), and so are states of interface values from code that is not verified (see State that a value owns, second part). Waits for nothing.
2. **JSON**: built as F29 (2026-11-23; see JSON). What it leaves is listed there: an encoder or a decoder held in a variable, `DisallowUnknownFields`, methods of the project that encoding/json runs, a wrapper of the project that encodes a value of type `any`, and a map with string keys whose values hold elements of unknown length (see Maps, Not built yet).
3. **Package-level variables that hold code or are assigned**: built as F31 (2026-11-23) and F32 (2026-11-24, part 4 of F31; see Package-level variables). Built: assigned variables of data types, interface types, and named function types with a contract, with `Modifies v`, `v'`, and `old(v)`; the state of a package for what a caller in another package changes; invariants of groups of variables, kept by frame; what a call through a value may reach; struct variables whose address is taken, with the closed address; a mutex in a group with a lock clause; initialisers that call functions, `regexp.MustCompile`, and a logger in a variable. What is left, each listed under Package-level variables, Not built yet:
   - code kept and run later by another package (`flag.Usage = usage`, item 11);
   - changed error variables;
   - a named condition of a package's state that importers can require.

   The trial is 75 of 152, from 74: with the trial's contracts listing `stdout` (b877680), `nodelist.println` verifies by the root of F40 (see "Use it on a real package" under Before it is called ready). `laascmd.main` reads `os.Args` and stops at its bound and at `os.Exit` (item 8). `allpaths` and `digraph` get past `stdout` and `stdin` and stop at other things; `digraph` also stops at converting a `*bytes.Buffer` to `io.Writer`.
4. **Dynamic types and typed errors** (7 functions, first in 4, alone in 1; parsers and a command): `return 0, ErrSyntax` with `type Error int` in strconv, `return i, InvalidByteError(p)` in hex, `io.WriteString`. Behind it in the same parsers, arithmetic that wraps on purpose (4 functions), which stays decided against (below). It is the fourth part of interfaces: type assertions, type switches, `==` on interface values, and typed errors (`errors.As`, a type switch on an error, what an error of a struct type holds, a struct error type converted to `error` as a third known origin). Constraints recorded in Interfaces bind it: an assertion to an interface whose contracts are not declarations in the static type's method set stays `cannot verify` unless the assertion checks them, and the tags of dynamic types follow `types.Identical`. The helpers that decide what can hold code a call may run (`holdsContractedValue`, `holdsCallable`) must then count every interface, errors included. Waits for nothing, but is more useful after the third part. An assertion must keep the reference of the value, as a conversion between interfaces does, so that the result has the states of what it holds (see State that a value owns, second part). Entries for functions that find a method of a value they are given by asserting it to an optional interface and call it wait for this too, and it is the other way to a local `bufio.NewWriter` or `NewReader` that is written through, since the result is new exactly when the argument is not already a large enough Writer or Reader: `(*bufio.Writer).WriteString` (which calls the kept writer's `WriteString`), `io.Copy`, and `io.WriteString`, through `io.StringWriter`, `io.ReaderFrom`, and `io.WriterTo` (see Trusted contracts for unproved code, Not built yet).
5. **Arrays** (7 functions, first in 7, alone in 3; 2 packages: hex's stream encoder, decoder, and dumper, and `sha256.Sum256`), and with them every struct that holds one.
6. **The rest of what was item 6 before the trial** (blocked 0 functions after rewrites, forced 4 rewrites: `copy` twice, a labelled `continue` twice, `fallthrough` once): `copy`, comparing structs with `==`, labelled `break` and `continue`, `goto`, `fallthrough`, `select`, `defer` inside a loop (one inside a function literal that the loop calls is built), a call with several results as the arguments of another call (`g(f())`), an `append` inside a larger expression, and a call in the target of an assignment beside an `append` that may write in place, unless the call modifies nothing and its signature refers to no memory.
7. **Pointers to anything but a struct** (1 function: `flag.String` returns `*string`), such as `*int`.
8. **Functions that never return** (3 functions, all `main` or `usage`: `os.Exit`, `log.Fatal`; see Variadic calls and formatting, Not built yet).
9. **Linked structures** (21 functions, first in 20, alone in 19, counting list's helpers whose neighbour `Requires` no verified caller establishes; but 2 packages, container/list and ring, the most finely cut code of the trial, 158 lines). After the nine by decision: the local operations already verify (`insert`, `remove`, `move`, `Link` with a `Requires` about neighbours, `Init` with a full contract), and what is missing is a whole-structure invariant, a large feature for code that services rarely write. See Structs and pointers, Not built yet.
10. **Generic functions and types** beyond calls under contracts (built with F21, see Trusted contracts for unproved code, Generic functions under entries): verifying a generic function or type of the project (the trial's `sortedKeys[V any]`), methods of generic types (`atomic.Pointer[T]`, `(*glib.Box[T]).Get`, refused as `calls of methods of generic types are not supported yet`), `sync.OnceValue` and other generic functions that return code, generic functions as values and handed over, iterators and range over a function (`maps.Keys`, `maps.Values`, `slices.Sorted`, `slices.Collect`, `slices.All`), type arguments that can hold code (each entry would need `runs`, `keeps`, and `vouches` for those instantiations only), and generic variadic functions over several slices (`slices.Concat`).
11. **Goroutines and channels** (2 functions in the trial, both `main`: `mux.HandleFunc`, `http.ListenAndServe`). A proof is about one goroutine; nothing is said about what runs beside it. It is also what trusted functions that keep code and run it on another goroutine or at another time wait for: `time.AfterFunc`, `http.HandleFunc`, a registry whose `Fire` is handed nothing, a constructor that starts a goroutine over what it keeps, and `context.WithCancel` (see Trusted contracts for unproved code, Not built yet).
12. **The rest that waits on interfaces** (fifth part of interfaces; 0 functions in the trial): `recover`, which returns `any` (see Function literals for what giving it a meaning would assume) and needs dynamic types; `defer cancel()` after `context.WithCancel`, which keeps its parent and runs it on other goroutines, so it waits for goroutines too (see Trusted contracts for unproved code, Not built yet); `sort.Slice`, which needs `any`, dynamic types, and a contract under `calls` that can demand that `less` is an order (see Callbacks handed to trusted functions); and implementations that call through interface values (decorators, a `Writer` wrapping a `Writer`), which need a termination argument across values, such as a depth at which values are made, and that needs the wrapped field to be immutable, which footprints can give. Its first customer is a `*bufio.Writer` converted to an `io.Writer`, as in `bw := bufio.NewWriter(w); fmt.Fprintf(bw, ...)`, and `bufio.NewWriter(bufio.NewWriter(w))`, refused since step 5 of footprints with a message that says to format into a string and write it with the Writer's own `Write`; and the immutability that argument was waiting for is now known of the shipped wrappers: the `owns` claim says that what a `bufio.Writer`, a `bufio.Reader`, or a `bufio.Scanner` keeps does not change except by memory their methods make, with `Reset` not shipped, and F32's reflection check tests it against the real types.

Shipped entries for the standard library that the trial met first, item 1 of this list until F19 (2026-11-20), are built: 126 new entries and four strengthened, each tested against the real function, of `strings`, `bytes`, `unicode/utf8`, `unicode`, `strconv`, `sort`, `math`, `time`, `os`, `path`, `path/filepath`, `regexp`, and `net/http` (Trusted contracts for unproved code, Shipped entries of F19). The trial rerun: 60 of 152 from 58 (evalresults.writeFile and memory's fileStore.Write), with the trial's project entries replaced by the shipped ones but for `strconv.Unquote`, which says more (see Before it is called ready, After the shipped entries). What was left out is listed there with what each waits for: `os.ReadFile`, `os.ReadDir`, `os.Stat`, `flag.Parse`, `sort.SearchStrings`, `bytes.Split` and `Fields`, the methods of `bytes.Buffer` and `strings.Builder` that grow (shipped since F26, see Buffers and builders), `(*regexp.Regexp).FindStringIndex`, `regexp.MustCompile`, `http.Error` and `(net/http.ResponseWriter).Header`.

The small things the trial met in several places, item 1 of this list until F18 (2026-11-20), are built: `fresh` in a body and the message that offers it (Structs and pointers), character literals, `cap`, a slice compared with nil, `apart` of two slices, and `&` in formulas, and a `Snapshot` annotation (The annotation language), identity of interface values under `=` (Interfaces), `Vouches` in the contract of a type in source (Errors), and local type declarations and `string(x)` of an integer (Structs and pointers, Strings). What was left out of them is listed where it belongs: `fresh(s.items)` without the prime (The annotation language, Missing), `apart` of pointers and of interface values, snapshots of sequences, pointers, and interface values, `&` of a variable, and bit operators in formulas.

Function values of named types with a contract are built (see Function values), and so are functions handed to a trusted function that calls them while it runs (see Callbacks handed to trusted functions), trusted functions that keep what they are given in a result or in memory a parameter reaches (see Trusted contracts for unproved code), and interface values whose methods have contracts and write nothing behind their receiver (see Interfaces), and contracts in the trust file for methods of interfaces and for function types of other modules, five of which ship (see Interfaces, second part), and states of interface values from code that is not verified, with `(io.Closer).Close` (see State that a value owns, second part). Three parts of function values were left out and are listed under Function values, each as a feature of its own: a literal used as a value whose contract has to speak of what it captures, a value that calls a value, and a contract for an unnamed function type at the place it is used.

Two more things come up as often:

- **Conversions that drop bits and arithmetic that wraps**, as in `byte(x)` and a hash, are errors by design. `byte(x & 0xFF)` verifies. A way to say "this wraps on purpose" at one operation is decided against (by the orchestrator after the trial on real code): it met 4 functions, all in parsers and all behind typed errors (`maxVal := uint64(1)<<uint(bitSize) - 1`, `ch -= '0'; if ch > 9` in strconv, `byte(h.n >> 24)` in hex), and the Target leaves exact parsing out.
- **Contracts for the standard library.** Since F19 the shipped contracts cover what ordinary code calls of `strings`, `bytes`, `unicode/utf8`, `unicode`, `strconv`, `sort`, `math`, `time`, `os`, `path`, `path/filepath`, `regexp`, and `net/http` (see Trusted contracts for unproved code, Shipped entries of F19, with what was left out and why). They also include, since variadic calls, the formatting and logging functions of `fmt` and `log`, the methods of `*log.Logger` that print, and `path.Join` and `filepath.Join`, and since trusted functions that keep a value, `log.New`, the constructors of `bufio` with some methods of what they return, and the wrappers of `io` (see Trusted contracts for unproved code); of the methods of concrete types only those of `sync.Mutex` and `sync.RWMutex` that lock and unlock (see State that a value owns), of `*log.Logger`, and of `bufio.Scanner`, `bufio.Reader`, and `bufio.Writer`, and five of the methods of interfaces and function types: `(io.Writer).Write`, `(io.Reader).Read`, `(io.Closer).Close`, `(context.Context).Err`, and `context.CancelFunc`; since F21 also the generic functions of `slices`, `maps`, and `cmp` that ordinary code calls (see Trusted contracts for unproved code, Generic functions under entries); and since F26 the methods of `strings.Builder` and `bytes.Buffer` that write and report the length, and the `prints` claim of `fmt.Fprint*` (see Buffers and builders).

## The annotation language

Settled on 2026-11-16, after trying whether formulas could be Go expressions parsed by `go/parser`. They could, for the most part, but implication and quantifiers have no Go spelling and came out worse, and `go/types` rejects formulas that mix integer types, which mathematical integers allow. The language stays its own, which also leaves room for forms that Go syntax would make awkward.

- **One spelling for each thing.** An annotation is `// @Keyword ...`, with one space after the slashes and none after the `@`, which `gofmt` leaves alone (it turns `//@Keyword` into this form inside doc comments); `// @ ...` continues the annotation above it; any other spacing is an error. Length is `|A|`; there is no `len`. `Ensures` stands in the doc comment and nowhere else. `Variant` serves loops and, in a doc comment, recursion; `Measure` is gone.
- **No annotation is skipped quietly.** A comment that looks like an annotation and is not read as one is an error: a keyword that is misspelt, glued to its formula, behind another spacing or behind other text, an `Ensures` outside a doc comment, and an ordinary comment line directly after an annotation, which may have been meant as its continuation. A comment after an annotation needs an empty `//` line before it. Since the keyword follows the `@` directly, a wrong keyword looks like the tag of another tool, as in `// @Post` and `// @Summary`. So every unknown word there is an error, and a project that has such tags declares them with `-vego.other-tags`; guessing by how close a word is to a keyword let `// @Post r > 100` pass without the clause. The rule holds wherever an `@` starts a line but for signs, block comments included. Behind other words, as in `// note: @Post ...`, only a keyword or a word within two letters of one is reported, since an address or a handle looks the same; that is the limit. A package in which collecting the annotations drew any report hands none of its contracts or invariants to other packages, since an annotation that was not read, or was dropped or misplaced, may have been meant for any of its functions; the rule is tied to the collecting step as a whole, so a report added there later is covered without being remembered. One thing is let through on purpose: an annotation behind slashes of its own, as in `// // @Ensures ...`, which is one commented out or shown in an example. The scanner compares letters, so a keyword spelt with letters of another alphabet that look the same is not recognised.
- **An `Assert` stands between statements, or at the end of a block or a case clause** (F17, from the trial's `assertcase`). One after the last statement of a case is checked in that case, with what the case declared in scope, as one at the end of an `if` block is.
- **A contract that cannot be read is reported where it is used** (F17, from the trial's `badcallee`; `unusable.go`). Before, a caller of a function whose `Ensures` could not be read was told nothing until the callee was fixed. Now each use reports what cannot be read, so the caller fails there, and the caller is verified on with what can be read, so its own faults show at once. For a function of this package whose `Requires` and `Modifies` can be read, a call assumes the clauses that can be read; if a `Requires` cannot, the call forgets what `Modifies` lists and assumes nothing more, since an outcome whose precondition was not checked tells nothing; and for any other contract that cannot be used (a `Modifies` that cannot be read, annotations that cannot be parsed, a trust entry with a defect) the path ends at the use, as a panic would. The contract of a type or of an interface method declared in source that was refused only for clauses that cannot be read is called with the `Requires` that can be read and no `Ensures`, and a value is not checked against it. A backstop panics when a clause of the contract is misread after that, at the place that asked. Every use goes through `viewOf`: calls, calls through values and interfaces, hand-overs, formatting, invariants, and the call sites of loops.
- **A verified function names its results.** A contract refers to results by name, and Go does not let a result share a name with a parameter, so a name in a contract cannot mean both. `True`, `False`, and `nil` stay reserved for the formula language. `result` and `result1`, `result2` remain only for trusted functions that leave their results unnamed. Open question for the user, from the trial on real code: 101 of the trial's 152 signatures had to be changed to name their results, the largest single piece of friction it measured (16 `:=` statements became `=` with it).
- **Unchanged:** `And`, `Or`, `~`, `->`, `<->`; `=` for equality; the prime for the state on return and `old` inside bodies; intervals such as `[0, n)`.
- **Go's operators are refused** (F19, from the review of F18). The lexer read `&&`, `||`, `!`, and `==` as second spellings of `And`, `Or`, `~`, and `=`, and the parser `ℤ` as a second spelling of `Z`, against the rule of one spelling. `lex` still recognises each operator, only to refuse it with the spelling to use (`goSpellings`), and the parser refuses `ℤ` where it reads a quantifier's domain, while elsewhere `ℤ` stays an identifier that a formula may name (F9 of the review of step 1); `!=` stays, the one spelling of inequality, and literals are lexed before operators, so a character or a string holding these is unaffected. No formula of the corpus, the guide, the trial, or the shipped files used any of them. `gospellings` holds each in source, in a body, and in a trust file, and `TestGoSpellings` each place a formula can hold them.
- **An `Ensures` speaks of the return through results, primes, or `fresh`** (built with state of interface values, at the orchestrator's request). A top-level conjunct of an `Ensures` that has none of these is a fact about the state at the call, which the `Requires` must give anyway; when it reads memory the function may modify, its author most likely meant the value on return, as in `@Ensures p.x = 7` on a function that sets `p.x`, which verified whenever the `Requires` said so. Such a conjunct is refused, with a message that shows the primed path (`entryonly.go`). Whether it reads such memory is decided by proof: its value on entry and its value after every target of the frame is forgotten must be proved equal, so a read through a predicate, recursive or not, a quantifier, `has`, a length, or a state counts, and so does one that may alias a target; an inconclusive proof refuses. It applies to verified functions, contracts of function types and interface methods, and trust entries. A clause relating a result to the entry state, `r = p.x`, is kept. In the corpus and the guide it found one clause, `defects/defects.go` `EntryVersusExit`, written to show exactly this mistake.
- **Two more keywords**, `State` on an interface type and `Receiver` on a method of one (see State that a value owns, second part).
- **Characters, capacity, nil slices, `apart`, and `&`** (F18, from the trial and the first readers of the guide). A character literal is the number of its character, read by `strconv.UnquoteChar` as Go reads it. A quote directly after an identifier is a prime, and any other quote starts a character, so a malformed one, as in `x ' = 1`, is an error and not a prime. `cap(A)` is the capacity, and `A = nil` for a slice is `isNilRef`, the term code's `A == nil` builds; both are last steps of a path (`Step.Cap`, `Step.Nil`), so they work for slices of structs too, primed and under `old`. A comparison with nil whose other side is no slice is resolved as before (`logic.NotSlice`), and one of a string is an error. gofmt turns two quotes in a row in a doc comment into `”` and two backquotes into `“`, which in a string of a formula changed its meaning without a word (F11 of the review of step 1), so `lex` refuses both marks anywhere in a formula and says how to write each; `"\u201d"` is the mark. `apart(a, b)` is `separate`, the assumption made of slice parameters of one element type, and is refused for slices of two element types, which never share memory. `&p.f` and `&s[i]` are the address code's `&` computes, from the last pointer or slice of the path and the field offsets after it; the path must reach memory and end in a struct, and a pointer to a struct of size zero compares only with nil. When the last pointer of the path is nil the address has the allocation 0, which is nil, where Go panics; through an earlier nil pointer nothing is known of it. `cap` and `apart` joined the reserved words (`logic.Reserved`), which predicates, states, applied parameters, and suggested result names cannot take. The always-fails check offers, besides its slice of equal length and capacity, one with spare capacity when a formula names the slice's `cap`, and a nil one when a formula compares it with nil (`noteNamed`); offering them for every slice whose capacity a path condition mentions made five honest contracts of the corpus be reported, since `append` mentions it everywhere. Bit operators in formulas, and `&` of a variable or of anything but a struct, are not built.
- **`Snapshot` names a value at a point** (F18, decided by the orchestrator, from the trial's three helper variables kept alive with `_ = x`: `r0 := r` before Clean's inner loop, `n0 := len(chunk)` in matchChunk, `o0 := offset` in digraph). `// @Snapshot name = term` stands where an `Assert` may; the term, an integer or a boolean, is resolved in the body's environment there and stored in the state (`state.snapshots`) as the term itself, not as a constant tied to it by a fact, so whatever renames the facts of a state renames it too. From there to the end of the innermost block or case clause (`snapshotScope`), a name in a body annotation resolves to it before anything else (`snapshotAt` in `locateAt`). Refused, at collection (`checkSnapshots`): a name that is a word of the language or names anything visible there, universe included; a name declared again in its scope, by Go or by another snapshot; a quantifier in its scope that binds the name; a snapshot no annotation in its scope names; one named from inside a function literal it was not taken in; and one that follows an `Invariant` or `Variant` in its comment group, since a loop's annotations are gathered before it and a snapshot below them would be read above where it is taken (F2 of the design review). Primed, in `old`, with fields, or of another sort, it is an error where it is read. Why not `Ghost`, the usual word: the scanner takes every word within two letters of a keyword for a misspelt keyword and protects it from `-vego.other-tags`, and `Ghost` is within two letters of `host` and `Host` (swag's `// @host` on every swagger-documented `main`) and `Post`, which could then no longer be declared as another tool's tags. Why not `old` at the start of an iteration: the three uses take a value before an inner loop, inside the outer iteration, which "the start of this iteration" does not name, and `old(x)` already means the value on entry inside a body. Not built: snapshots of sequences (the contents of a slice or a string at a point), of pointers, and of interface values.
- **`elemsize(A)`** (F21): the size in bytes of an element of the slice `A` on the target, `Step.ElemSize`, which reads no memory and is 0 for values of size zero; a reserved word, so no predicate, state, snapshot, or applied parameter can take the name. It lets one entry of a generic function bound an allocation for every element type. Refused for anything but a slice.
- **`Visited` and quantifiers over the keys of a map** (F23, see Maps). `// @Visited done` above a range over a map names the keys the loop has visited, read as `|done|`, `has(done, k)`, and in `Forall k in done`; `Forall k in m` and `Exists k in m` range over the keys of a map, with `k` of the sort of the keys. `has`, `|...|`, and quantifier domains take `old(m)`. `Z` stays the integers in a domain, and a name `Z` in scope there, or a bound variable named `Z`, is an error.
- **`valid(x)`** (F27, step 2): the invariants of the struct `x` is or points to, with those of what it owns, resolved in the type's package; see Ownership in source. A reserved word.
- **Missing: equality of whole structs.** A struct cannot be compared or used as a whole in a formula, so the generic entries say nothing of elements that are structs. Building it needs a decision on what `=` means at float, blank, and slice fields, where Go's `==` and identity differ.
- **Missing, from the first readers of the guide:** `fresh(s.items)` being written without the prime although it speaks of the state on return.

## Before it is called ready

- **Use it on a real package.** Done as F16 on 2026-11-20. The trial module is `/home/spachava/dev/vego-trial`, outside this repository (whether other people's code joins the corpus is the user's decision): commit 1 is the unchanged copy and every later commit is annotations, rewrites, or entries; `report/REPORT.md` is the report, `report/classes.tsv` the class and blockers of every function, `findings/` the programs of the faults, and `measure.sh` reruns the trial and prints the functions proved per package, with any that changed against the classification, at the verifier it is given (`./measure.sh path/to/laas`; about 85 s, one process in a memory-limited scope). Run it after a feature to measure what it unblocked.

  The code: 152 functions, 2,647 lines of function bodies. Parsers and formatters: Go's `path`, `internal/strconv`'s atoi, `encoding/hex`. Data structures: `container/ring`, `list`, `heap`. Commands: x/tools `cmd/digraph` v0.42.0, this repository's `cmd/laas` and `evals/golang-docs` results and files. Services: the go-sdk memory server (an MCP knowledge graph, JSON through a store interface) and the stdlib REST server of eliben/code-for-blog with its task store (`net/http` handlers, `json.NewDecoder(req.Body).Decode`, `http.Error`, a mutex-guarded map). Every function got a contract that says what it is for, the smallest rewrite a user would accept where vego stopped, and 13 project entries that state only documented behaviour.

  | kind | functions | verified as written | after a rewrite | with a partial contract | blocked |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | parsers | 38 | 9 | 4 | 10 | 15 |
  | data structures | 39 | 8 | 0 | 8 | 23 |
  | commands | 34 | 4 | 1 | 3 | 26 |
  | services | 41 | 7 | 1 | 0 | 33 |
  | all | 152 | 28 | 6 | 21 | 97 |

  The first two columns have contracts that state what the function is for; "partial" means a contract that states only part of it, or rests on a `Requires` no verified caller can establish. 608 of the 2,647 lines verify, 206 with a full contract. No handler, no `main`, and nothing that does I/O verified. The rewrites: a nil test of a slice written as a length test (3, for the `nillen` fault), `copy` written as `append` or a conversion (2), a labelled `continue` written with a flag or `break` (2), `fallthrough` written out, range indexes named for invariants (2), an always-true guard around a write in a map range, three helper variables. Rewrites tried and not kept: path.Clean's buffer allocated up front (it verifies, but Clean of a clean path then allocates, and upstream TestCleanMallocs fails under GOMAXPROCS=1, which skips it otherwise), digraph.split's `bytes.Buffer` replaced by a `[]byte` (blocked by `fresh`). Every rewrite kept the upstream tests passing, also under GOMAXPROCS=1. The ranking of what blocked the rest is the order of the list at the top, with its numbers there. Where the measures disagree: linked structures block the most functions alone (19) but in two packages of one kind, the most finely cut code; footprints and package-level variables hold the most lines (412 and 467) and are alone in one function each; standard-library contracts are the first message in 17 functions and alone in one, so they are what a user meets first, and what comes next is one of the others. The order follows the reviewer's ranking where it differed from the first recommendation (decided by the orchestrator): `fresh` in invariants and the other small things second, since `fresh` alone blocks Clean and ring.New; footprints sixth, since in the greedy order they complete only 3 functions until shipped entries, maps, JSON, and the small things are done; and the invariant of a struct behind a pointer field with them.

  Friction, counted: 101 of 152 signatures had to name their results (see The annotation language), which turned 16 `:=` into `=`; 39 length bounds in `Requires`, most for formatting (the suggested platform bound changes with every format string) and appends in loops; 54 `Modifies` lines; 3 helper variables with `_ = x`; 3 loops repeating an invariant of a struct, 2 a field of a local struct, 3 what holds when they run zero times; 21 contracts that could state only part of what the function is for, each with its reason in the trial's classification (8 exact text processing, which the Target leaves out; 6 list neighbours no caller establishes; 3 opaque by decision, formatting, pattern matching, floats; 2 `&` in formulas; 1 Decode's typed errors; 2 not attempted); three struct invariants dropped because they could not be proved of nodes reached through pointers or established by callers in another package. What helped: the messages that name the length to bound and suggest a `Requires`, the place of index and nil messages, `-vego.cost` pointing at a loop head, and the message that a callee's verification failed in its package. What misled was fixed with F17 (see After the trial, below). Where the guide was silent it was completed with F16, and what the faults made it wrong about with F17.

  **After the trial (F17, 2026-11-20).** What the trial found wrong was fixed, each fault with a program that verifies and one that is refused in `vego/testdata/src/trialfaults`:

  - `badcallee`: a call of a function whose contract cannot be read in full is reported at the call, and the caller is verified on with what can be read, so its own faults are reported too (see A contract that cannot be read is reported where it is used, under The annotation language).
  - `nillen`: a nil slice has length and capacity 0 (`layout.go`: a reference 0 has capacity 0), so `if a == nil { return len(a) }` gives 0 and the three rewrites of path's lazybuf are not needed.
  - `withincover`: `within(p, l)` for two struct pointers says that `p` lies at an exact offset where `l`'s struct, or a field of it held directly or in a struct it holds, has the underlying type of `p`'s struct, tags ignored (`offsetsOf`), so `@Modifies l` covers a write through `p`. When `p`'s struct has a size other than zero and there is no such place, `within` is an error; for a struct of size zero the range of bytes is kept.
  - `anyresult`: a produced interface value chooses its home (`chooseHomes`, see Function values, A contract on a type must be one that can be met), and a nil interface value has no home, which also stopped two nil results from making the state contradictory (`freshnil.go`).
  - `assertcase`: an `Assert` after the last statement of a case clause is checked in that clause.

  The messages that misled now say: for `copy`, that it is not supported yet and how to write it; for an array, which field holds it, through nested structs; for `flag.Usage = usage`, that a variable of another package cannot be assigned; for a callee that failed in its package, one line; for a trust file that cannot be read, the problem of the file at each call, and no "has no contract"; for results to name, names taken from the function that clash with nothing in it; for a result, that a `Modifies` clause cannot list it and what to do, and for a parameter, the path to list, as in `s.To.X`; and for recursion, the cycle (see The call graph, under Function values). `searchNodes` asks 85 problems instead of 1,897 (see Errors, The always-fails check waits for the function's own faults).

  The trial rerun at e71f725 with `./measure.sh`: 55 of 152 functions verified with some contract, as before, and no function changed class. The 18 functions of the memory server that the recursion message stopped draw no cycle message since the shipped entries of `os.IsNotExist` and its kind say what they find on the error; each is stopped by another blocker already in the classification: `os.ReadFile` and `WriteFile` without entries, an error of unknown origin formatted, `json.Marshal`, generics, a `Requires` of a callee no caller establishes (among them that of `saveGraph`, itself blocked), the bound of an `append`, and a conversion to `mcp.Content`. The memory package took 51 s instead of 83 s, at 1.2 GB of memory at most instead of 1.3 GB.

  Soundness repairs made with F17, each a way to accept something false that the feature found:

  - Variants were not checked across a call with no static callee, since function values and interfaces were built (2026-11-19): `cycles/hole` verified and loops (see The call graph, under Function values).
  - A nil interface value had a home, since state of interface values: two nil results of a trusted `fresh(result)` made the caller's state contradictory, so `BothNil` with `@Ensures r = 1` verified while it returns 2 (`trialfaults/freshnil.go`; see State that a value owns, A nil interface value has no home).
  - `chooseHomes`, as first built in this feature (a985106 and 53775b4), read a cell through the outcome's own write with the table of homes, so `FillsBoth`, which cannot be met when its two boxes are one, was accepted as one that can (F12 of the review of step 1; `trialfaults/anyresult.go`).
  **After the small things (F18, 2026-11-20).** The trial rerun with `./measure.sh` at the end of F18: 58 of 152 functions verified with some contract, from 55. path.Clean, ring.New, and digraph.split were blocked and verify, each by `fresh` in a loop invariant: Clean with its buffer lazy, as upstream wrote it, whose TestCleanMallocs passes under GOMAXPROCS=1, which the eager rewrite did not. No function stopped verifying. The classes that changed in `report/classes.tsv` (trial commits 9e0f249 and 88edc7b): Clean, ring.New, and split from blocked to verified with a partial contract (Clean and split are exact text processing, which the Target leaves out, and New does not state the shape of the ring); lazybuf.index and lazybuf.string from verified after a rewrite to verified as written; list's Element.Next and Prev from a partial contract to a full one. The classes are now 32 verified as written, 4 after a rewrite, 22 with a partial contract, and 94 blocked. The trial's commit 9e0f249 undoes the rewrites the new forms make unnecessary and adds what they allow: lazybuf tests `b.buf == nil` again, the three helper variables are snapshots, list's `Element.Next` and `Prev` state their full contracts with `&e.list.root` (partial before), ring's `init`, `Next`, and `Prev` say `r.Value' = r.Value`, and the memory server's `store.Read` vouches for its errors. What now stops the three functions whose first blocker this removed is already in the classification: digraph.somepath a value that calls a value, the REST server's createTaskHandler `(net/http.Header).Get` without an entry, and the memory server's loadGraph `json.Unmarshal`. lazybuf.append keeps its rewrite of `copy` (item 6) and digraph.split its rewrite of `bytes.Buffer` (its growth needs an integer state; see Trusted contracts for unproved code, Shipped entries of F19, and State that a value owns).
  **After the shipped entries (F19, 2026-11-20).** The trial rerun with `./measure.sh` at d4bb43c and later: 60 of 152 functions verified with some contract, from 58. evalresults.writeFile (`os.MkdirAll(filepath.Dir(path))`, `os.WriteFile`) and memory's fileStore.Write (`os.WriteFile`) moved from blocked to verified with a partial contract (WORLD in `report/classes.tsv`: the file system is not modelled, so their contracts say only that they return). No function stopped verifying. The trial's 13 project entries were replaced by the shipped ones (trial commit cf4781d), which say as much or more, and the count stayed 60; `strconv.Unquote` stays a project entry, since the shipped one gives no length and digraph.split needs the `3 * |s|` read from source, and it gained the shipped entry's requires and vouches: without the requires it claimed that Unquote returns for a string so long that its buffer cannot be made, a finding about the trial's entry. What now stops the other functions a missing entry stopped first: `os.ReadFile` (fileStore.Read, readJSON) and `os.ReadDir` (summarizeJob), not shipped; `flag.Parse` (memory.main); `(*strings.Builder).Grow` (hex.Dump); `io.WriteString` (digraph.usage, past `strings.Cut`); a `*bytes.Buffer` as an `io.Writer` (toDot); `http.Error` and `mime.ParseMediaType` and json in the REST handlers; `HandleFunc` (tasks.main); `packages.Load` (trustReport); json's `Encode` (summarize); `filepath.WalkDir` (copyTree); `(embed.FS).ReadDir` (writeTooling).

No function stopped verifying. What now stops the other ten of the twelve that a generic call stopped is listed at the top of this file, under the paragraph of F21.

No diagnostic of the trial speaks of an error's origin. memory.loadGraph, the function the orchestrator named to watch, was not stopped by the rule over every assignment at 9c39fbb either: since F18 `store.Read` vouches for its errors and every assignment to `err` there is of known origin, and it is stopped by `json.Unmarshal` (JSONDEC in `report/classes.tsv`). What stops the rest of the 15 is in the paragraph of F23 at the top of this file.
  **After footprints, part 1 (F25, 2026-11-22).** The trial rerun with `./measure.sh` at ca4e1c5: 68 of 152 functions verified with some contract, as after maps, and no function changed class; the 19 functions that item 1 counted are all still blocked, each by something part 1 does not build. Diagnostics compared one by one against a run at c2fe4b6, the commit before F25: `toDot` and hex's `Dump` now draw the message for a `bytes.Buffer` and a `strings.Builder`, which says what they wait for, in place of the advice to add a trusted contract, which would have been false; two reports that the run at c2fe4b6 made twice, at `createEntities` and `createRelations`, are made once; 24 of the 27 notes that Z3 reached its resource limit are gone, on the same reports; and two appends, in `searchNodes` and `openNodes` (kb.go:410 and 453), now stop with Z3 out of memory where they stopped at the limit, the same verdict, on two runs alike. What stops the 19 now: hex's `Dump`, `toDot`, and the `digraph` command a `strings.Builder` or a `bytes.Buffer` written (buffers as writers, the orchestrator's candidate for the next feature; the command also its package-level `stdin` and `stdout`, item 3); `NewEncoder` and `NewDecoder` arrays; `Dumper` the state `closed` of `io.WriteCloser` (ghost states, moved out of part 1) and arrays; heap's six methods `sort.Interface`, whose `Len` needs an integer state (part 3), and `Push` also an `any`; and the seven handlers JSON (item 2), `mime.ParseMediaType`, `http.Error` (the `net/http` entries, moved out of part 1), and the invariant of the store behind `ts.store` (ownership in source).

Before d32bbee it also drew a false `list w` inside its loop (Buffers and builders, the fix after the trial). hex's `Dump` stops at `converting a value of type *strings.Builder to io.Writer is not supported`, since a Dumper keeps the Builder as its writer, and the Dumper holds arrays (item 5). The `digraph` command is still stopped by `stdin` and `stdout` (item 3).

  - `getTaskHandler`, `getAllTasksHandler`, `tagHandler`, and `dueHandler`: the bound of `json.Marshal` of a `Task` needs a bound on its text, its tags, and each tag, and of a `[]Task` on their number too, which only the store can give. The task store bounds every task it holds (`|tasks| <= 10000`, the text, the tags, each tag), `Task` has an invariant of the same bounds, `CreateTask` requires what keeps it, `DeleteTask` lists only the entry it deletes (`@Modifies ts.Mutex, ts.tasks[id]`), the getters ensure `valid` of each task they return, and `GetTasksByTag` reads the tags with `slices.Contains` (a rewrite: the nested loop is not proved, see Invariants kept by frame, Not built). `getTaskHandler`, `getAllTasksHandler`, `tagHandler`, and `dueHandler` verify with a partial contract: the store's invariant on return is kept by frame across the writes through `w`, and `deleteTaskHandler` still verifies. No function stopped verifying. What stops the rest of the REST server: `createTaskHandler` `mime.ParseMediaType` (step 5 of F30 ships its entry) and `CreateTask`'s `ts.nextId < MaxInt` and bounds on the decoded task, which the handler would have to check; `main` `HandleFunc`.
  **After package-level variables that are assigned or hold code (F31, 2026-11-23).** The trial rerun with `./measure.sh` at 2875090, after the trial's commit b877680, which lists `Modifies stdout` and requires `stdout != nil` in `println`, `allpaths`, `somepath`, and `digraph`, as F28 listed `Modifies w`, and bounds each name `println` prints (`Forall i in [0, |l|) : |l[i]| <= 100`): 75 of 152, from 74, in 49 s. `nodelist.println` verifies, by the root of F40: without it, it stops at the second `Fprint`, having lost the bounds of the names after the first write through `stdout`. `allpaths` stops at the memory `reachableFrom` returns, which it changes, and at the bounds of the edges it joins and prints; `somepath` at its recursive literal of an unnamed function type; `digraph` at the bounds its callees require of what it reads and at `*bytes.Buffer` as an `io.Writer`; `main` at `flag.Usage = usage`, now with D3's message.
- **Speed.** Measured with speed on 2026-11-19 (F11), on a machine with 24 cores and 31 GB, other agents idle, load average about 1. What was built is recorded under The prover (the cache of answers, what each answer costs, `-vego.cost`, the guard of the corpus costs) and under Errors (the always-fails check asks only what is read, the question whether no state forces success only for a reported contract, the order of the questions). Three programs of the measurement are in `vego/testdata/src` (`slowhttp`, `slowresp`, and `manywrites`, the function of thirty pointers); the others are described in the table. Times at 35dca07 against the end of speed, same messages everywhere:

  | program | 35dca07 | after | problems asked | resource units (M) |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | `go test ./vego/...` | 150 to 158 s | 116 s; 29 s with `VEGO_CACHE` warm |
  | the main corpus run of TestAnalyzer | 65 s | 40 s | 22,309 to 20,263 | 369 to 226 |
  | `slowhttp.Fetch`: `resp, err := get(url)` with a trusted `get` returning `*http.Response` | 99 s | 80 s | 54 to 51 | 32.2 to 26.5 |
  | one function writing one field through 30 pointers | 10 s | 10 s | 1,021 | 128 |
  | 120 straight-line statements on a slice of 16 | 32 s | 29 s | 1,564 | 176 |
  | the guide's programs (`guide` alone) | 28 s | 13 s | 2,330 to 2,120 | 58.5 to 21.8 |
  | `semantics` alone (chains of 30) | 17 s | 8 s | 1,866 to 1,472 | 27.8 to 14.0 |
  | 60 short functions with 2 or 3 `*P` parameters that may be the same | 2.1 s | 0.3 s | 840 | 1.2 |
  | a chain of 25 calls with contracts | 0.8 s | 0.7 s | 344 | 0.4 |
  | nested loops (pair count, smoothing in place, a triple loop) | 0.5 s | 0.4 s | 111 | 0.7 |
  | four functions on a struct of 43 fields of another package of the module | 0.1 s | 0.1 s | 27 | 0.02 |

  The generated packages of short functions with aliasing pointers, which took about ten minutes with the prover written in Go, are no longer slow. Z3 is about 94% of the CPU of a run: the main corpus run used 15.5 s of Go CPU, a third of it collecting garbage, against 233 s in Z3 at 35dca07. At 35dca07 the always-fails check took 46% of Z3's time on the corpus, proof obligations 37%, contradiction checks 10%, checks whether a Modifies entry is needed 5%; 200 problems that ran to the limit took 56% of it. What is known and not fixed:

  - **Measured and built with speed, third part** (F22, 2026-11-20, on the same machine, one measurement at a time, each in a 12G/4G scope, with the reviewer's and other idle agent threads present, load average 1 to 6). Ten features after F12, `go test ./vego/...` had grown to 150 to 158 s. The main corpus run of TestAnalyzer kept all four Z3 processes busy (217 Z3-seconds; 266 problems at the limit took 96 of them); the other 90 s of the vego package used less than one process, since every test ran one after another; TestShippedContracts was 106 s of Go CPU, 68 s of it garbage collection from a quadratic evaluator. A run at `-vego.rlimit=1000000` cut the main run from 56 to 43 s but changed 12 expectations (10 in `manywrites`, one each in `algorithms` and `values`), so no lower limit for programs meant to fail was adopted (decided by the orchestrator). Repeated runs ask the same texts: two corpus runs agree function by function, and a run of `memory` of the trial after one `Requires` changed asked 502 of its 597 solved problems word for word as before (a line inserted at the top changed none). Built: the tests side by side, problems sent without comments, and the answer cache on disk (see The prover). Times before and after, cold unless said:

  | run | before (4df9a91) | after |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `go test ./vego/...` | 150 to 158 s | 116 s; 29 s with `VEGO_CACHE` warm |
  | the vego package alone | 148 s, 3.8 GB peak | 79 s, 5.0 GB peak; 135 s and 3.2 GB with `-parallel 1` |
  | the main corpus run of TestAnalyzer | 56 s | 58 s; 2 s warm |
  | the solver package alone | 58 to 73 s | 109 s, of which 24 s are the sweep without e-matching that guards F11 and 10 s TestStoredAnswersAreZ3s; 29 s warm |
  | TestProveIsSound | 58 to 67 s | 62 s; 4.3 s warm |
  | the trial (`measure.sh`) | 93 s | 53 s with speculation (93 s without), the same 62 of 152 proved |
  | `memory` of the trial, alone | 90 s | 33 s with speculation (91 s without); 18.9 s with `-vego.cache` after one `Requires` changed (527 of 624 answers from the cache); 2.7 s unchanged |

  Left: the trial's `memory` spends 82 of its 90 Z3-seconds on 123 problems at the limit, and the always-fails questions and failing obligations of functions that do not verify are most of what any cold run costs.
  - **Deep type facts in the questions after a call** (found with speed; solved with F12, which found that the facts were not the cost: see Problems in which a region holds sequences are solved without extensionality, under The prover). In `slowhttp`, every slow question is one whose answer is open when Z3 finds no model: the contradiction check of the trust entry of `get`, `canReach`'s contradiction check after the call, the proof that the error is nil after the call, and the proposals and accepting proofs of the input search, for `get`'s contract and for the path after the call. They carry the type facts of what the call returns, which `deepFacts` and `contentFacts` (`calls.go`) state for the results and the arguments of a call (`involved` in `call`) and for the inputs of the always-fails check (`errors.go`, `contractInstance` and `forcesSuccess`): every byte of `Status` and `Proto` in 0..255, every value of `Header` and `Trailer` for every key a slice whose bytes are bytes, every string of `TransferEncoding`. These are universally quantified facts over nested arrays (`SeqSeqSeqInt`). Each question runs to the limit at 0.3 M units a second, against 1.6 M on average in the corpus: in one, 36,721 array extensionality axioms and 3,431 final checks. The state itself states type facts only at reads, so only these questions carry them. Seven of the problems, at 35dca07, were in `testdata/slow` until F12 removed it (they are at befc067), each with a first line that says what asked it and what it cost (two of them were asked twice; the cache now answers the second). What removing facts did, with the pinned Z3: without any quantified fact every one is sat within 9,000 units in 0.02 s; keeping only the facts about bytes, the trust entry's check (`trust-entry-contradiction.smt2`) is still open at the 4 M limit, and sat at 8.4 M; without only the facts about map values, it and the two others tried (`reach-contradiction.smt2`, `accept-sample.smt2`) are still open at the limit, in 0.6 s instead of 11 to 19 s; with every fact, at ten times the limit, the trust entry's check gives up after 23 M units with "incomplete quantifiers". So no limit finds a model, and the facts about map values are what make each unit slow. `slowresp` reproduces the cost without `net/http`, an interface, or a pointer field: a trusted function returning a struct with the fields of `http.Response` that hold strings, slices of strings, maps of them, integers, and booleans took 84 s for `Code`, with 31 s more for the entry, as the reviewer measured it; one `map[string][]string` alone in a struct took 3.1 s.
  - **A proof obligation that fails costs its whole limit**: 4 M units, 1 to 4 s in the corpus and up to 18 s in `slowhttp`. That is what `-vego.rlimit` bounds.
  - **Long functions cost the square of their length.** Every obligation carries every fact so far: the 1,300th problem of the 120-statement function is 102 KB with 1,403 assertions, and the function sends 75 MB in all. `ctx-simplify` took 550 k of the 607 k units of such a problem, and encoding is 65% of Go's CPU there (4.8 s of 7.4 s). Since F12 the chain has no `ctx-simplify`. The program of the table and of these figures is not in the repository. Another of the same description, `testdata/src/straightline` (887 problems, 27 MB of text, not part of the tests), went from 42.4 M to 19.2 M units and from 50 questions that stopped at a limit to 2, but its time stayed at about 9 s, since encoding and reading the text are what grow with the square. Encoding a shared prefix of hypotheses once was left out (decided by the orchestrator): whether a term becomes a `define-fun` depends on its uses in the whole problem, so the text of a prefix depends on what follows it. Sending fewer hypotheses would lose proofs, and where a failed proof is the quiet outcome it would make vego quieter: a Modifies entry would count as needed, `dependsOn` would report a dependence, `canReach` would report a path as possible, and the contradiction checks after calls and of entries would report nothing. That breaks the loudest-path rule, so it was not done.
  - **One field written through twenty-four or more pointers is not proved at the default limit**; see The prover. With ten to twenty-three it is since F12, which took `ctx-simplify` out of the chain.
  - **Each problem costs Z3 about 1.2 ms to build its context after `(reset)`** (the first command after it builds the AST manager; `(reset)` alone takes 0.02 ms), about 12% of Z3's time on the corpus. Starting the context early with `(reset)(push 1)(pop 1)`, while Go builds the next problem, was tried and dropped: it adds 8 units to Z3's count on 148 of 150 problems drawn from the corpus, and on one (a problem of `callbacks.countdown`) the search itself changes, 4,700 units and 25 decisions against 7,536 and 36. Since the count is what the limit bounds, it could change an answer. To try it on another Z3, compare `(get-info :all-statistics)` of every distinct corpus problem with and without it, leaving out time and memory.
  - **The functions of one package are verified ahead on idle processes** (F22, step 4, decided by the orchestrator after a prototype; see Functions are verified ahead, under The prover). Before it, in a run of one package three of the four Z3 processes were idle; the trial's `memory` ran alone on one process for its last 68 s.
  - **Closing an interface parameter with states and then reading through a wrapper of it** (found with F15, measured by the reviewer). `rc.Close(); w := io.NopCloser(rc); _, err = w.Read(p); return err`, with `rc` an `io.ReadCloser` under `@Requires ~closed(rc)` and `@Modifies rc`, costs 53 problems, 17 M units, and 8 s: 6 questions of the always-fails check, 2 contradiction checks, and 2 quick checks stop at the limit. Without the `Close` it takes 0.13 s, and without the wrapper 0.15 s. The likely cause is the one found for the samples of the always-fails check (see Errors, A sample says nothing of the homes of values made after entry): Z3 runs to the limit on problems whose table of homes is not one value everywhere and whose facts hold a quantifier over a written row, and an input with a state has a home of its own while the `Close` writes the row of states. Not fixed; a fix would start from a captured problem of that function.
  - **Found by the trial on real code** (F16). path.matchChunk asks 7,441 problems for 74 lines (11.6 s), and memory.searchNodes 1,897 for 45 (47 s), most of them the always-fails search of a function that will not verify; since F17 it asks 85 (see Errors). path.Clean is not slow: the first report said so, from a weak invariant of the trial's; with `out.w < r` in its inner loop it verifies in 3 s; since F18 it does so with its buffer lazy, as upstream wrote it, and `fresh(out.buf)` in its loop invariants.
  - **The tests of the vego package run side by side** (F22). Every test calls `parallel(t)`, except those that change a setting of the whole package or the process (`growthRatio`, `refuseCache`, `skipCopyWalk`, `costStderr`, `scratch`, standard output, the standard logger, the working directory), which call `alone(t)`; Go runs those first, and `alone` fails while a parallel test runs, which a subtest of a parallel test would be (F6, F12 of the review). The shipped entries whose harnesses change the process run in TestProcessWideEntries. The observers of costs and of the verifier are keyed by analyzer, so the cost table counts only the main corpus run. At most two loads of the corpus or of the standard library from source are held at once (`corpusRuns`; F7): peak RSS 4.8 to 5.1 GB against 3.8 GB before and 7.8 GB without a bound. The test evaluator walked a chain of pushes by copying the sequence at each push, which made TestShippedContracts 106 s of CPU, 68 s of it collecting garbage; it walks the chain once now.
- **What the first readers of the guide could not do.** Two reviewers tried to verify small realistic programs with only `docs/verification.md`. A new module went well; a service with a mutex, a map of structs, wrapped errors, an interface, and a global did not. What stopped them, beyond the list above (character literals, `cap`, comparing a slice with `nil`, and `apart` for two slices that share no memory are built since F18): `fresh(s.items)` is written without the prime although it speaks of the state on return; a sum or count over a slice that is being written cannot be carried through a loop; an invariant of a struct type is not known of the elements of a slice.
- **Continuous integration.** The tests, including those of the shipped contracts, run by hand and on one toolchain. 32-bit targets have only been tried in a few spots.

## By area: what is built, why, and what is missing

### The prover

Built: obligations are discharged by Z3, run as a separate program and fed SMT-LIB text (`vego/internal/solver`). It replaced a prover written in Go on 2026-11-17. The old one was deliberately incomplete in ways that kept showing up as work for the user: no model of a failing goal, a fixed depth of instantiation and unfolding, and a step budget that ran out on true goals.

Decisions:

- **One pinned version.** `solver.Version` names the release a version of `laas` uses (5.4.1), and any other is refused with a message saying how to install it. Another version may prove different things, and a proof that comes and goes with the installed Z3 is not a result. Moving to a new release is a change of its own, run against the whole corpus. Z3 is looked up only for a package that has something to prove.
- **A binary, not bindings.** No cgo. The protocol is SMT-LIB on standard input, one problem at a time, each preceded by `(reset)` and followed by an `echo` marker; any reply other than `sat`, `unsat`, or `unknown` with its reason is a fault of the run, never a proof. The output is read while the problem is written, since Z3 prints an error for each bad command. Up to four processes are kept per run, each limited to 1 GB; a process that ran out of resources is replaced.
- **Deterministic effort.** Each problem has an `rlimit`, which Z3 counts the same way on every run of one binary. Z3's default tactic tries some solvers for a fixed time before others, so problems are solved with an explicit chain of simplifications and the core solver, which has no timed step (see The chain has no `ctx-simplify`). A wall-clock limit of five minutes is only a backstop and is reported as such. Seeds are fixed on the command line, since options set in a problem survive `(reset)`.
- **An answer is kept by the text of its problem** (decided with speed, by the orchestrator). A `Solver`, one per binary and cache directory, keeps in its process what Z3 answered to each problem: the status, the reason, the values asked for, and the resources counted, under a key of the whole SMT-LIB text sent and the names whose values were asked. Since F22 the text sent has no comments (see Problems are sent without comments); on disk only with `-vego.cache` (see Answers are kept on disk when asked). A problem asked again is answered from there. This rests on the decision above: after `(reset)`, with the options fixed on the command line and the limit in the text, Z3 of one binary answers a text the same way every time, which is already what lets any of the four processes serve any problem. What depends on the machine is never kept: a problem past the wall limit, one out of memory, a process that died or replied with anything else, and an unknown for want of memory. An unknown because Z3 was canceled is kept: vego gives Z3 no time limit, so Z3 cancels only at the resource limit, with the same count every run (two quick questions of the guide come back `canceled` at 200,917 and 202,770 units against a limit of 200,000), which is why `exhausted` reads it as the limit. The text kept is bounded at 256 MB per process, the oldest forgotten first. Soundness does not rest on the premise: an answer is reused only for the text that produced it. The premise is what makes the messages the same with and without the cache. At 35dca07, 5,976 of the 22,309 problems of the corpus repeat a text asked before in the same run, 4,842 of them in the same package.
- **Every answer says what it cost.** Each problem ends in `(get-info :rlimit)`, and a reply without the count is a fault like any other unexpected reply. `Options.Observe` is told the result, the length of the text, the count, whether the answer came from the cache, and the time; a cached answer reports the count Z3 gave when it solved the text, so the count does not depend on which package asked first.
- **The cost of the corpus is recorded per function, and must not move unseen** (decided with speed, by the orchestrator, with the reviewer's F2). `testdata/costs.txt` records, for each function of the main corpus run of TestAnalyzer, by package and name, the problems asked of Z3, the resources Z3 counted, and the problems it left open (at a limit or unknown). A cached answer counts as Z3 counted it, Z3's count ignores the comments of a text, and the questions come in a fixed order, so the numbers are exact. With the Go version, system, architecture, and Z3 that recorded the table, any difference fails, growth and drop alike, so a change that makes verification slower is seen where it happens and one that makes it faster records the gain; with another Go version only growth of more than 10% fails, since the types of the standard library may change what is asked. A function missing from the table fails, and so does one the table lists that asked nothing, so a program is added to the table deliberately. Bytes of text are not part of the table. A count of resources does not track time, which ranged from 0.3 M to 45 M units a second; the count of open problems is what catches a quick question that comes to run to the limit. `VEGO_RECORD_COSTS=1 go test ./vego -run 'TestAnalyzer/verifies'` rewrites the table.
- **`-vego.cost` tells what each function asked** (decided with speed, by the orchestrator). It prints, on standard error, per function: the problems, the resources Z3 counted, the bytes of text without comments, the seconds, and each place and kind of question that stopped at a limit (`cost.go`). It changes no result. A question counts against the function of the innermost `pv.run`, at the statement being executed or the position of the obligation, as one of five kinds: proof obligation, question of the always-fails check, contradiction check, check whether a Modifies entry is needed, quick check. A question asked while no function is being verified panics as an internal error, so a question added later cannot go uncounted. The seconds include waiting for a free Z3 process, and leave out the time of other functions a function leads to; they are the one number that moves with the machine.
- **Problems are sent without comments** (F22, decided by the orchestrator). The encoder ends each declaration with a comment naming the term of the logic, and records where it is (`remark`); the text sent to Z3, kept as the key of an answer, and returned by `Script` leaves them out, and only the problem a `Fault` saves keeps them (`encode` returns both). The comments carry numbers the process hands out in the order packages ask (the `#N` of `typeKey`, the counters of fresh names), so with them 1,681 problems of the main corpus run were solved again although Z3 had read the same text (6.4 of 217 Z3-seconds). Z3 ignores comments: the cost table did not move. A capture of what a process reads (the wrapper in the memory of how to capture problems) now has no names; the saved problem of a fault does. The order and the names of the text depend only on the terms: two corpus runs ask, function by function, the same texts, and a run with the `typeKey` numbers started at 7 (as the test binary now does, `typeKeyBase`) asks all 30,815 texts of a run started at 0, which a cache filled by the one and read by the other showed.
- **Answers are kept on disk when asked** (F22, decided by the orchestrator, with F1 to F5 of the review). `-vego.cache=DIR`, off by default, keeps what each process printed for each problem, as raw lines, under the exact text it read (`disk.go`). Earlier the cache was ruled to stay in the process, since a cache on disk adds ways for the premise to be false. Each is excluded or fails the package (and, since F24, every later question of the run: see What a run can depend on besides the source and the binary; `laas` exits with status 1), never answered quietly:
  - Another Z3 or other libraries. The entries are under `DIR/z3-<hash>` of the format, the command line, GOOS/GOARCH, the processor's model and flags, the SHA-256 of `/proc/<pid>/exe`, and of every file the process maps with code (not `ld.so.cache`, which a package install changes). Every process is identified after its first exchange, so that exec has happened, and must match the first; `exe` must be the file the path names, so a script that runs Z3 is refused, and a binary replaced between two processes fails the package.
  - A damaged entry, or one for another text. An entry is a format line, the key compressed, the reply, and a SHA-256 of all that; the stored key must equal the key asked byte for byte, which also covers a key that is a prefix of another. A missing file is a miss (another run trims); a file that is there and wrong stops the package with how to empty the cache.
  - Two writers. An entry is published with `link(2)`; the loser compares replies, and a difference means Z3 answered one text two ways, which fails the package.
  - A drift. One in 256 of the answers read, drawn at random per run, is solved again live and compared, count included.
  - What depends on the process. Z3's error for a get-value without a model names a line number that counts what the process read before; it is kept as a fixed line (`noModelReply`), found by TestStoredAnswersAreZ3s, which solves 400 random problems into a fresh directory on four workers and then solves every entry again.
  - What is sent around a problem. `(reset)`, the two `get-info` lines, and the form of `get-value` are constants of `z3.go` that the identity holds, so a change to them moves the directory (F18).
  The key is the comment-free text, NUL, and the names asked: fixed before solving, unlike the marker or whether a get-value follows. The version of vego is not in it: a reply to the same bytes does not depend on who sent them, and the reader parses it with its own code. The directory must be new, empty, or marked by an earlier run with a `CACHEDIR.TAG` that vego wrote, and the trim lists and removes only the cache's own layout (`z3-<32 hex>/<2 hex>/<62 hex>` and `.tmp-*` beside them), since an early version deleted every old file under the directory given (F14 of the review: `-vego.cache=$HOME` would have emptied a home directory). The directory may not be at or below a `go.mod` or a VCS root (`/tmp` holds a `.git` on the development machine, so the tests stop that search at their own directory, and the guide says to use `$HOME/.cache/vego`), and works only where `/proc` exists. Trimmed once a day: entries unused for 5 days (a hit touches an entry older than an hour), temporary files older than a day, then the oldest until under 1 GB.
