Visible wherever it is used: a package that read any answer from it prints `vego: N of the M answers of Z3 for P came from the answer cache in DIR` on standard error, `-vego.cost` adds the count per function, and the trust report names the directory (`Usage.AnswerCaches`). The orchestrator asked for one line at the end of a run; `multichecker.Main` ends the run with `os.Exit` and vego runs per package, so the line is per package. "What a proof assumes" states that nothing but vego writes the directory, beside the binary. The test binaries use it when `VEGO_CACHE` names a directory and run cold without it (`testCache`; TestProveIsSound too, decided by the orchestrator, since every proved goal is still checked by evaluation). Tests: TestDiskCache, TestStoredAnswersAreZ3s, TestAnswerCache; each rule was checked against a mutant. The test binary of the solver stands in for Z3 when `VEGO_FAKE_Z3` is set (`fake_test.go`), since a script cannot pass the identity: it checks that an answer for want of memory and a fault are not kept, that the no-model reply is kept in its fixed form, and that a Z3 that ends at start fails the package, where at first it released its slot twice and hung (F15, F16).
- **Problems in which a region holds sequences are solved without extensionality** (F12, decided by the orchestrator, with F1 to F3 of its review). The last tactic of their chain is `(using-params smt :array.extensional false)`, written in the problem, so it is as deterministic as the rest (`tacticWithoutExtensionality` and `encode` in `solver.go`).

  Soundness does not depend on which problems are picked. Without the axiom of extensionality, by which two arrays with the same elements are equal, the theory is weaker, so a problem without a model there has none with the axiom, and every `unsat` stands, for whatever problem it is used.

  The rule that picks them is there so that no proof is lost. The logic has no equality of sequences (the encoder faults on one), and a sequence is an argument of only two kinds of uninterpreted symbol: the key function `S.key`, and a recursive predicate with a sequence parameter. Every other symbol applied to a sequence (select, store, the constant array, the lambdas of concatenation and cut, `ite`, the constructor and fields of the datatype, a quantifier, `Options.Forall` over constants of sequence sort) gives equal results for sequences with equal elements. In a model without the axiom, identifying arrays with equal elements then gives a model with it. So a problem without a key or such a predicate has a model with the axiom exactly when it has one without, and a problem with either keeps the axiom (`tellsApart`). That the two have the same models does not make Z3's answers the same (F2 of the review). Its procedure is incomplete with quantifiers, and without the axiom it may stop at a limit, or give up on the quantifiers at once, where with the axiom the new indices of its axioms led to a contradiction. That this loses nothing is measured, not proved.

  Of the problems the rule allows, only those in which a sequence of sequences of sequences is declared leave the axiom out: a region that holds strings, slices of strings, or slices held as map values, inside structs or slices. That choice comes from measurements of this corpus and is a rule to revisit, not a law. Leaving the axiom out of every problem the rule allows was measured on the 15,721 problems of one corpus run. With the old chain it made 21 problems that Z3 answers `sat` within 5 to 45 k units with the axiom run to a limit, since Z3 uses the axiom to finish a model, and 7 more reached a limit. With the chain without `ctx-simplify` it lost one proof (a problem of `manywrites`, `unsat` at 3.55 M units with the axiom), sent 22 `sat` answers to a limit, and left 23 problems at a limit in all. The narrower rule selects 73 of the corpus problems. It changes none of their answers, and the count of one goes up, a `sat` from 5,586 to 7,459 units, which `memory.CountDigits` asks twice (60,522 to 64,268 units in `testdata/costs.txt`, the only change of the table). Someone who finds a slow problem outside the selection, one whose questions after a call carry quantified facts over nested arrays without a region of strings, should measure it against the axiom before anything else.

  Where the axiom made Z3 slow: with quantified facts about what the types guarantee of contents (bytes of strings, values of maps under every key), Z3 kept adding extensionality axioms between the rows of a region, each with a fresh index whose read matched the facts, whose instances made new array terms. On the trust entry's check of `slowhttp` that was 36,721 `array-ext-ax` and 40,721 `array-splits` in 4 M units and 11 s. `smt.mbqi=false` and dropping `ctx-simplify` changed nothing there. Without the axiom it ends `unknown (incomplete quantifiers)` after 36,941 units in 0.01 s. Through vego, at befc067 against now:

  | function | problems | units | seconds |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | `slowhttp.Fetch` | 39 to 39 | 13,485,852 to 268,333 | 81.7 to 0.16 |
  | the trusted contract of `slowhttp.get` | 7 to 7 | 13,027,224 to 1,109,851 | 40.8 to 0.34 |
  | `slowresp.Code` | 27 to 27 | 13,438,828 to 263,414 | 81.4 to 0.15 |
  | the trusted contract of `slowresplib.Get` | 7 to 16 | 14,007,481 to 1,282,371 | 30.7 to 0.46 |

  The check of `Get`'s entry asks more now: its proposals get values instead of stopping at the limit, so the search offers more of them. No message changed anywhere. `slowhttp` and `slowresp` are now in the main corpus run, where the table of costs holds them to these counts.

  Guards. `TestProveIsSound` solves its random problems also without the axiom, the same problems with the same seeds: 1,127 and 1,276 are proved either way, each confirmed by evaluation (F1 of the review). `TestExtensionality` checks the rule. Two problems need the axiom, a recursive predicate and a key inside a quantifier of a sequence that a write left as it was, and each also declares a region of strings, so that only `tellsApart` keeps the axiom. Both failed on a version without that condition, at the choice of the chain and, with that check off, at the proof (F3). Other problems check reads through writes without the axiom. `testdata/src/deepfacts` holds six trust entries whose promises contradict what the types guarantee: a byte of a string, a map value, a string in a slice, a quantified promise over every byte, a byte of an argument's element, and a value of a map argument. Each is still reported. The questions of four of them are solved without the axiom; those of the map value and the map argument read the map by a string key, whose key function keeps the axiom (F8 of the review). Two more entries cover the values of a map with integer keys, which needs no key function, in a result and in an argument, and the checks of both entries are solved without the axiom (F2 and F8; written by the reviewer). The argument's entry also reads a string of the map value, without which its check declares no region of strings and keeps the axiom. Beside them is a contract that the always-fails check reports. That these questions are in the selection was checked by turning the rule off: their counts went up, for `deepfactslib.Must` from 28,921 to 4,009,750 units.
- **The chain has no `ctx-simplify`** (F12, decided by the orchestrator). The chain was `simplify propagate-values ctx-simplify simplify elim-uncnstr smt`. `ctx-simplify` used the whole limit on true goals that the core solver proves at once: in a problem of the function of thirty pointers below it used 400,002,695 units in 8.8 s at a limit of 400 M, against 10,609,678 units in 2.4 s without it, and it took 550 k of the 607 k units of a late obligation of a long function. Candidates were compared by replaying the 15,721 distinct problem texts of one main corpus run of TestAnalyzer with each chain, comparing every answer and count, and then by the corpus and the guide through vego. Without `ctx-simplify` no `unsat` was lost, 22 were gained (all in `manywrites`), the count fell from 322.3 M to 260.4 M units, the problems left at a limit from 175 to 153, and only 2 problems cost more, by about 50 units each. Through vego, the only messages that changed were 22 of the 29 of `manywrites`, in the main corpus run and the guide, and the two of `Cube` in `testdata/src/costs`, which TestCostFlag verifies at `-vego.rlimit=600` so that some questions stop at the limit: its `s * c` is now proved within 600 units, and it became `Product` of four numbers, whose two multiplications stop at the limit. No function of `testdata/costs.txt` costs more: the table went from 354.1 M to 291.5 M units and from 262 to 240 problems left open, with the same problems asked. Also tried, on the problems of `manywrites` that remain: `(then simplify smt)`, `smt` alone, `solve-eqs`, `blast_select_store` (6 M to 28 M units instead of 4.3 M to 10.6 M), and the core solver with relevancy 0, case_split 3 or 5, phase_selection 0, luby restarts, theory_case_split, or array.weak; none proves them within the limit, and tuning options for one program was rejected. `TestManyWrites` (solver) holds ten writes through pointers that may be the same, which the old chain does not prove.
- **One field written through twenty-four or more pointers is not proved at the default limit** (found with speed, F3 and F14 of its review; measured again with F12, F4 of its review). A function that writes `p_k.x = 1` through `n` pointers and ensures `p_k.x' = 1` for each verifies with up to twenty-three pointers, and leaves postconditions not proved with more: 1 of 24, 1 of 25, 2 of 26, 4 of 27, 6 of 28, 6 of 29, 7 of 30, 15 of 35, 23 of 40, each with the note that `-vego.rlimit` raises the limit, and `-vego.rlimit=40000000` proves all thirty. The seven need 4.3 M to 10.6 M units, with the same count under every chain tried, spent on read-over-write case splits in the core solver (78 k `array-ax2`). The cause is how a region is written for Z3. A region is a sequence of rows (`SeqSeqInt`), and a write of one field is `store(T, r, store(select(T, r), a, v))`, whose inner read of the previous region needs a chain of case splits of its own. On a generated problem of thirty writes through pointers that may be equal, the count of a read after later writes is:

  | encoding of a region | after 29 later writes | after 20 | after 2 |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | a sequence of rows (as built) | more than 4 M | 3.6 M | 11 k |
  | an array of arrays without the datatype | more than 4 M | more than 4 M | 7 k |
  | a two-dimensional array `(Array Int Int Int)` | 5.9 k | 10.9 k | 5 k |

  Turning extensionality off does not change this. Through one pointer the encodings cost the same (thirty writes, about 4 k units each), so what a two-dimensional region would buy is code that writes one field of one struct type, or elements of one element type, through many references that may be the same and then reads it: a function that resets a field of many structs it is handed or reaches (`ps[0].x = 0`, `ps[1].x = 0`, ..., or `a.n = 0; b.n = 0; ...`), and one that links many nodes of one type (`n1.next = n2; n2.next = n3; ...`). Different fields are different regions, and a slice of structs is one reference, so filling a table or a struct is not this shape. It is a feature of its own (decided by the orchestrator), a redesign of the encoding of memory with the scrutiny the memory model had. A row is also a sequence value: a slice is a cut of a row (`view`), an allocation stores a filled row (`storeRow`), a havoc relates rows, predicates and quantified formulas take rows and cuts of them, and the samples of the always-fails check store rows. Each of these would need a row built from the two-dimensional array, which is a lambda unless every read is pushed through to the array, and reads through lambdas made Z3 loop before (see No lambda where a read will do). `testdata/src/manywrites` holds the thirty-pointer function, expected to verify and marked with its 7 failures (1,021 problems, 69 M units).
- **The translation keeps the meaning the logic had.** Integers and references are `Int`; floats and errors are uninterpreted sorts, with the nil error a constant and some other error not nil. Two errors are the same error when they are equal, and `errors.Is` is an uninterpreted function of two errors, `error-is`, about which only the facts the verifier adds say anything (see Errors). A sequence is a datatype of an array and a raw length: reads are total, a filled sequence holds its value everywhere, and the length is the raw length or zero where that is negative. That last rule replaced "every length is at least zero", which as an axiom would contradict a sequence built with a negative length, and which as a quantified fact kept Z3 from finding models. Go's truncating `/` and `%` are written with SMT-LIB's Euclidean ones, and division by zero is an uninterpreted function of the dividend. Every node is sort-checked before it is written, since Z3 converts between booleans and integers silently.
- **No lambda where a read will do.** A read of a cut, a fill, or a concatenation is written as the read it equals, and a predicate that does not reach itself is written out in place with its bound variables renamed. Reads through the arrays that lambdas build made Z3 match facts about a cut against every read of the whole, and loop.
- **Facts about moved indices get a copy.** Z3 matches `A[k + lo]` in a fact only against reads whose index is a sum with `lo`. For an asserted `Forall` without a quantifier inside, a read of a fixed sequence at `c*k + t` gets a copy of the fact over the index itself, triggered by every read of that sequence; in the copy that read is written `A[i]`, so an instance makes no new read. Copies for denied facts, or for nested quantifiers, cost more than they gave on the corpus.
- **Keys are related pairwise.** The key of a sequence is an uninterpreted function, and every two sequences keyed outside quantifiers get the fact that their keys are equal exactly when their contents are. A quantified axiom with a multi-pattern does the same for keys inside quantifiers, but kept Z3 from finding models; it was tried and dropped.
- **Recursive predicates are axioms.** A predicate that reaches itself is a declared function with its definition as an axiom triggered by its applications. Recursion only in positive position has a fixed point, so the axioms have a model.
- **A model is never evidence.** The translation describes more states than Go has: a map whose count disagrees with its keys, junk past the length of a sequence, a division by zero with a chosen value. So `sat` decides nothing. Where the verifier needs an input to exist, as in the always-fails check, Z3 proposes values for a sample of a simple shape and the verifier proves the facts about the sample with those values before it accepts it. `unsat` is used as evidence where it is sound to: that no state of the logic forces success means no input does, and the message then says so.
- **Functions are verified ahead on idle processes** (F22, step 4, decided by the orchestrator, with F8 to F10 of the review). The verification of a package is what it was: one function after another, the only one that reports, exports facts, and counts costs. For a package with two functions to verify or more, up to `maxSpeculative` (3) speculative verifiers in the whole process each build a `pkgVerifier` of their own on a copy of the pass and verify the package's functions from the end of the order that the verification has not begun (`speculate.go`), only to put answers into the solver's cache. A copy of the pass drops reports and exports, keeps its own facts, and reads the facts of the dependencies from a snapshot taken once the verification has collected the annotations, without those of the package itself, so that it never reads what the verification writes. A panic of a speculative verifier is recovered and dropped (since F24, except an error that says the answer cache or the solver cannot be trusted, which fails the package; see A process ended for another asker serves no one). In the solver (`Options.Speculative`, `acquire`, `answer`): a problem in flight is not asked twice, a speculative asker takes a process only after it holds one and only while no other asker waits, at most all but one process serve speculation, none starts within 100 ms (`quiet`) of another asker finding every process busy, and another asker that finds every process busy ends the youngest speculative process (its asker gets `errPreempted`, an open answer, and a waiter on its problem asks it again). Why nothing but time depends on it: the verification asks the same questions in the same order with and without speculation, every answer it gets is Z3's to the same text (the premise of the cache), and a cached answer counts as Z3 counted it. The second rests on the invariant of the pool, not only on the premise: as first built it did not hold, and runs of the trial with speculation on printed different diagnostics (F24; see A process ended for another asker serves no one). Measured, each run alone in the scope: the trial 93 s to 53 s (peak RSS 1,035 to 1,165 MB), `memory` alone 91 s to 33 s (780 to 783 MB), the main corpus run through laas 54.6 s to 54.65 s over four pairs, outputs byte for byte the same on every run. Without the quiet window the corpus ran 1% slower (preempted problems waste their work and a process start); a gate on the number of packages being verified took most of the trial's gain away, since the package a run waits for is not known ahead; speculative verifiers for every analysed package, the standard library included, held the places with nothing to verify, hence the count of functions. On by default; `-vego.speculate=false` turns it off. Tests: TestSpeculation (vego: diagnostics and costs equal on and off, functions verified ahead, a speculative verifier that panics on every function changes nothing) and TestSpeculation (solver: preemption, the waiter of a preempted problem, the bound of speculation; each failed on a mutant), `-race` on the corpus programs of the tests and on four trial packages through laas. The `#N` of `typeKey` now also follows which verifier asks first; that it reaches no text is guarded (see Problems are sent without comments). Found in review: an asker that panicked between taking a problem and ending its flight, as on a cache that cannot be written, left the waiters of that problem blocked for ever, so a flight now ends on every path and its waiters ask again (F22); a `CacheError` that a speculative asker raised was dropped with the verifier, while the main verification could then read the entry just found wrong, so the first one fails every later question of another asker (F23; since F24 one met by any asker does); and a speculative verifier now stops at its next question once its package is verified (since F24 a question in progress stops at once too), and `alone` waits for any still at work.
- **A process ended for another asker serves no one** (F24, 2026-11-21, decided by the orchestrator, with F1 to F8 of the review). Found by the reviewer of F23 at `bb4e7be`: four runs of the trial each proved 61 of 152 where a run should prove 62, each failing `path` at another place, and `Clean` asked the same 601 problems and bytes in every run with different counts of units. With `-vego.speculate=false` every run was the same. The cause was a check-then-act race in the pool. `release` read whether a process had been preempted before it took the pool's lock. A question of the verification that found every process busy could, in that window, take a speculative process that had already answered, mark it preempted, and kill it; `release` then put the dead process among the idle ones, the same asker took it, and its failure came back as `errPreempted`, which `Model` read as an open answer for any asker. The obligation was reported as not proved, without a note, and its units were missing from the cost. Z3 was not at fault: on four captured runs (23,342 problems, 5,811 distinct texts) no text got two replies, and the reviewer replayed every problem of one run alone in a fresh process with the same replies. The evidence: one differing run lost exactly the 17,534 units of one problem of `Clean`, asked once, on a process that had answered its previous problem and replied to nothing more; in 16 instrumented runs, each of the 3 that differed had as many questions of the verification given `errPreempted` as dead processes put among the idle ones, and the 13 that matched had none. The trial showed it in `path` because `path` is verified last, while its three speculative verifiers hold the other processes. F22's tests missed it: the fake Z3 of the solver's `TestSpeculation` slept a second before every answer, so every preemption landed while Z3 was solving; the vego comparisons ran on an idle machine, where the window is microseconds; `-race` sees no check-then-act on synchronized state. The cache was never poisoned (an answer with an error is not kept), but a waiter on the same flight got the same false open answer, and a `watch` that drew the dead process returned without a word. A run without speculation was never exposed, since only speculative processes are preempted.

  Built (`z3.go`, `solver.go`, `speculate.go`):
  - **The invariant of the pool**, in the comment of `pool`: a process is idle, held by one asker, held for speculation (in `speculating` once it can answer, and only then preemptible), preempted (out of `speculating`, flagged, and killed in one critical section; never idle again and never handed out), or ended; only a holder of the lock moves it. `release` reads the flag under the lock, so a process is among the idle ones before anyone can preempt it, or preempted before it would be. `check` verifies the states and counts at the top of every pass of `acquire` and after every change, and records a violation as an `InternalError` (it never panics under the lock), which every later question and every waiter in `acquire` gets.
  - **Only a speculative asker can be told it was preempted.** `preemptedOr` gives any other asker an `InternalError`, and so does `Model`, as a second line. The analyzer turns it into a failed package with "internal error of vego: …; run again with -vego.speculate=false, and report it". A `watch` that fails for another reason, or a crash or junk reply on solving again, is a `CacheError` that the entry cannot be confirmed, for both kinds of asker (F6, F7).
  - **A speculative question stops at once when its package is verified** (F5): `Options.Stop` is closed by `speculation.end`, and the question ends with no answer wherever it is, in the quiet window, waiting for a process (`WakeAll`), waiting on another asker's flight, or being solved, whose process is then preempted. The broadcast that was missing when a spawned speculative process became preemptible is added (F2: an asker that found every process busy during the spawn waited for some later change).
  - **What a speculative verifier finds that says the cache or the solver cannot be trusted fails its package** (F4): `answer`'s recover and the speculative verifiers' recover record a `CacheError` or `InternalError` in the solver (`Distrust`); the verification of a package, once its loop is done, stops its speculative verifiers and waits for those that began (at most 10 minutes, `speculationBound`, else an `InternalError`), then fails the package with `Untrusted()`. Before, a drift found by a speculative `watch` after the verification's last question was dropped, and the run passed on an answer Z3 had just contradicted. The wait measured on the trial: at most 1 ms per package. This is tested in two halves that meet in `Solver.Distrust`, which is the one place both kinds of error are recorded: `TestSpeculativeDrift` (solver) has a speculative asker's `watch` find a real drift, which `answer`'s recover records; `TestSpeculativeDistrust` (vego) has a speculative verifier panic with a `CacheError` after the verification's last function, which the verifier's recover records, and the package must fail. They are apart because one test of a real drift after the last question would need a `watch` held inside the vego test binary, whose solver has no hook for it; in vego, a drift from `watch` passes both recovers, so either records it.
  - **Tests**, each seen to fail on a mutant of what it guards: `TestPreemption` (after an answer, before solving, while solving, once registered, of the verification and its waiter, of a watched answer), `TestInconsistentPool`, `TestStoppedSpeculation` (each of the four places), `TestSpeculativeDrift`, `TestWatchFailure` in the solver; `TestSpeculativeDistrust` and `TestQuestionsCarryTheStop` in vego. The order of events is forced with one test hook, `paused`, called at every point where the pool or the flights dropped a lock or are about to take one, and where an asker begins to wait; a fake Z3 answers at once, and a problem that must be in progress waits for a file or for its kill. `TestConcurrentQuestions` asks 2,400 questions with known answers of both kinds of asker at once on three processes, with random sleeps at every such point: on `bb4e7be` with only the internal error added (as a `Fault`) and the same pause points, it failed every run, 10 of 10 by the coder and 3 of 3 by the reviewer, with 21 to 40 faults each; it passes, also under `-race`.
  - **The check of the outcome** is `vego/repeat.sh`, a script rather than a test since what goes wrong is between `laas` processes (in one test binary two runs would share a solver and the bound of speculation): it runs a module N times, two at a time, in a 12G/4G scope it starts itself, and compares each sorted output, exit status included, with the first, leaving out only the seconds of `-vego.cost` and, with `-filling`, the counts of answers read from a cache that the runs fill together. On the trial with `-vego.cost`, 20 runs: `bb4e7be` 6 of 19 differed from the first; after the fix none did, and the output equalled a correct run of `bb4e7be` byte for byte. On the same binary after the fix, all equal to that output: five pairs of runs that each start on one empty `-vego.cache` (with `-filling`; the raw counts of answers from the cache differed within every pair, 96 to 148 lines that name the cache), ten runs on a warm cache, equal to each other with nothing left out but the seconds and equal to that output once the counts of answers read from the cache are left out, and ten with `-vego.speculate=false`. The cost table (`testdata/costs.txt`), recorded with speculation on, equals a main corpus run with it off.
  - **What the fix costs**, each run alone in a 12G/4G scope, `bb4e7be` against the fix: `go test ./vego/...` cold 124 s and 122 s, filling `VEGO_CACHE` 129 s and 129 s, warm 33 s and 35 s; the trial 55.4 s and 55.5 s; `memory` of the trial alone 33.5 s and 34.1 s with speculation, 94.1 s and 94.1 s without. The diagnostics of each pair are the same. So the fix costs no time beyond noise.
- **What a run can depend on besides the source and the binary** (F24, decided by the orchestrator). The same binary on the same source prints the same diagnostics, exit status, and `-vego.cost` figures on every run, with speculation on or off, with the cache cold, warm, or shared, on an idle or a busy machine, except for these, which are the only outcomes of a run allowed to depend on the machine or on timing:
  - **The seconds** of `-vego.cost`.
  - **The wall limit** of five minutes per problem, which depends on the speed and load of the machine. A problem that reaches it is reported with its note and never kept in a cache.
  - **Z3's memory limit** (`-memory:1024`), which Z3 checks against its live allocations (`g_memory_alloc_size` in `memory_manager.cpp`, counted down on free). After each `(reset)` of the longest captured stream of the trial (469 problems in one process), the live memory read with `(get-info :all-statistics)` stayed between 0.48 and 0.67 MB (measured by the reviewer of F24). So what a process did before moves a problem's distance to the limit by under 1 MB of 1024; a problem within that margin could still go either way. It is reported with its note and never kept in a cache. Moving to another release of Z3 should measure this again.
  - **The drift sample of the answer cache**, one answer read in 256 solved again, drawn anew for each run: a wrong entry is found in some runs and not in others. When it is found, the package fails.
  - **Where a damaged or drifted cache directory is reported.** One rule (F24, decided by the orchestrator with F11 of the review): a cache error met by any asker, the verification or a speculative verifier, fails its package and every later question of the run, whether it is a drift, an entry that cannot be confirmed, or a damaged entry. It replaces F22's "other packages of the run go on": the remedy is the same for each (empty the directory), a run whose later packages go on reading a directory it has shown wrong is the quiet path, and a rule with one case cannot depend on which asker met the error first or on whether speculation is on. Packages verified before the error keep their messages. An error a speculative verifier meets fails its package once the verification has stopped the speculative verifiers (see A process ended for another asker serves no one). Timing can still decide in which package the run first reports the directory, or whether this run reaches a wrong entry at all, since a speculative verifier may reach an entry before the verification would and the drift sample is drawn anew. That is a fault of the directory, not of the program verified; no answer is taken from a damaged entry, and every case errs toward failing. For a damaged entry only when the error is raised changes: a speculative verifier asks the texts the verification asks, and an entry found damaged is not kept in the process, so the verification reads the same entry itself and fails. For a drift it can change whether: the verification draws its own sample and would have used the entry, which the check at the end of the package prevents. TestCacheErrorFailsTheRun damages one entry of `cachefaila` and holds `cachefailb`, none of whose entries is damaged, until the error is recorded; both packages fail with the same message with speculation on and off, and with only a speculative asker's errors recorded, as before, `cachefailb` passes.
  - **The counts of answers read from the answer cache**, whenever two runs start from different contents of the directory: a cold and a warm run differ in them every time, and in runs that fill one directory at the same time, which run solved a text first and wrote it decides whether another read it from the directory. The diagnostics and the other figures stay the same. `vego/repeat.sh -filling` leaves those counts out.

- **A contradiction check is asked first without e-matching** (F22, decided by the orchestrator, F11 of the review). The checks of the shipped entries of `slices.Delete`, `DeleteFunc`, and `Compact`, and the quick checks after each call of them, ran to their limits at every use (1.3 s per instantiation, 22 s of the shipped-entry tests): their facts are satisfiable, but the type facts quantify over a row written through a store, and e-matching kept making instances to the limit (`unknown ("push canceled")` at 4,000,754 units), where Z3 without it builds a model in 5,956 units. Rewriting the facts (dropping the offset forms or their copies, a pattern on the frame fact) did not help, so the fix is in how the question is asked: every question that counts as a contradiction check (not one of the always-fails search, which reads an open answer as a limit it reports) is first asked with `(using-params smt :ematching false)` at a tenth of its limit, at most 200,000 units (`Options.ModelFirst`, `modelFirstEffort`); its `sat` or `unsat` ends the check, and an open answer leads to the check as before. This is not a model taken as evidence. The rule above is that a model does not show that a Go state exists; a contradiction check asks only whether the facts of the logic contradict each other, and a `sat` says they do not, which is what an open answer is already read as. An `unsat` without e-matching is an `unsat`: in Z3 5.4.1 the option is read only by the e-matching plugin and by pattern inference, so it removes instances and cannot make a contradiction appear; a `sat` with quantifiers rests on the same model check by MBQI either way (the reviewer read the source at the pinned commit). On 6,458 distinct problems of six corpus packages, solved both ways at the same limit, none was `unsat` one way and `sat` the other; 43 went from open to `sat` and 2 from open to `unsat`, but 53 went from `sat` to open, which is why the attempt is a first try and not a replacement of the chain. Through vego, no diagnostic of the corpus, the guide, or the trial changed. The cost table was recorded again (units down; problems up where an attempt is open; the guide's quote of `-vego.cost` for `Load` lost its two contradiction checks at a limit). An attempt at the full limit of quick checks made the corpus run slower (57.1 s against 55.5 s), a tenth of it did not (55.4 s). Guards: TestProveIsSound solves its wide problems without e-matching too (1,276 proved, all confirmed by evaluation); `testdata/src/deletes` calls the three entries, verifies a function and rejects a wrong one, and TestAnalyzer requires that nothing in it is left open, which fails with the attempt off and with e-matching kept in it. And in the vego test binary, on every run, every contradiction check that its attempt settles is also asked in full (`solver.CompareModelFirst`, set in `init`, decided by the orchestrator), and the package fails if one answers `sat` and the other `unsat`. The full check is not observed and goes through the in-process cache, so the costs and the diagnostics stay as they are; it adds about 16 s to the main corpus run (55 s to 71.6 s), which the solver package, the longer of the two test binaries, hides, and nothing with a warm `VEGO_CACHE`. Run with speculation off and the checks counted, the main corpus compared 5,976 checks, all the same. A version that fails on agreement fails at once, so it runs on the checks of the corpus.
- **A write names its region once.** Writing one element of one sequence of a region is `Write(T, ref, at, v)`, which means `Store(T, ref, Store(Select(T, ref), at, v))` and holds `T` once; the encoder writes the long form with `T` shared. Written out, the old form doubled the region as a tree with every write, so 24 writes to one slice used more than 4 GB before this.
- **Every formula has a bounded size.** A term records its size as a tree, and building one larger than 2^20 nodes stops the function with `cannot verify F: a formula built to verify it has grown beyond 1048576 nodes`, so that no input uses memory without bound. Terms share structure, but printing and walking them costs their size as a tree. So no rule may put an operand into what it builds more than once: `min` and `max` on integers are a new number with their two bounds and the choice, and a signed `>>`, a substring to the end, the count of a map write, `[]byte(s)`, a bitwise operator whose `and` is an operand, and any write of something read from the region it writes name the repeated part with a constant of its own (`named`, `apart`). The test binary also checks that a value bound or stored is not more than eight times larger as a tree than its distinct nodes, so a rule that doubles fails on any corpus program that uses it twice in a row; `semantics/chains.go` repeats each of these operations thirty times.

Not built yet: the answer cache on systems without `/proc`, such as macOS, which would need another way to identify the files a process runs; one line at the end of a run that says how many answers came from the cache, which needs a driver of `laas` of its own instead of `multichecker.Main`; a region as a two-dimensional array, so that a field written through many references that may be the same is read back cheaply (see above); starting Z3's context early, tried and dropped (see Speed); counterexamples in diagnostics, which the models make possible once the names of the logic are mapped back to Go and a model is checked against Go's states; floats through Z3's floating-point theory; and moving to new Z3 releases with a test that the corpus still verifies. A move of the release must pass, besides the corpus: the comparison of every contradiction check's first attempt with the check in full, which the tests run every time, since the attempt rests on what `:ematching false` does in this release; the measurements behind the chain without extensionality (see Problems in which a region holds sequences are solved without extensionality); `vego/repeat.sh` on the trial, 20 runs two at a time with `-vego.cost`, none differing from the first (see A process ended for another asker serves no one); the memory a process carries across `(reset)`, measured again as F24 did, with `(get-info :all-statistics)` after each `(reset)` of a long captured stream (see What a run can depend on besides the source and the binary); and an empty answer cache, which the identity of the binary gives anyway.

### Structs and pointers

Built: the memory model, structs as values, pointers to structs, methods, `Modifies` clauses checked in both directions, nil checks, slices that share arrays (assignment, slicing, `append` in place or moving, and since variadic calls `append(s, t...)`), `fresh` and `within`, trusted contracts for methods and for functions returning pointers and slices, and contracts with `Modifies` across packages.

Decisions:

- **Sharing is reasoned about, not forbidden.** The verifier models memory as a whole. Where two pointers may refer to the same struct it considers both cases, so a contract must hold when they are equal or require that they differ.
- **Memory is a set of allocations, and a pointer is an allocation and a position in it.** Everything that Go allocates in one piece (a struct made with `&T{...}`, the array of a slice, a variable whose address is taken) is one allocation. A pointer holds which allocation it points into and where; a slice holds the allocation, the position of its first element, its length, and its capacity. Structs lie at byte offsets, as the compiler places them, so that a struct inside another struct or inside an array has a position of its own and a pointer to it is an ordinary pointer.
- **Memory is split by struct type and field, which is sound because the split follows what Go can express.** There is one region for each field of each struct type, ignoring names and tags since conversion ignores them, and one for each element type of a slice of anything but structs. A region holds, for each allocation and position, the value of that field there. Two regions never hold the same memory: without `unsafe`, the same bytes can only be seen as fields of two struct types when one converts to the other, and those share their regions. Within a region nothing is assumed apart: two pointers, or a pointer and a slice, overlap unless the facts at hand say otherwise. An earlier design gave each pointer a whole object and assumed that objects of different types never met; a converted pointer broke that, and it was replaced rather than patched.
- **A variable whose address is taken lives in memory.** The function is scanned for `&x`, `&x.f`, and calls of pointer-receiver methods on `x`; such a variable gets an allocation where it is declared, a new one each time the declaration runs, and every use of it reads or writes that allocation. The variable of a `for` loop gets a new one for each iteration, holding the value the last one ended with, as Go does from 1.22 on; in a file compiled as older Go the iterations share it, and taking the address of a `range` variable there is not supported. Other variables stay values the verifier holds itself, which is cheaper to reason about.
- **`Modifies` is declared, and checked in both directions.** A function lists what it may write, as paths from its parameters read on entry. A write to anything else that existed on entry is an error, and so is a listed entry that no write needs, so the declaration cannot drift from the body. Callers know that everything unlisted is unchanged. Whether an entry is needed is decided by a quick proof attempt for each write; when that attempt is inconclusive the entry counts as needed.
- **A slice entry covers the capacity.** `Modifies A` allows writing every element up to the capacity of `A`, because an `append` to `A` may write there. Callers lose what they knew about that stretch, and cut the capacity with `A[:n:n]` to keep the rest. A finer entry that stops at the length, for functions that never append, is possible later.
- **Slice parameters of one element type do not overlap**, capacities included: assumed on entry, proved at every call. There is no way yet for a function to say that it tolerates overlap.
- **A field of a type that is not modelled is still part of its struct.** It is a hidden part that is copied, overwritten, and forgotten with the struct, so that assigning the whole struct counts as writing it. A struct that holds an array is the exception and is not modelled at all, since a slice of the array is a second view of the struct's memory.
- **A value without size has no identity.** Two pointers to such values may or may not be equal in Go, so nothing is assumed about where they are, and annotations may compare them only with nil.
- **`defer` is a call at every return.** The function, receiver, and arguments are settled where the statement is written; at each return the results are assigned to their variables, the calls that were put off are verified last one first like calls written there, and the results are read back from their variables, since a deferred function literal may assign them. Inside a loop the number of calls is not known, so that is not supported; a `defer` belongs to its own frame, so one inside a function literal that the loop calls is.
- **Nil.** Every dereference carries a proof obligation that the pointer is not nil. The verifier generates and discharges it; the user writes `Requires p != nil` for parameters and nothing at the dereference.
- **An `append` that moves must be able to allocate twice what it needs** (repaired with variadic calls, dbc1cc8; reviewed against `growslice` and `nextslicecap`). The runtime does not allocate the length an append needs: it picks a capacity of its own, twice the old one below 256 and a quarter more after, or the new length when that is more than twice the old, rounds it up in bytes (to a page above 32 KB), and panics with `growslice: len out of range` when that is more than `maxAlloc`, even when the length is not. vego checked `grown * size <= MaxAlloc`, so `memory.Doubled`, which appends up to `|A|` elements with `|A|` bounded only by memory, verified for a slice the runtime cannot grow, and so did an append to a full slice whose length fits (`memory.GrowFull`, the defect that guards it). An append moves only when the old capacity is less than the new length, so the capacity picked is less than twice it: the check is now `2 * grown * size + 65536 <= MaxAlloc`, with the same message. `memory.Doubled` needs `@Requires |A| <= 1000000` now.
- **A nil slice has no length and no capacity** (with what the trial found wrong, F17). `s == nil` is the slice's reference being 0, and the type facts now say that such a slice has capacity 0, true of every slice Go makes without `unsafe`; before, `if a == nil { return len(a) }` did not prove a result of 0 (the trial's `nillen`, which forced three rewrites of path's lazybuf). The fact is in every problem with a slice, so most counts of `testdata/costs.txt` moved a little; in functions that verify, the open counts that grew are quick contradiction checks after a call (four in `guide.Apply`, after the call through `f`) that answered sat before and stop at the quick limit now, which is the quiet direction there, and no message changed.
- **A pointer within a struct lies at a field of its type** (with what the trial found wrong, F17). `within(p, q)`, for two pointers to structs and a pointee of non-zero size, is `p` not nil, in `q`'s allocation, at `q`'s position plus the offset of a field of `p`'s type at any depth through struct fields, or `q` itself when the types match, ignoring tags (`offsetsOf`). The byte range it was before allowed positions where no value of that type lies, so `@Modifies l` with `@Requires within(p, l)` did not cover a write through `p` (the trial's `withincover`). A struct that holds no field of the type makes the clause an error. A slice keeps the range, since a slice target covers one.
- **A type declared inside a function is a type like any other** (F18, from the trial's digraph.somepath and a handler). The declaration executes nothing. Its regions come from `typeKey`, which keys by Go's identity of types through a `typeutil.Map` of the untagged underlying type, so two local types of one name in two functions are two types, and a local struct with the fields of a package-level one shares its regions, as a conversion between them needs (`smalls.Converted`). Contracts and states are read only on declarations at package level, so a local interface or function type has no contract, and an annotation anywhere on a local type declaration, its doc comment and the comments of its fields and methods included, is reported (`localTypeAt`), as one that no step reads. An `Assert` or a `Snapshot` directly above one is read in the body, since such a doc comment holds no annotation and those two belong there (F14 of the review of step 6). `TestRuneString` also evaluates the facts in Go at every code point, since a byte changes inside a range at each multiple of 64, 4096, and 262144, and the values given to Z3 missed a wrong divisor there (F13).
- **`append` forks.** Whether an append writes in place or moves the slice is decided by the capacity, which is often unknown, so execution continues once for each outcome. That is why an append has to be a whole right-hand side or a returned value. Inside an expression it would have to be a write on condition of the capacity instead, made the way a call on the right of `&&` is (see Order of evaluation), with a header that is one of two; that is not built, since using it for every append would change the proof of every append.
- **Loops forget memory by region.** A region the loop writes, in its body, its post statement, or its condition, gets a new unknown value. If every write to it goes through a variable the loop does not assign, or through a slice the loop only appends to, everything else that existed before the loop is known to be unchanged. Otherwise only what existed on entry and is not listed in `Modifies` is known to be unchanged.
- **A loop keeps what a call keeps** (changed on 2026-11-19, with state that a value owns, decided by the orchestrator; F10 of the review). A write to one struct, or to some of its fields, is a target whose cells are known (`target.single`). A layer of a region kept unchanged only what lay outside the positions from the target's first cell to its end, in every region the struct reaches; it now keeps everything but the position of each of the target's cells in that region, which is exactly what `havoc` writes for the same target. The old rule lost the fields of a second struct whenever the model could not rule out that it lay a few bytes after the first in one allocation: two distinct `*sync.Mutex`, one locked in a loop while the other is held, and the same with `reset(q)` on a `Point`. A layer now records its region (`layer.key`). A call also roots its write at a variable when it is passed a pointer to a field of the struct the variable points to (`fieldHolder`), as `h.mu.Lock()` passes `&h.mu`; before, such a write fell back to forgetting all of `Modifies`. The corpus `layers/` has the programs that verify now and defective ones for each way the rule could keep too much: a variable the loop assigns, pointers that may be equal, a pointer into a slice of structs, a write through an element, a field written directly or by a call, a nested loop, a literal called in the loop, a state beside a field, a field behind a second pointer, and a promoted field. Since F26 the model knows that two struct values of one type are one value or at least its size apart, so a write of `p.b` keeps `q.a` under `p != q`, with or without a loop (see Buffers and builders).
- **Where a pointer or slice comes from is stated with `fresh` and `within`.** A function that returns something new says `fresh(result)`; one that appends to a slice it holds says `fresh(s.items) Or within(s.items', s.items)`. Without such a clause the caller knows nothing about what the value may share.
- **`fresh` in a body is "allocated since the function was entered"** (F18, from the trial: path.Clean's `out.append(...)` on a local lazybuf, ring.New's `p.next = &Ring{prev: p}; p = p.next`, digraph.split's `current = utf8.AppendRune(current, r)`, each a loop that assigns memory of the function's own through a call or a field and so forgets that it is). In an `Invariant` or an `Assert`, `fresh(x)` is `freshSince(entryBrk, x)` with `x` read where the annotation stands: the bound that `checkWriteOr` uses for memory the function may write without a `Modifies` entry, so the clause states exactly what the frame needs, and an invariant that says it is checked on entry and preserved like any other. It is the notion of an `Ensures`, measured at the annotation, as `old` means the entry inside a body. Inside an in-place or handed literal the verifier and its bound are the function's, and inside a literal used as a value they are the literal's own, which is what each frame uses. It says nothing of what else refers to the memory, which the frame does not need: a caller knew nothing of memory made after it called. Refused, with a message that says where it may stand: in a `Requires` and in the invariant of a type. The message for a write through a variable that a loop assigns says, as a condition, to add `// @Invariant fresh(p)` if the memory is the function's own (`writtenThrough`); `smalls.GrowsCallers` keeps a program where it is not, whose invariant then fails on entry.
- **A nil interface value is fresh, and a nil pointer or map is not** (F18, F7 of its design review). `fresh` of an interface value read `home(0)` for nil, which nothing constrains since a nil interface value has no home, so neither `fresh(x)` nor `~fresh(x)` was provable of a nil `x`, and an entry's `fresh(result)` for one that may be nil constrained `home(0)`. It is now `x = nil Or before <= home(x)`, as a slice without capacity is fresh; that only weakens what entries give, and every shipped entry with `fresh(result)` of an interface also says `result != nil`. A nil pointer or map stays not fresh, so `fresh(p)` gives `p != nil`, which contracts such as `fresh(result) And result.n = 0` rely on.

Not built yet:

- **Checking a trusted `modifies` against source.** For a trusted function whose source is available, analyse the body and report a write the entry does not declare. This catches some wrong entries; it cannot certify one, because the analysis stops at assembly, `unsafe`, and calls through interfaces or function values. The trust report should say for each entry whether the check ran to completion.
- **A wider search for inputs.** A function that returns an error has to have a contract that some input makes succeed. That input is looked for among samples of a fixed shape: slices up to 4096 long whose elements are all alike or a literal the contract mentions, strings of one repeated byte or a literal, pointers followed two deep, and maps with one key. Z3 picks the numbers of a sample, and a proposal is accepted only once the facts about it are proved; up to 32 shapes and four proposals for each are tried. An honest contract whose only successes lie outside those shapes (elements that differ from each other, a chain of three pointers, a map with two keys) is reported, in a message that says the search found nothing, and its author has to add a clause that a simple input satisfies. When Z3 shows that no state at all forces success, the message says so instead. Shapes could be chosen by Z3 too, with one sample of symbolic shape whose choices are constants.
- **Checking that annotations mean something.** An index out of range or a division by zero inside an annotation is not an error today. The plan is the usual one: each formula gets a condition under which it is defined, read left to right through `And`, `Or`, and `->` and through the bounds of quantifiers, and that condition is proved where the formula is: a `Requires` from the clauses before it, an `Ensures` in the state on return, an invariant at the loop head.
- **Arrays.** Until they are built, a struct that holds an array is not modelled at all, because `s.arr[:]` is a slice into the struct and a hidden field would not show that writing the struct changes the slice. The plan: an array field of `N` elements of a single value occupies `N` positions in the region of its element type, starting at the field's byte offset, which cannot collide with a neighbour since every element takes at least a byte; an array of structs lies at byte offsets like a slice of structs. A cell then covers a run of positions, and an array value is a sequence, as a string is.
- **Pointers to anything but a struct**, such as `*int` or `*string`. A field of type `int` lives in the region of its struct and field, and an element of a `[]int` in the region of `int` elements, so an `*int` could point into either and would have to say which. The way to build it is to key the regions of single values by their type alone, at byte offsets, for all structs and slices; fields of different structs then share a region and are told apart by position, which costs the solver the separation it now gets for free. Worth doing once it is measured.
- **Linked structures** such as lists and trees. They can be built and read, but a loop that walks one forgets every node the function may modify, and there is no way to state an invariant over all nodes.
- **`apart` of two pointers, and of a pointer and a slice** (left out of F18, which built `apart` of two slices). A struct pointer's extent is known, and since F26 two struct values of one type are known to be one or apart (Buffers and builders), so the term is that of `separate`; what is left is the formula, its proof at calls, and its assumption on entry.
- **Letting a function accept overlapping slices.**
- **Runs of elements in `Modifies`**, such as all the structs a slice of pointers refers to.

### Order of evaluation

Built on 2026-11-19: a call that modifies memory may stand wherever an expression may, in the condition of an `if` or a `for`, the tag and the cases of a `switch`, as an operand or argument, on the right of an assignment to anything, among several results, in the arguments of a `defer`, and on the right of `&&` and `||`. Before, it had to be a statement of its own, the only returned expression, or the whole right-hand side of an assignment to variables.

Decisions:

- **One meaning, or reported.** Go fixes the order of calls, `&&`, and `||` among themselves (lexical, left to right), runs an operation after its operands and the right of `&&` and `||` after the left, and stores the targets of an assignment after every operand. It does not fix when the other reads of memory happen relative to the calls. A read whose value would depend on that order is reported where it is written, as an obligation that failed, not as `cannot verify`. It is never given the meaning one compiler happens to give it; gc reads after the call in every form that was tried.
- **Decided by the value, from the contract.** The operands of one statement, condition, case expression, range expression, or `defer` are one unit. Memory has a version at the start of the unit and after each call that may modify it and each `append` that writes into its array. Each read records from the syntax which versions Go may let it see: from after the latest call or decision of `&&` or `||` among its operands, or the decision whose right side it is on, up to before the innermost call whose receiver or argument it is part of, the decision on whose left it is, or the `append` whose operand it is, and otherwise up to the end. The verifier proves that the read gives the same value in each two consecutive versions of that window, under the guard of the read. It compares values and not places, so a call that writes back what was there does not collide; what a call may change comes from its `Modifies` and `Ensures`. Variables the verifier holds itself are not memory, and no call can change them.
- **The message shows the fix where one fits.** It ends with a statement that makes the read first, as in `, as in n := s.n before it`, with a name that is not visible at the read and not declared in the block that holds the statement. It shows none for a read Go may make on some runs of the statement only, or more than once: on the right of `&&` and `||` (any read under a guard), in a case expression, in the init statement or condition of an `else if`, and in the condition or post statement of a loop. Nor for a read described in words, such as the receiver of a method, or for one that would call something if made again.
- **A read that is not recorded crashes the run.** Every read of memory goes through `table`. While a unit is open, a read there outside `readAt`, or outside `quietly` (what a contract says, and the bookkeeping of a map write), panics with an internal error, whether or not the read tells a state what it learns. So a read site added later without its order cannot pass quietly. The one read Go makes that does not go through `table`, the copy an `append` makes when it moves, is recorded on its own; the other uses of regions outside `table` are writes.
- **A read is as narrow as Go's.** A field of a struct that lies in memory is read alone, and a pointer receiver reached through embedded fields reads only the embedded pointers on the way.
- **An allocation made in a unit** is put into the earlier versions by the same terms. Nothing could refer to it there.
- **Built-in functions are not ordered like calls,** since the specification does not say that they are (decided by the orchestrator). An `append` runs after its operands, and is ordered after the calls of the targets only by a call or a decision of `&&` or `||` among its operands. Its write in place is a version of the unit. When nothing orders it, every call in the targets must modify nothing and have a signature that refers to no memory, with no pointer, slice, or map at any depth of the receiver, the parameters, or the results; otherwise the function cannot be verified. Every read of the targets, including those in the arguments of such a call, is compared with the write. The copy of an `append` that moves is a read like the others. `u[t[0]] = append(t[:0], 5)` verified before and was unsound, since Go may write `t[0]` first; it is now reported. The rule on signatures rests on contracts naming only parameters, results, and error variables; since package-level variables, a contract that names one whose value refers to memory (a pointer, slice, or map at any depth) makes the call one that reads memory, at all three places that decide it (`callMemoryFree`, for static calls, calls through function values, and calls through interface values), also for an imported contract, whose keys travel in its fact. A variable whose contents are only read counts as reading memory too, which is stricter than needed.
- **A call on one branch** (the right of `&&` and `||`, a case expression) keeps the single state with guards. Its targets are forgotten as usual, and where the guard fails, memory and the bound of allocations are as they were. No region becomes an `Ite`, so the layers of loops are walked as before. A call of a function literal on one branch joins the state where it did not run with the ends of its body, and a region that differs becomes a constant of its own, which `table` follows like a layer (see Function literals).
- **A variable that a function literal assigns is read like memory.** No other call can change a local variable, but a call of such a literal can. Each version of a unit holds the value of every such variable under a key of its own, and a read of one in an open unit is recorded, as narrow as Go's for a field. A write of the same value does not collide.
- **A condition of a `for` loop that modifies memory.** What it writes is forgotten at the head with what the body writes. The invariants hold where the condition is about to run, and the variant is measured there too, so it must decrease over the condition and the body together: a condition could otherwise undo what the body did. The loop is left in the state after the last evaluation of the condition.
- **The keys of a map literal must differ** unless both are constants, since Go does not fix the order of the entries. `map[int]int{a: 1, b: 2}` with `a == b` verified before and was unsound; it is now reported. A literal whose two keys may be equal is reported even when their values are equal, as in `map[int]int{a: 1, b: 1}`, which has one meaning.

Not built yet: an `append` inside a larger expression (see Structs and pointers); a call in the targets beside an `append` that may write in place, when its contract reads memory, which would need a frame of what contracts read and is not planned; a call with several results as the arguments of another call, `g(f())`; and accepting two keys of a map literal that may be equal when their values are equal.

### Function literals

Built on 2026-11-19: a function literal that is called or deferred where it is written, or that declares a new local variable (`:=` or `var`) which the function only calls. The literal may read and assign the variables of the function and of literals around it, write memory the function may write, and use everything a body may, including loops, `defer`, and further literals of these forms.

Decisions:

- **Verified in place, at each call.** A literal has no contract. Its body is executed at each call, in the state there, as part of the function around it, so every check of the function applies to it, its `Modifies` clauses included, and annotations in it are checked at each call. Each call runs in a frame of its own: `return` ends the literal, its results are assigned to its result variables, its own deferred calls run, and the end state goes back to the call. A diagnostic found in a body names the calls it came through (`, in the function literal called at f.go:L:C`, or `deferred at ... and run at the return at ...`), and the deduplication of reports then lists each failing call.
- **Only where every use is seen.** `literalUses` binds a literal declared with `:=` or `var` whose variable is only called, and refuses a use of such a variable but as the function of a call when the variable has the literal's own type. Every other literal is a value, made where Go converts it to a function type (see Function values). A literal used in place never leaves the function. It can outlive the block it is declared in when it is deferred, but `defer` stays refused in a loop unless it is inside a literal that the iteration calls, so no literal used in place runs after the iteration whose variable it captures. That is why a loop variable is the same for it in Go 1.21 and 1.22. A literal used as a value can run after its iteration; it knows the variable of the iteration only where Go 1.22 gives each iteration its own and nothing assigns it, and otherwise nothing of it.
- **Recursion cannot happen.** A variable is not in scope inside the literal that declares it and cannot be assigned again, so no literal reaches itself. A guard reports one that would. A call of the enclosing function from a literal is recursion of that function, checked against its variant. The analyzer counts a call of an in-place literal as resolved: its body is part of the function's callees already.
- **The ends of a body are joined.** A call inside an expression needs one state. The states the body ends in, and under a guard the state where the call did not run, become one: a constant `path` names the end, the facts each end added hold under `path = i`, and every variable, result, and bound of memory that differs gets a constant equal to that end's value under `path = i`. A region that differs gets a constant recorded with its alternatives in `joins`; `table` states, at each reference it reads, that the region there has the length and every element of the region of the end `path` names, and walks on into the alternatives with a set of what it has seen. So no term holds more than the name of what an earlier join made, and a chain of calls does not grow. The join is a `forward` for `tieFailures`, and a fact stated later at a read of a joined region is added to it. Measured against the same logic written with `if` statements, the join is faster: 2.1 s against 4.1 s on three functions with three ends per call, and 0.37 s against 0.47 s for a deferred literal with six ends run at three returns. Sixteen joined calls on one region take 0.21 s and forty take 0.48 s, where twelve written as `if` statements stop at the limit on paths after 34 s; twenty-five deferred literals with an `if` each take 0.24 s. So the join is used for statement calls too, and a second way that continues once per end was not built (decided by the orchestrator).
- **Captured variables are shared.** They are the function's own, read and written in place. The order rule covers them (see Order of evaluation), a loop forgets what a literal it calls assigns since `effects` walks the bodies of bound literals called in it, and a join merges them.
- **`old(x)` names the parameter at that point.** Inside a body it used to be resolved by name among the parameters, so a variable that hid a parameter, as a parameter of a literal does, was read as the parameter. That was wrong and is now an error.
- **Origins of errors.** A parameter of an in-place literal gets the arguments of its calls as sources, and one of any other literal is unknown. A `return` is a source of the named results of its own frame. A named error result that any literal assigns is judged, at every return, by everything assigned to it, since a deferred literal may change it after the return; any literal counts, which can only make fewer functions publish known errors. Since F20 the origin is decided along the paths for a variable that no literal but one deferred where it is written assigns, and a use inside a literal is decided from every assignment (see Errors, Origin is decided along the path).
- **`recover` is `cannot verify`** (decided by the orchestrator). In a verified function nothing panics, so `recover` could be given the value nil, but that would need an assumption about the compiler: gc gives nil to a deferred function that runs because its function returned, also while a panic further up is running deferred functions and during `runtime.Goexit`, and the specification's sentence on `recover` read literally allows a non-nil value there. It returns `any`, so the only form gained before dynamic types would be `recover()` as a statement. It waits for the fifth part of interfaces (see the list at the top).

Not built yet:

- A call of a literal with several results as the arguments of another call, and a literal whose parameters or results have types that are not modelled. Variadic literals are built (see Variadic calls and formatting).
- A call of a literal in the targets of an assignment beside an `append` that may write in place. It is always refused, also when the body changes nothing and reads no memory.
- `recover`, with the fifth part of interfaces.

### Function values

Built on 2026-11-19: a contract on a named function type, in its doc comment; values of every function type held, passed, stored, returned, and compared with nil; calls and deferred calls through a value of a type with a contract; and a function, a method value, or a function literal made into a value of such a type where Go converts it, as in `Apply(xs, double)`, `handlers[name] = h.serve`, `return func(c *Config) { c.Timeout = d }`, and `for _, opt := range opts { opt(&cfg) }`. Contracts of function types travel to other packages as facts.

Repaired on 2026-11-19, with interfaces: a trusted method made into a function value, as in `w.Get`, was not checked like a call written out. `checkHandedOver` asks for `runs` on a receiver that can hold code at a call, but `madeFrom` looked only at the parameters. A trusted `(*lib.Wrapper).Get` that runs a `func()` the wrapper holds, with `modifies: []`, made into a value and called, let a verified caller keep a fact about memory that the function wrote. `madeAs`, where function values and interface methods meet, now requires the receiver under `runs`, and then stratification refuses the method, since it may call through what the receiver holds (773e276; `interfaces/trusted.go`).

Repaired on 2026-11-19, with trust-file contracts for types of other modules: a literal made into a value of another package's function type resolved the error variables of that contract in its own file, so a literal returning `funcvaluesclient.ErrStop` verified against a contract that says `err = nil Or err = ErrStop` of `funcvalueslib`. `madeLiteral` now gives the literal the spelling of its type's contract (`funcInfo.spelled`), which name resolution uses (aaf38ce; `funcvaluesclient/client.go`, `WrongStop`).

Repaired on 2026-11-19, with the next feature: `effects` gave a call through a function value nothing to forget, so a loop, a literal called in a loop, and a deferred value call in such a literal kept what the call's type lists under `Modifies`. `Configure` with `@Ensures cfg.Timeout = 0` verified, and is false. `callSites` now gives such a call the `Modifies` of its type, with roots found as for a static call (18b28f2; `funcvalues/callsdefects.go`).

Decisions:

- **A contract is written on a named type, and only there** (decided by the orchestrator). It travels with the value into parameters, results, fields, slices, and maps, which a contract on a parameter would not. `func Apply(xs []int, f func(int) int)` has to declare a type; a call of `f` says so. The contract names the parameters and results of the signature, its results must be named, and it has `Requires`, `Ensures`, and `Modifies` only.
- **A function value is one reference, zero for nil.** Nothing else is known of it: Go compares function values only with nil, and the behaviour of a call comes from the type. In a formula a value is `FuncVal(ref)`, which `RefEq` unpacks and `Incomparable` refuses beside anything but nil. A struct field of function type, which used to be a hidden leaf, is a modelled leaf; nothing that verified before changed.
- **A call through a value is a call of the type's contract.** It is an event of the unit, its value and arguments are operands before it, the value must not be nil, and its frame is checked against the caller's. Where a verified callee and a trusted one differ, it is treated like a trusted one: the promise is checked against the state, no struct invariant is assumed of what it hands back, its errors have unknown origin, and a weak contract makes it a source of unpredictable failure. An argument that can carry code nobody has seen (an error of unknown origin, a value of an uncontracted function type, an `any`, an interface value with a method that has no contract) is refused, since the value may come from code that is not verified. An interface value whose methods all have contracts is not such an argument (see Interfaces).
- **A value is made in few ways, each checked.** From a function, a method value, or a literal written where Go converts it to the type; from nil; or from a value of an identical type. Anything else is refused, among them `Mapper(p)` for a `p` of another named type with the same underlying type, which would give a value a contract nobody claimed. Making a value of a type without a contract is refused too.
- **A value is checked as a function of its own** (`subVerifier`). Its verifier has its own parameters, entry memory, and allocation bound, at least the one where the value is made. What is known where the value is made comes in as a copy in which every constant and bound variable is renamed with the prefix `^ `, except the constants of error variables and nil. So the copy says what is known of the values the function value keeps, and nothing of the memory it sees when it runs. No name the verifier makes starts with `^`; `fresh` panics on one. A function is checked as a body that calls it, a method value as one that calls it on the receiver evaluated where the value is made, and a literal by running its body. A diagnostic ends in `, in double used as a value of type Mapper at f.go:L:C` or `, in the function literal used as a value of type Mapper at f.go:L:C`.
- **What a literal used as a value captures.** A variable that nothing in the declared function around it assigns but its declaration, whose address is not taken, and that is not a named result keeps the value it has where the literal is made. A `range` variable of Go 1.22 and later counts as declared by its iteration. Any other captured variable is an unknown value of its type; for one in memory the pointer to it is fixed and what it holds is read from the literal's memory. The literal may not assign a captured variable and may not use one bound to an in-place literal, so no call through a value changes a local variable, and in-place literals and the order rule stay as they were. The post statement of a three-clause loop counts as an assignment, which loses the variable of an iteration in Go 1.22. That is a loss, not an error. The design first sketched captured variables living in memory; that is not needed once nothing a value runs can assign them.
- **What a value runs makes no call through a value** (stratification, decided by the orchestrator). No contract of a function type has a variant, and a value could be handed back to code that calls it. So a function, method, or literal made into a value must not reach a call with no static callee, a range over a function, or a call of a function that may make one. `mayCallValues` is the least set of this package's functions closed under calls, from their bodies, trusted functions included, also by `runs` on a parameter that can hold a function; other packages publish `CallsValues` in their contracts. Calls in a literal used as a value are not recursion of the function around it. Since F17 the call graph finds a cycle through any value whose code is in the package, and stratification and the graph share one test of a call that may run code held in a value, `handedCode` and the graph's sites (see The call graph, under Function values). What stratification is still for is the case across packages: a verified function of another package, made into a value here or called by what a value of this package runs, has a body that this package's graph cannot see, so `CallsValues` in its published contract says whether it may call through a value, and the value is refused when it may. What remains assumed is what code of another package keeps and runs later. The same rule settles what a function assumes unchanged across a call that may run a value: a value's writes stay inside its type's frame, and no value can assign a local variable.
- **A contract on a type must be one that can be met.** A value from outside is assumed to meet it, so a contract that no outcome meets for some call its `Requires` allow would make the promise a fact about the arguments, as `2 * y = x` would make `x` even. `canBeMet` proves, from the `Requires` and the types, that some outcome exists: Z3 chooses the results, the new allocation bound, and a new value for each struct or field that `Modifies` lists. Slices and maps keep their contents, so a contract only a change of their elements or memory the call creates could meet is refused. An interface value the outcome produces chooses its home as it chooses its value (`chooseHomes`, since what the trial found wrong, F17): the facts become a disjunction over which produced references are nil, and one that is not nil gets a home of its own. The outcome writes produced values into what `Modifies` lists too, so each read of a cell through such a write is first written out as the value where the place is the one written and what was there otherwise (`readBack`), which means the same; a produced reference that then occurs anywhere but inside its home or compared with 0 keeps the home the table gives it (F12 of the review of step 1, which found reads through those writes using the table). For one that occurs only there, which number it is decides nothing, since a formula compares an interface value only with nil, so it can be taken to be new; and a converted value's home has no value of its own in the model, so any home below the bound after the call is one that some outcome has. A value read from memory that existed on entry is typed with the outcome's bound, which made it a goal about the table; its fact with the bound on entry is given (`typedReads`). Z3 does not find a home and a bound above it by itself, so beside the question go instances of it: new homes at the bound on entry, and the home of each interface value the entry reads outside a quantifier. Before, `@Ensures True` on a method returning `any` was refused (the trial's `anyresult`), and so were `x != nil`, `x = nil`, and `fresh(x)`. Still refused: a state of a new result, which lies in memory the call creates (`trialfaults.FreshClosed`), a result that only one of the values in a slice it is given meets (`trialfaults.Picks`), since no instance is offered for a value read under a quantifier; a mix of one new home and one old, as in `a.c' != nil And fresh(a.c) And a.d' != nil And ~fresh(a.d)`, which moving the old `c` into `d` meets, since the instances give every chosen home the same value (S2 of the review of step 1); and a fact about a read under a quantifier, as `Forall i ... xs[i].c != nil -> ~fresh(xs[i].c)`, since its type fact is not given with the bound on entry, as `typedReads` gives it for a read outside one (S6 and Q4 of that review). Each is refused loudly, and each was refused before F17 too. Facts that reading the `Ensures` notes about the entry state are given, not goals. A primed path must read memory that `Modifies` lists, as in a trusted entry.
