  - Not built: a writer the caller made by a conversion or got from a constructor with `fresh(result, w)` (step 5, the fourth case of the design), and escape of new memory into memory another argument cannot reach (F20).
- **A method declared on an interface with states that lists its receiver changes the states only** (D2, decided by the orchestrator: no contract that is legal changes its meaning without a word; F31 of the review of step 2). Before footprints, such a contract changed the states of the value and nothing else, and `(io.Closer).Close` is one. Giving it a footprint made every `Close` forget what may lie in it, and a dozen programs of the guide, `ifacestate`, and `variadic` that verify stopped. So a footprint comes only from a method declared on an interface without states, and an interface with states gets one by embedding such an interface (`SinkCloser`, `PutThenClose`). The claim is in the reason of `Close` and in "What a proof assumes": a `Close` writes nothing verified code can see, which a `gzip.Writer` over a buffer verified code holds breaks. Step 1 refused a call through a value with states of a method that writes behind its receiver; that refusal is gone.
- **Shipped owners** (step 3, merged commit 2 of the plan). A shipped file declares, in an `owns` section, the fields whose memory a value of a struct type of the standard library owns: `bytes.Buffer` and `strings.Builder` their `buf`, `bufio.Writer` `buf` and `wr`, `bufio.Reader` `buf` and `rd`, `bufio.Scanner` `r` and `buf`, `log.Logger` `out` (`addOwns`, `ownedFields`). A project file cannot declare one: what a type of another module owns depends on fields its module may change, and what a type of the project owns is part 2 (`@Owns`). `TestOwnsDeclarations` holds every declaration against the toolchain's types: the field exists, is not exported, and holds a pointer to a struct, a slice, a map, or an interface value.
  - **`Modifies p` of a pointer to an owner covers what it owns**, read where the target is located: the array of an owned slice from its start to its capacity, an owned map, the struct an owned pointer points to and what that owns, and the footprint and states of an owned interface value (`ownedParts`, `target.owned`, spread by `targets` as targets with `part` set and the index of their clause in `mod`). A call forgets them, a loop forgets them by region (`ownedSites`), and the frame check of a part is reported as `what bw owns`, once per owner when the owner's struct is refused. The check that `Modifies` lists nothing a function never needs counts a clause once, by `mod`.
  - **What an owner owns grows only by memory the call creates**: after a call that writes an owner, what each owned field refers to is what it referred to before, within the same array for a slice, or new (`ownerGrowth`), and so at the head of a loop that calls it for an owner the loop does not assign. It is what lets a Scanner made in the function be scanned in a loop and a Writer passed in be written twice. It holds of the shipped entries by the assumption that "What a proof assumes" states, and of a verified function because its own calls are such. Of any other function it is not known, so a call of a project's trusted function, a function value, or an interface method that may write an owner is `cannot verify` (`footprints.ResetsByProject`): `Reset` may hand a Writer a writer of the caller's, and a project entry says nothing about that.
  - **The growth claim is tested against the real types** (F32 of the review of step 3): the harnesses of the shipped entries of the bufio types and of the Logger read the owned fields by reflection around every call of a shipped method, and fail unless each owned slice stays inside the stretch of its array it held or lies in an array that overlaps none the harness had seen, and every other owned field keeps its value (`ownerArrays.checkGrowth`). `TestGrowthCheck` shows the check failing on `Scanner.Buffer` with an array of the caller's and on `Writer.Reset`, and passing a Scanner that grows into a new array; a harness whose `Flush` resets the Writer first, or whose `Printf` sets another output first, fails. `Buffer` and `Builder` get the same with their entries (step 4).
  - **An owner is not copied**, as a value with a state is not (decided by the orchestrator): `holdsState` counts a type that owns memory, and the refusal says why (`ownerCopyReason`, `footprints.CopiesBuffer`). The bufio types and the Logger declare a state already; `bytes.Buffer` and `strings.Builder` are new to the rule.
  - **`fresh(p)` of an owner** also says that what it owns is new (`ownedFreshSeen`), the footprint of an owned interface value included since the first commit of step 4 (F2).
  - **The cost, and four changes it needed.** Owners alone multiplied the cost of the functions that use the bufio types by 10 to 80 (`CountLines` 7.1 M units to 61 M, `Report` 0.4 M to 35 M), and `WriteTotal` and `Report` drew the always-fails report at Z3's limit. The questions that failed were searches for a model, after a call that writes a buffer. Each change is about how a fact is stated, not about what is known, and each is sound on its own:
    - **A write of a stretch of unknown values is a term** where it was a quantified fact. `SpliceAt(table, ref, inside, lo, hi)` is the table whose row at `ref` holds `inside` from `lo` up to `hi` and the old row elsewhere, with the table once as in `Write`; it expands to `Store` and `Splice`, an SMT-LIB function defined with a lambda and emitted only in problems that splice. A read of a row of a spliced table is reduced to an `ite` of two reads (`reduceRead`), and a pattern never reads a splice (`shiftOf`). Z3 cannot build a model of a quantified "kept outside the stretch" fact, which is why the searches failed; it can of the term. It is used for what an owner owns only. A row Z3 sees whole is a lambda, which takes the patterns away from the quantified facts that read it: for `utf8.AppendRune`, whose type facts read the slice it writes, the contradiction check went from 13 k units to 4 M. The caller names no owned memory, so few facts read it. `TestRules` has the reads at both ends of the stretch, beside it, in another row, and through a write that makes Z3 read the row whole, and `TestProveIsSound` generates splices of sequences and tables with a law that is off by one half the time; each of ten wrong versions of the definition, the reduction, and the expansion fails one of them. `semantics.SpliceChain` writes a Writer thirty times (358 problems, 11.9 M units; with the quantified fact, 50 M).
    - **What a loop's layer keeps is stated of the cell a read reads**, when the position has no bound variable, as `fetch` reads (`tableAt`). The fact was quantified over every position of the row at the reference, also for a read of one field; it is now an instance of that fact. A read of a row as a whole states the quantified one as before. `CountLines` went from 61 M units to 0.9 M, below the 7.1 M of the base.
    - **The contents of an unexported field of a struct of another package have no type facts** (`contentFacts`). No formula of the package can name the field, and what the field refers to gets its facts where it is read through a reference of its own. The facts of a Scanner's `token` and `buf` were quantifiers over the bytes in every question after a call.
    - **The search for the outcomes of a call** takes the type facts of the arguments in memory before the call and of the results, and not those of the arguments in the memory the call wrote (`given` in `apply`). Leaving facts out makes a path likelier to be reached and a call likelier to be a source of failure, the side the always-fails check errs on. The check that a trusted promise contradicts nothing still takes them all. What the check could miss is a function excused by a call that succeeds, or cannot fail, only by what the types say of memory the call wrote. For a trusted callee that cannot happen: a source is a callee whose contract forces success for no input, and `trustForces` asks that with the same type facts (`contractInstance` states them in memory on return, or in memory on entry when no `Ensures` reads memory on return, and then no condition on success can read the written memory). A verified callee excuses its caller only when it is excused itself, and one whose contract forces success is not. So no always-failing contract becomes unseen, and the corpus's reports are those of 6752c3c, the programs of the always-fails check (`trust.AlwaysFails*`, `values/witness.go`, `trialfaults/`) included.
  - Measured against the base of step 3 (6752c3c) on the corpus: 501 M units to 384 M, and 1088 problems left open to 730. No function more than doubled; the most are the bufio trusted contracts (1.6 times, about 30 k units more each). Functions that wrote a slice through a trusted call and failed through it fell most (`buffered.WrapsErr` 9.2 M to 0.16 M, `guide.ReadLines` 7.4 M to 1.2 M).
  - Corpus (`footprints/owners.go`): a slice handed in beside a Writer is read before the write (`ReadThenWrite`) and refused after it (`WriteThenRead`, also in the guide), memory the function makes is kept (`KeptOwn`), and the two refusals. Wrong versions caught: the owned stretch kept whole, owned parts not forgotten at calls, a loop's cell kept without its condition, a project's call allowed to write an owner, and owners copied.
- **`fresh` and `within` over footprints** (the first commit of step 4, which changes no shipped entry; F2 and F12 of the design review). `fresh(x)` of an interface value whose type has a footprint also says that every cell of its footprint is new: its home is new and every cell in its row was made at or past the bound (`freshOf`, `rowNewOrIn`). For an owner it says the same of the footprint of each owned interface value, which `ownedFreshSeen` refused before. `fresh(x, w...)` says that every cell of what `x` refers to, read on return (now, in a body), was made at or past the bound or lies in the footprint of one of the `w`s, read on entry; each `w` must be an interface value whose type has a footprint, or the annotation is refused. Cell by cell: a pointer is not nil and every position of its struct is new or in a footprint, and so is every cell of what it owns; a slice up to its capacity, a map at its cell, and an interface value's home must be new and its row's cells new or in a footprint. `within(x, w)` for an interface value `w` says that every cell of `x` (a pointer, a slice, or a map) lies in `w`'s footprint, read on return in an `Ensures` (`withinRow`); for an owner `w`, `x` may also lie inside the stretch of an owned slice or in the footprint of an owned interface value (`withinOwned`). The facts of `rowAt` stand as premises inside the quantifiers. `within(slice, pointer)` no longer allows the slice inside the struct's bytes unless the struct holds an array of its elements by value (`holdsArrayOf`); arrays are not supported yet, so no verified program could reach that case. The origins count a result placed within an interface value or an owner as unknown, since it may be memory a writer writes (`PassesTable`). `canBeMet` gives the outcome a growth layer on the footprint region and offers, beside the homes it chooses, an instance in which their rows are empty, so that a contract with `fresh(s)` of an interface value can be met (`Factory.Make`, `SinkMaker`). Corpus (`footprints/fresh.go`, `guide/footprints.go`): a write through a new sink keeps an old pair (`MakeAndKeep`, `LogToNew`), `MakeAndWrite` no longer needs `Modifies`, a sink made over `w` is written under `Modifies w` and refused without it (`WrapAndPut`, `LogThroughTee`), a pair and a map placed in `w`'s footprint and a slice within what a Writer owns are written under `Modifies` and refused without it, and `fresh(p, q)` with a pointer `q` is refused. Wrong versions caught: `fresh` ignoring its other arguments, `fresh` of an interface keeping old cells, no empty row offered, `within` a footprint holding nothing, a result within an interface counted outside, and `within` an owner ignoring the end of the stretch.
  - **A result within the receiver of a method that lists it** (F34 of the review of step 4), the form of `(net/http.ResponseWriter).Header`: a contract may name its receiver only to place a result within it (`placesWithin`, `Viewer`); `canBeMet` offers an outcome in which a result that holds memory is new and lies, whole, in the rows of the footprints that `Modifies` lists, and the receiver of an interface method is not nil on entry, since a call through a nil interface value panics first (`Tabler`); and the frame check of a write covered by a listed footprint states what the layers of the current footprint region say of the cell, so that a cell a call put in the row lies in the row on entry or in memory made since (`StoresInTable`, `WritesGrown`). Wrong versions caught: the premises of the current row left out, the instance not offered, the receiver allowed to be nil, and a receiver named for `within` refused.
  - **Messages** (F35): a write through a value that a call made over a writer names the writer to list and the call, as `it may lie in the footprint of w, as the contract of wrapSink at fresh.go:36:7 says; list w in a // @Modifies clause`, and a write through a pointer such a call returned says that its contract places it in the footprint of `w` when it is not new.
- **D1 and the entries** (the second commit of step 4, decided by the orchestrator: a trade, not a repair). `(io.Writer).Write` names its receiver `w` and lists it, `(io.Reader).Read` lists `r` and `p`, and `fmt.Fprint*` list `w` (with `vouches: [w]`). So a function that writes through an `io.Writer` or reads through an `io.Reader` lists it, and so does one that reads through a `bufio.Scanner` or a `bufio.Reader` made over it, or through what `io.NopCloser`, `io.LimitReader`, or `io.TeeReader` return: 32 functions of the corpus and the guide gained `Modifies` (8 in the guide), none of whose meaning changed otherwise. What the entries that keep a writer or a reader say of their result became `fresh(result, kept)`: `bufio.NewScanner`, `NewReader`, `NewWriter`, `log.New`, `io.NopCloser`, `LimitReader`, and `TeeReader`; their harnesses know a result that is what the argument holds. What D1 needed so that nothing that verified stops (each decided or confirmed by the orchestrator):
  - **A conversion of a value whose methods write nothing behind their receiver** (taken forward from step 5): when each method of the concrete type that implements a method writing behind the receiver lists nothing rooted at its receiver (`writingImplementation`), the converted value gets a new home whose row is empty, a layer of the footprint region that `rowAt` states per cell (`emptied`). That row is what its methods may write, so the invariant of rows holds. `*os.File` and the corpus's `discard` types are such values (E12, E14, E15: `Usage`, `LogsToStderr`, `ToStdout`, `ToFile` list nothing); a counter whose `Write` writes its receiver is refused (`CountsThenFormats`), and an unlisted writer beside a converted file is still refused (`ConvertsThenWrites`).
  - **`formats` claims that the function formats every value before it writes or runs anything its entry lists**, so `Fprint*` may list `w` and the Logger may run its writer; `calls` stays a defect (`TestFormatsBeforeWriting`).
  - **`Modifies` of an interface value covers its footprint and the states of what it holds**, except the receiver of a method whose interface declares no state (F7 (a)): a trust entry's `modifies: [r]` on an `io.Reader` meant the states of what `r` holds before `io.Reader` had a footprint, and keeps that meaning (D2; `ConsumedUnlisted`).
  - **The sharing check is keyed on contracts that name a state of both parameters** (F5; `namesStateOf`): `Echo(rwc, rwc, buf)` verifies once `Pump` lists its reader and writer; `CloseOne` now names the state of its second parameter, so handing it one value twice is still refused.
  - **A nil interface value has no footprint and no states**: the frame check of a call that writes one and the layers of an epoch hold nothing of a nil value's row, since none of its methods can run (a Writer's kept writer may be nil to the model), and the frame check of a call that writes the states of what a nil value holds holds nothing. The second was reported at the base too, as `Maybe may modify the states of nil` for an `io.Closer` handed nil (`NoCloser`, and `CloserUnlisted` still refused); it would have reached every interface with a footprint now that `Modifies` covers the states of what such a value holds. A nil value handed for a footprint parameter is still refused at the proof of separation (`PassesNil`, below).
  - **The owned interface value of a shipped owner is separated like an interface parameter** (the reviewer's note on step 4): a function that lists `bw *bufio.Writer` assumes what its other parameters reach outside the footprint of `bw.wr`, and a verified caller proves it (`target.ownerParam`, `WriteAllKeeps`, `KeepsBeside`, `CallsWriteAll`; `CallsWriteAllLocal`, whose Writer is made over `w`, is refused until the fourth case of step 5).
  - **The fact before a call leaves out the call's own pollution** (`skipPollution`): what a call stores happens while it runs, which its callee accounts for; the pollution of other calls of the node, as in its arguments, still counts (`PollutesInArgument`).
  - **`io.MultiReader` and `io.MultiWriter` say `fresh(result, readers)` and `fresh(result, writers)`, read only where a call writes each element out** (decided by the orchestrator). A pack is a new slice of known length, so there the clause is the finite statement `fresh(result, a, b)` (`specEnv.packed`): `Joins` and `Copies2` verify with `Modifies a, b`, and `JoinsFiles` and `ToBoth`, over files, list nothing. Anywhere else the slice's length is unknown and the clause says only that what the result holds is new, which is weaker than the claim. That is how the checks of the entry itself read it (the check that it does not contradict itself, and the always-fails check's question whether it forces success): neither could find a contradiction or a forced success in where a footprint lies. A call that hands a slice whole sees it so too, and the write through what it returns is refused with a message of its own (`returned.spread`): `JoinsAll` and `guide.ReadAllParts`. A contradiction in such a clause is still caught at a call that writes the elements out, where the finite form is checked: `keepslib.Joined` of the corpus trust file says `fresh(result, rs)` and `~fresh(result)`, which contradict each other only where each reader's footprint is empty, and `JoinsContradiction` over two files reports it while the entry's own check and `JoinsContradictionGiven` do not.
  - **The question whether a promise contradicts what is known at a call takes what the row and stretch claims stand for**, quantified (`claimDefs`). Assumed at the cells read only, two claims about one row that cannot both hold never met there, as `fresh(result, rs)` and `~fresh(result)` did not; the claims stay lazy everywhere else. The definitions are those of the claims the call's own promise makes: a claim left by an earlier call stays lazy in that question, so a promise that contradicts the `fresh` of an earlier call is not reported there. That is quiet but not unsound, since the lazy claim of the earlier call says less than its promise and the contradiction cannot be used either (noted by the reviewer of step 4).
  - Two entries of the corpus trust file were false under footprints and are fixed: `(io.Reader).Read` listed only `p` for readers that write their own struct, and `keepslib.Closing` said `fresh(result)` of a closer that keeps `r`; `(*keepslib.Box).Fill` now lists `b.r`, and `Wrap` says `result.r = r`.
  - **The methods of `*log.Logger` that print list `l`** (F36 of the review of step 4): they write through the writer the logger owns, so their `modifies: []` left the F14 hole open through a logger, as `LogThenClose` and `LogsUnlisted` showed by verifying. They now list `l`, which covers what the logger owns, vouch for it, and ensure `ready(l')`, which the logger harness checks. Eight functions of the corpus gained `Modifies l`, or `Modifies w` for a logger made over `w` (two in the guide: `Inventory` and `LogsTo`); `LogThenClose` and `LogsUnlisted` are refused. No other shipped entry that runs a value lists nothing it writes: `(*bufio.Scanner).Text` and `Err`, `(*net/http.Request).PathValue`, and the `os.Is*` functions run nothing that writes. Its cost: `formatting.Logs` 3.3 times and `guide.LogsTo` 2.4 times, from the epoch each print now starts.
  - **A value whose footprint a call may not write is reported once**, not again for the states of what it holds, which the same `Modifies` entry would cover (the reviewer's note on step 4).
  - **What stops verifying, unsound before.** `CopyOnce` and `Relay` (F14), which verified at the base, are refused, since a write through `w` may close `rc`, as a `ResponseWriter` may close the body of its request; `CloseFirst` closes first and verifies.
  - **What stops verifying, not unsound before** (decided by the orchestrator). `iowrap.JoinsAll` and `guide.ReadParts` handed `io.MultiReader` a slice whole. They are refused because the model that lets a buffer be a writer cannot say what the readers of a slice write; the guide's program became the written-out form (`ReadParts(header, body)`), and the slice form is `ReadAllParts`.
  - Not built: a `Modifies` form for the footprints of a slice's elements, which `io.MultiReader(rs...)` and `io.MultiWriter(ws...)` wait for; a nil writer handed for a footprint parameter, refused at the proof of separation (`footprints.PassesNil`, w4 of the reviewer), since the proof asks of an argument that the caller's own `Modifies` lists it.
  - **The cost, and how it was won back** (decided by the orchestrator: the performance work before the commit). D1 alone took the base's rows of the corpus from 374 M units to 440 M, `LogToFile` 87 times. Each change states a fact differently or asks less, and leaves what is known as it was, but one: the contract instance of the always-fails and contradiction checks keeps what the footprint of an owned interface value holds, which narrows the outcomes those checks look at (they can only find more), named for the reviewer. The others: the empty row as a layer; the row and stretch claims of `fresh(x, w)` and F2 stated per cell where a promise is assumed (`rowClaims`, `stretchClaims`, a boolean in place of the quantifier, its instance at the first cell of a stretch), the quantified form kept for goals; the reach closure of step 2 stated only for values and containers whose types the other parameters can reach; the check that a clause is needed asked once per clause, not again for one known needed, and the question whether a written cell is new skipped when nothing is left to learn. On the base's rows, cold: 374.1 M units to 358.6 M, and problems left open 761 to 801. The open problems that rose (`SpliceChain` 44 to 76, the scanner and reader functions of `guide` and `buffered` up to 16 each) are all first attempts of a contradiction check at a call that writes a footprint, before and after its promise, made without e-matching at a cap of 20 k units: they stop at about 23 k, and the full check after them answers (captured on `CountLines`). Their cause is the epoch that each such call now starts, which makes the state at the next call larger; bringing them down would mean a higher cap for every contradiction check or a smaller state after an epoch. What more than doubled, each named: `WriteBuffered` and `WritesLocally`, which now verify where they stopped at a refusal; the scanner loops (2.4 to 2.9 times), `SpliceChain` (2.2), `Echo` (2.2), and `ConsumedUnlisted` (2.0), from the same epochs; the contract checks of the constructors that now say `fresh(result, kept)` and of a few small functions of `trialfaults` and `footprints`, under 25 k units each; and three programs that before stopped at a defect this commit removes. Cold `go test ./vego/...`, measured one after the other from a load of 2.9 before the first (a screensaver on one core; the tests themselves raise it to 8): 126 s at `5edc51d` and 125 s with this commit, which the solver's package bounds; the `vego` package alone took 110 s and 123 s.
- **Conversions** (step 5, first commit). A pointer to a struct of the project converts to an interface whose methods write behind their receiver, and the footprint of the interface value is the struct, fixed at the conversion:
  - **The row of a conversion's home is defined at the conversion** (decided by the orchestrator, attacked by the reviewer; it replaces the case split of the design's 1.3, and F4's bound of eight conversions goes with it). A layer of the footprint region says, of each cell `rowAt` reads, that it is in the row at the new home exactly when it is in the structural footprint as it is then (`layer.defines`, `structuralFootprint`), the generalisation of the `emptied` layer of step 4, which is the case of a value whose methods write nothing behind their receiver. After that the row is a row like any other. Why it is sound (F18's invariant: no fact makes a row smaller than what the value's methods may write): at the conversion they may write exactly the struct, which the method check shows; rows keep the cells of memory that existed, which the struct is; and no owner is converted in part 1, so nothing a converted value holds grows after the conversion. Why it replaces the case split: every reader of footprints already goes through `rowAt`, so the frame check, the epochs, the separation, `fresh`, and the proof at a call need nothing of their own; nesting needs no unfolding and so no bound; and one row is read at a call, not a disjunction over the function's conversions at every cell. What it loses against the case split is memory an owner makes after its conversion, which no fact keeps out of the row; with no owner converted, nothing. The reviewer's three conditions: the definition is an `iff`, and a nil pointer has none (`PutsNil`); every call that may grow an owner leaves a growth layer, which matters once owners convert; an owned interface field's row is read once, at the conversion.
  - **The check of each method** assumes that the struct lies in the row of the interface method's receiver (`placeFootprint`), which holds at every call by the definition and because rows keep old cells, so `(*Clicker).Inc` with `Modifies c` meets `Incr.Inc` with `Modifies r`, and a method that writes outside its struct is refused: `Inc may modify what it calls l.next, which Incr.Inc may not modify; it is not part of the footprint of *Linked, which is the struct l points to`.
  - **A footprint write is covered by a stretch the frame lists**, not only by a footprint it lists (`checkFootprintWrite`): every position of an object is one of its leaves, so `Modifies c` covers the footprint of a converted `*Clicker`, and a map listed whole covers the map. The message names the conversion: `i.Inc may modify the footprint of i, which BumpUnlisted may not modify; it is the memory that c points to, converted at conversions.go:47:15; list c in a // @Modifies clause` (`convertedAt`).
  - **A value converted in a loop** has a home that is new at the loop's head, so its row is unknown after the loop and a call through it is refused (F18, `ConvertedInLoop`), with a message that says so (`convertedInLoop`, F40); one converted before the loop keeps its row (`BumpInLoop`). A pointer a call returned, converted, is reported as memory the call returned without saying it is new, with the remedy of taking it as a parameter, and a struct held by value whose method writes through a pointer field as having an empty footprint (F40).
  - **The fourth case of the proof at a call, for results** (`placedFootprint`): a variable assigned once, where it is declared, from a call whose contract says `fresh(result, w)` for arguments that are placed in turn, as `bufio.NewWriter(w)` and `log.New(w, ...)` do, is placed: what the call made is new, and another argument reaches it only through a reference to the result, which is of unknown origin, so a store of it pollutes the function, which the proof requires it is not. Test 5's caller verifies (`CallsWriteAllLocal`), and so does a logger made over a writer and handed on with data (`LogsReport`); storing the Writer where the other argument reaches it is refused (`StoresThenKeeps`), and so is a Writer made over a writer nothing places (`OverUnplaced`).
  - **The fourth case of the proof at a call, for conversions** (F21, F39 and F41 of the review of step 5): a pointer converted at the call, explicitly or where an interface value is wanted, or a variable declared from such a conversion and never assigned again, has the struct it points to as its footprint, and another argument reaches that struct only if a value of its type can refer to a value of the struct's type, at any depth, by what each kind can share memory with in Go: a pointer (and an `atomic.Pointer`) with memory that holds the struct inline or lies inline in it (a `*Inner` points into an `Outer` that holds an `Inner`); a slice with an array that holds the struct, or an array the struct holds, which `s.a[:]` makes of a field `a`, but never with a field that is not an array (so a `[]int` beside a struct with an `int` field is apart, as an `*http.Request` is from a `*Repo`, though it reaches `x509`'s `[]int`; while arrays are not supported in verified code, the array case is never met); a map or a channel with nothing, since what they hold lies in memory no pointer reaches, so they only lead on to what their keys and values refer to; with two types the same when Go converts pointers between them, by their underlying types (a `*Twin` with `Outer`'s fields); it stops at interface values and function values, as the callee's own assumption does (`reachesType`), since what an interface value holds is no part of what it assumes apart (`convertedPointer`, `typeReaches`, `holdsInline`). This holds wherever the struct lies, a parameter's or new, so it needs no escape analysis. `UsesConversion(c, log)`, `UsesNewConversion`, `UsesConversionAny` (a `[]any` beside the counter), `Serve` (the orchestrator's `handle(repo, req)`), `IntsBesideCounter`, and `MapBesideCounter` (a `map[string]Clicker`) verify; a `map[string]*Clicker` (`PointersBesideCounter`) is refused, and so are a second `*Clicker` (`UsesConversionAlias`), a pointer into the converted struct (`Overlap`), the same pointer as a `*Twin` (`AsTwin`), a struct that holds the counter inline (`UsesConversionWrapped`), and an `atomic.Pointer[Clicker]` (`UsesConversionAtomic`).
  - **Not built: the fourth case for new memory that has not escaped** (F20's refined rule, decided by the orchestrator to wait). A counter the function made, handed on beside an argument whose type can reach a `Clicker`, cannot be that argument's, but types alone cannot show it: `useWith(&Clicker{}, d)` with `d *Clicker` (`UsesNewBesideSame`) is refused with `the footprint of &Clicker{} is the struct that &Clicker{} points to, which a value of type *Clicker can refer to`.
  - **Not built in this commit.** Converting a `*bufio.Writer` or a `*bufio.Reader` to `io.Writer` or `io.Reader`: their methods call through what they keep, which stratification refuses (`WrapsWriter`), so `bufio.NewWriter(bufio.NewWriter(w))` is refused; relaxing it for the shipped wrappers needs an argument that their kept chains are finite and cannot reach back into verified code that calls through them. A fact about another pointer of the same struct type across a call through a converted value, which the model did not know (`KeepsOther`, `d.n` under `c != d`), is built since F26 (Buffers and builders: two struct values of one type are one value or lie apart; `distinct.KeepsOtherCounter`).
  - **Writing a `bytes.Buffer` or a `strings.Builder`** stays refused in part 1 (decided by the orchestrator: (b), narrow). Their writing methods panic when the buffer is too large to grow: `bytes.Buffer` with `ErrTooLarge` when `growSlice` cannot make the larger of the length plus `|p|` and twice the capacity, or when `c > maxInt-c-n`, and `strings.Builder` in `append` past `maxAlloc`; on a 32-bit target either is reached at one or two GB. A sound entry needs a bound, which needs the buffer's length and, after a `Reset` that keeps a large array, its capacity too, so whatever part 3 designs must bound both, as a second state or as an invariant the writing methods keep under their `requires`. The reviewer proposed a way that needs no ghost: for a shipped owner whose `owns` names exactly one slice, `|b|` and `cap(b)` read that slice's length and capacity from memory, so `Write` can require `2 * (cap(b) + |p|) + 65536 <= MaxAlloc` and ensure what `growSlice` picks. It is the first candidate for these two types (the orchestrator: a good idea, and not part 1), with one question open that is to be decided once, with the entries of both types, their harnesses, loops that write, `Reset`, and whatever integer states become for interface values, so that there are not two half-related ways to say how long something is: for a `strings.Builder` the owned slice's length is `Len()`, but for a `bytes.Buffer` it is the write end, which is `Len()` only until something reads from the buffer, so `|b|` would mean something a user cannot see from the documentation of the type; it may be the right answer with the right wording, or the measure may want a name of its own. The orchestrator intends buffers and builders as writers to be the feature right after part 1, ahead of ownership in source, and decides the order when part 1 is accepted. A call of a growing method (`growingMethods`) and a conversion of either to `io.Writer` are refused with a message that says why and that a trusted contract without the bound would be false (`growsUnbounded`), and the guide says so where trust entries are described. When the writing methods of `strings.Builder` ship, it gets no `copied` state: the sentence of "What a proof assumes" that says code which is not verified does not copy a `sync.Mutex`, `sync.RWMutex`, or `sync.Once` after its first use is extended to a non-zero `strings.Builder`, whose documentation says the same, with the harness showing the panic of `copyCheck` on a copy (decided by the orchestrator on F38 of the review of step 4, changing its earlier ruling: one rule from one kind of documentation should not be an assumption for one type and a `Requires` on every function for another). The reviewer's program for when the Buffer ships: `w := io.Writer(&b); b.WriteString("abc"); p := b.Bytes(); first := p[0]; b.Truncate(0); w.Write([]byte("z")); return p[0] == first`, under `@Ensures r`, must be refused. Built as F26 (Buffers and builders), with `|b|` the length `Len` returns and no capacity: the sentence above that a bound needs the capacity after a `Reset` is wrong, since `grow` allocates only when the slide to the front fails, which bounds the capacity by the length and what is written. The reviewer's program for when the Buffer ships, without the interface value the conversion would make, is `buffers.BytesOverwritten` and is refused; with it, it is refused at `io.Writer(&b)`.
  - Wrong versions caught: the defined row only around the structural footprint, the method check without the struct in the receiver's row, frame stretches not counted for footprints, the arguments of `fresh` not placed in turn, a nil pointer given its struct, and the buffers not told apart. The defined row only within the structural footprint is a weaker fact and still sound, since a caller keeps only cells it proves outside a row (the direction soundness needs is the method check's assumption, whose wrong version is caught); what the other half gives is `within(c, i)` of a conversion, which `ConvWithin` proves, and which the wrong version fails (the reviewer's program). Also caught: the type walk finding nothing (`UsesConversionAlias`), its two directions each dropped (`Overlap`, `UsesConversionWrapped`), types compared by name (`AsTwin`), `atomic.Pointer` not walked (`UsesConversionAtomic`), a slice taken to reach a plain field (`Serve`, `IntsBesideCounter`), a map's memory taken to reach the struct (`MapBesideCounter`), and a map's values not walked (`PointersBesideCounter`).
- **Facts under a quantifier about what the other parameters reach** (O1 of the orchestrator's review of part 1). The cause: a read under a bound variable was given no state to tell what it learns (`follow` passed `nil` after a bound step, and `element` and `mapValue` noted nothing for a bound index), so the closure of what the other parameters reach (`noteReached`), the type fact of what was read there (`noteTyped`), and what a layer of an epoch keeps at the cell were stated only at ground reads. A fact quantified over the elements of a slice of pointers, or the values of a map of pointers, about what they point to, one or two pointers deep or through a slice field, was therefore lost at a write through a footprint, in a loop invariant, an `Ensures`, or a callee's promise alike, while the same fact about one element was kept. The fix, where the cause is: while a formula's quantifier is read, those reads tell a state of their own that gathers their facts (`specEnv.OpenBound`, `CloseBound`, the `Gatherer` of the logic); the quantifier takes the ones that mention its variables as premises of a `Forall` or conjuncts of an `Exists`, and states them on their own as `Forall vars : guards -> facts` (`Keep`), so that a formula that is assumed has them as facts, not only as premises (premises alone weakened an assumed `Requires` in practice: its mutant breaks programs of `mapping` and the guide that verified). A fact that mentions only a quantifier around passes to it; one that mentions a variable no formula's quantifier binds, as the verifier's own quantifiers over the elements of a slice do, is left out, and a typed read under a quantifier is not restated outside it by `canBeMet`. Gathering happens only in a function whose Modifies lists a parameter with a footprint or one that owns memory (`listsFootprint`), the only functions whose separation uses it, so that a function without one, as `guide.TotalUnsaid`, costs what it did; and what is gathered is what a write through a footprint can use: the facts of layers with footprint targets, and the type facts, the closure, and the facts of a map only of values that refer to memory. A read of the memory before a call gathers its type fact with the bound before the call (`boundBefore`, F43 of the review of O1): with the bound of the outcome, the existence check took it for a goal the outcome must meet, and refused contracts such as `r -> (Forall k in [0, |ps|) : ps[k] != nil)` that it had accepted (`QCheckingBook`, `CountChecked`). Only the outermost quantifier states its facts on its own (F44): a quantifier inside another has its facts as premises only, since nesting a quantified fact of its own in every quantified formula over pointers made `Forall i : |xss[i]| <= 5 And (Forall j : xss[i][j] != nil)` cost past any limit (`KeepsLengths`). The closure is about the contents on entry, as the separation it proves requires, and does not reach through an interface value. Programs (`footprints/quantified.go`): `RecordAllThrough` and `RecordAll` (the orchestrator's B1 and B2), `KeepsEvery`, `KeepsOwners`, `KeepsTags`, `KeepsMapValues`, `KeepsAfterCallee`, `KeepsLengths`, `KeepsSome` and `KeepsSomeOf` (an `Exists`), `CountChecked`, and `CheckedBy` verify; `ListedAcross`, `OwnItemsAcross`, `ItemsOfBookAcross` (a slice the function made of a pointer the book handed out, which the write keeps while what it points to may change), and `ReownedAcross`'s second assertion (an owner the book handed out, put into an item of the parameter; that every item has an owner is kept, which needs the premises) are refused. Not proved at the default limit, and true (F45): `KeepsNested`, a fact two quantifiers deep across a write, and an `Exists` across a write over items whose first field is a pointer, as `{Owner *Owner; Qty int}` (the reviewer's `ExistsAcross`), which is not proved even at eight times the limit, while the same with the pointer after the quantity, the corpus's `QItem`, or with no pointer verifies at the default (`KeepsSome`). Not built: a footprint listed by a path, as `@Modifies h.B` for `h *QHolder{B QBook}` or `@Modifies s.out` on a method of a struct that holds its writer, gets no separation, since the separation is assumed per listed parameter, so nothing the other parameters reach is kept across a write through it, not even one element (`KeepsOneThroughField`, F47 of the review of O1, as at 0e54ce1); it is the form of a struct that holds its writer, which ownership in source carries since F27: a struct that declares `@Owns out` and is listed whole gets the separation C3 for `out` (Ownership in source, step 3b); a path still gets none. A twin for a map through an interface value cannot be written yet: an `Ensures` quantified over the keys of a map that an interface method returns is not proved satisfiable, and a map in the footprint named by a `Requires` would contradict the separation the function assumes. Wrong versions caught: the facts not stated on their own, not taken as premises (`ReownedAcross`), an element or a map value under a quantifier stating nothing, a gathered read stating its cell unchanged without the footprint (the unsound one, which `ItemsOfBookAcross` catches), a read of the memory before gathered with the outcome's bound (`QCheckingBook`), and inner quantifiers stating their facts on their own (`KeepsLengths`). Cost on the corpus against 0e54ce1: units 376.1 M to 376.3 M, problems left open 872 to 871; one row over two times, `footprints.Lines` (2.3 times, 0.23 M units, a `[][]byte` beside a writer whose lengths are now kept, with one problem fewer left open); B1 and B2 now cost 0.44 M and 0.90 M units, where at 0e54ce1 B1 stopped at the limit after 4.1 M and B2 failed.
- **Moved out of part 1 to the next feature** (decided by the orchestrator after step 4, with part 1 at twelve commits and 6,800 lines against 5,500 estimated): both go to ownership in source and invariants through owned fields, where the handlers move. Part 1 ends with conversions (step 5) and the last step. Not built yet, with what the design settled for each:
  - **Ghost states of values the project converts** (built as F28, step 4; see Ghost states of values the project converts, and `behind(r)`, below) (rule B lifted: a closer of the project as an `io.Closer`, a `Tx` of the project as `Tx`; E6 of the design). D4: the states of a value made by verified code are ghost, and its implementation may rely on none of them; an `Ensures` of a method that names a state of the receiver is met by choosing the state after the call, so the check proves the `Ensures` with each state atom of the receiver after the call replaced by each of its two values, joined by `Or`. Only the state after the call is chosen: a state read before the call is what the caller's state says, so `IsClosed() (b bool)` with `@Ensures b <-> closed(c)` is refused in its check, and an implementation whose own contract speaks of a state of its struct stays refused as reading receiver memory, with a message that says so. F9: every state of a conversion starts False, as an allocation zeroes the states of a struct, written into the region of states at the new home and nowhere else; `var c io.Closer = quiet{}; c.Close()` verifies, and `c.Close(); c.Close()` is refused at the second `~closed(c)`. The planned guards: the pair in both directions with a mutant that chooses the states before the results, and a `Close` that writes a package-level variable keeping today's message.
  - **`(net/http.ResponseWriter)` and `http.Error`** (built as F28, see The writer of a handler below; the separation drafted here was false at `req.Trailer`). The entries as drafted: `Header` ensures `result != nil And within(result, w)`, the form `placesWithin` already accepts (`Viewer`), so `w.Header().Set(k, v)` writes memory that `Modifies w` covers; `Write` and `WriteHeader` list `w`; `http.Error` requires `w != nil And 100 <= code <= 999`, runs and modifies `w`, and needs a bound on its message, since it formats it with `Fprintln` (whether the formatting assumption about the text of an error can carry `http.Error(w, err.Error(), 404)` was left to check). What Go 1.27.3's `net/http` writes, as the reviewer checked it on a real server (F37 of the review of step 4): the HTTP/1 writer writes no field of `*req`, of `*req.URL`, or of `req.Header`, and it closes an unread body when it flushes the header (wrong at `req.Trailer`, which that close fills: F1 of the review of F28, corrected below under The writer of a handler); the HTTP/2 path builds a separate `*Request` for the handler. Under the separation of step 2, everything reached from `req` without going through an interface value lies outside the writer's footprint, so `*req`, `*req.URL`, and the map of `req.Header` survive a write; only the footprint and the states of what `req.Body` holds do not. F1 of the design review: state cells are ordinary cells of a footprint; F14: a write through `w` may close what `req.Body` holds, which is why `CopyOnce` and `Relay` are refused, and `apart(w, rc)` for two interface values is listed as not built with the note that a handler passing `req.Body` makes it false. The planned tests run the entries against `httptest.ResponseRecorder` and a real server's writer. The trial's handlers need ownership in source (`deleteAllTasksHandler`, and `deleteTaskHandler` if `http.Error` can take an error's text) and JSON for the other five.
  - **A constraint on ownership in source, as binding as the invariant of rows** (decided by the orchestrator with the defined row of step 5; kept since F27 by the follow layer, see Ownership in source, step 4). The row of a converted value is fixed at the conversion, which is sound only while verified code cannot change which memory the value's methods write. In part 1 it cannot: a converted value of the project owns nothing, so its footprint is its struct, and owners are not converted. The day a type of the project owns a field, `var i Store = s; s.m = other; i.Put(k, v)` writes a map that was never in the row of `i`. So a write to an owned field of a value that may have been converted must put what is stored into every row that holds the struct's cells, as the join rule of the design's 1.5 said, or be refused. The same holds of every call that can make an owner in a row grow without going through the value: a direct call such as `b.WriteString(s)` after `w := io.Writer(&b)`, a verified callee handed `b`, a deferred call, a call in a loop; each must leave a growth layer, and the programs the reviewer is to write for it are a buffer grown past its capacity by a direct call or a callee between two calls through the interface value, with a fact kept about memory made in between that the new array could be, and the same for a `bufio.Writer` whose kept writer's row grows. Part 2's pair starts from this.
  - Nothing of the old step 7 (`io.MultiWriter` and `io.TeeReader` beyond what keeps their entries true) is left in part 1: both say `fresh(result, kept)` since step 4, and what remains is the slice form of `MultiWriter`, listed above.
- **The writer of a handler** (F28, being built; step 1). Shipped: `(net/http.ResponseWriter).Header` (`result != nil And within(result, w)`, modifies `[w]`), `WriteHeader` (`100 <= statusCode <= 999`), `Write` (modifies `[w]`; its parameter has no name, so nothing of the count), `http.Error` (the bound of `Fprintln` of one string, `2 * (|error| + 4097) + 65536 <= MaxAlloc`), `http.NotFound`, `(net/http.Header).Del`, and `http.StatusText` (`|result| <= 31`), each held by `shipped_http_test.go` against `httptest.ResponseRecorder` and the writers of a real HTTP/1 and HTTP/2 server, with chunked bodies, trailers declared and not, `Expect: 100-continue`, and bodies under and over 256 KB left unread and half read. Around every call the harness compares everything the request reaches without going through an interface value, checks that the writer comes to reach nothing the harness held, and that `Write`'s slice is unchanged; `TestHTTPHarnessCatches` shows the checks failing on a writer that keeps its slice and on one that writes the request. The harness ran on Go 1.27.3 with `go test` and, by the reviewer, under `GOTOOLCHAIN=go1.26.6` (350 s), passing on both. It calls `WriteHeader` and `Error` with codes -1, 0, 99, 100, 999, and 1000, each code outside the requires only before the final header, where it must panic; an entry widened to 99..1000 fails (F9 of the review of step 1). Three findings of the design, each built:
  - **A writer is scoped** (the stale writer, F2 of the review). "A ResponseWriter may not be used after ServeHTTP has returned": the HTTP/2 writer panics, the HTTP/1 writer writes a pooled `bufio.Writer`. A shipped `scoped:` declaration names the type (matched by the underlying interface, `scopedOf`); the kinds of `ownorigins.go` mark every value whose type is or holds one (an interface type is one when its underlying interface is the declared one's, or when it has every method of the declared type as the same declaration, as through embedding: F8 of the review of step 1, `FlushWriter`), wherever it comes from, and every value converted from or holding a marked one, and refuse storing it into memory, capturing it in a literal that may outlive the call, handing it to a parameter the callee keeps (a verified summary, a trusted `keeps`, a function value, a method of an interface of the project), and returning it (`refuseScoped`). A literal bound to a variable that is only called no longer counts as one that outlives the call (it runs where it is called, as `ownRunLiteral` applies it), which the trial's `dueHandler` needs; the mutant that counts it refuses `handlers.Bad`. Programs: `handlers/stale.go`.
  - **`Request.Trailer` is hidden** (F1 of the review). The server fills it while the handler runs: on HTTP/1 when a write that flushes the header drains an unread body under 256 KB (`readTrailer`, `mergeSetHeader`, also assigning a nil field), on HTTP/2 from the connection's goroutine. A shipped `hidden:` declaration names the field (matched as `owns` is); verified code cannot name it in code or in a formula (`checkHidden`, the planner of paths), and the separation of part 1 does not count the cell, or what it refers to, as reached (`directlyAt`, `reachedBy`, `hiddenOffsets`). Nothing gives verified code a reference tied to the cell without naming it (a trusted result is of unknown origin, and `within` of a map is refused), so the stop of the reach is defence in depth and no program observes it; the harness fails unless a write is seen to change the field on HTTP/1. Programs: `handlers/trailer.go`.
  - **`stores`** (F6 of the review, ruled by the orchestrator). An entry field naming parameters under whose memory, as `modifies` lists it, the function stores only memory it made during the call; `(net/http.Header).Set` and `Del` carry it. Such memory lies in no row: a row grows only by what the methods of its value create (assumed of values from code that is not verified) or, for a converted value, by its definition and the follow layers of stores into owned fields; and the methods of a value from code that is not verified do not write through what another call stored into memory the value handed out (the clause F33 of part 1 added to What a proof assumes, which this rests on). So a call whose every target that can hold a reference is listed there leaves no growth layer (`growsNothing` reads `targetsHold(fn, true)`; part 1's rule that every call with such targets leaves one is corrected where it is written, under F26 and the invariant of rows) and never pollutes: it stores nothing but what it made, whatever it is handed (F10 of the review of step 1; a function that stores what it is handed is what `keeps` is for). A target that `stores` does not list keeps the rules of part 1, so a call of an entry whose other target can hold a reference still pollutes (`FillThenRender`). Shipped and project entries alike; a type entry with it is a defect, and so is a name under which `modifies` lists nothing. `TestStoresClaims` holds it of every shipped entry that lists it, by two calls on two headers whose added memory must be disjoint and reachable from nothing the harness held, and fails on a function that stores its argument and on one that stores package state. Programs: `handlers/render.go` (`SetThenRender` and `RequestThenRender` verify; `FillThenRender` is refused).
- **What a handler reads of its request** (F28, step 3). `req.Method`, `req.Host`, `req.URL.Path`, and the other fields are read as fields; `req.Header.Get` and `req.PathValue` shipped with F19. New: `(*net/url.URL).Query`, `requires u != nil And 32 * (|u.RawQuery| + 1) + 65536 <= MaxAlloc`, `ensures result != nil And fresh(result)`, the bound read from `parseQuery` (the slice of one key holds at most (|RawQuery| + 1) / 2 strings, grown by append; the 10,000-parameter limit of `GODEBUG=urlmaxqueryparams` is not relied on, since code that is not verified may lift it), and `(net/url.Values).Get`, `modifies: []`. `TestQueryBound` measures the worst shape, `a&a&...&a` with the limit lifted, up to three million parameters, against the clause read from the entry (half the factor still holds, a quarter fails), and finds the longest query the clause admits on a 32-bit target, 134 million bytes, whose slice of one key fits. An unproved allocation bound of any trusted entry now offers a bound on each length it reads, through fields too, as `it holds when |req.URL.RawQuery| <= 1000000000000`. Not shipped, listed: `Header.Add` and `Header.Values` (an append in place into a value's array, which no `modifies` names, and a result within a map's value), `mime.ParseMediaType` (a vouch on its error read from source; shipped since F30), `Request.Cookie` and `http.SetCookie`, `http.Redirect`, and `FormValue`, `ParseForm`, and the readers of the body (out of scope). Programs: `handlers/query.go`.
- **Ghost states of values the project converts, and `behind(r)`** (F28, step 4; D2 kept and extended by the orchestrator's ruling on F3 of its review). A value of the project converts to an interface whose contracts speak of a state (rule B lifted): its home is new, its states are False there (`falseStates`, a store at the new home only), and beside them it gets part 1's footprint when the interface has one. The method check (`madeAs`) knows nothing of the states before the call, and for a method whose contract lists its receiver it proves the `Ensures` as an `Or` over every value of the receiver's states after the call, written into the row of the receiver's home (`chosenEnsures`): a choice made per outcome, after the results, over the receiver's states only. New in the language: `behind(r)` in the `Modifies` of a method of an interface with states, of its receiver only, names the receiver's footprint; `r` alone keeps its meaning, the states and nothing else (D2), and `behind(r)` alone writes the footprint and keeps the states (decided by the orchestrator). Elsewhere it is refused with a message that says what to write; only the form with a call is reserved. A method whose contract lists `r` alone must write nothing a caller sees, and its refusal names `behind`. **The shipped `(io.Closer).Close` lists `c, behind(c)`** (F3 of the review, GrewHeld: a gzip writer over a buffer verified code held made `r = 0` false, from the entry alone): closers flush and release what they hold, and `closer_test` holds the claim positively (`closeWrites`: a Close writes, of what the harness holds, only what the closer reaches, and makes the closer reach nothing the harness held; gzip, zlib, and flate writers over a buffer the harness holds; it fails on a closer that writes memory it does not reach, `TestCloseHarnessCatches`). With it, io.Closer and every interface that embeds it have a footprint; a Close starts an epoch.
  - **Two interface parameters with states** (the orchestrator's ruling (a)): where the homes are assumed apart (`sharing`), the states of each also lie outside the footprint of the other, stated on entry for the states each type declares (`statesOutside`; rows gain only what calls make, so it holds after every call) and proved at each verified call after the homes (one report a pair). What a proof assumes words it as "... or a value and one whose methods change the states of the other, as one that wraps it does". Kept by it: guide ShutBoth, ifacestate Both, CallsBoth, CloseOne, KeepOther. Refused: x beside `io.NopCloser(x)` (`ghost.WrapAndShut`), two closers read from memory (`ShutHeld`); verified: a converted closer of the project beside a parameter (`ShutBeside`).
  - **Refused by the repair of Close, not unsound themselves**, each with what it waits for: `variadic.CloseFirst` (a closer in a slice read again after the close: a footprint listed by a path gets no separation; building it would bring GrewHeld back as an assumption about callers, as Grew is, never as a proof); `iowrap.ClosesApart` (`io.NopCloser(orig)` closed, then `orig`: an entry cannot say that one method of a wrapper writes nothing of what it keeps); `ifacestate.Done`, over a `Response` of the project (the guide's `Status`, over `http.Response`, verifies again with the declaration of the second commit). Messages that changed only: `CloserUnlisted`, `GetStale`, `Elements`, `ClosesTwice`; `stateroot` gains the reports that statea and statec were proved without `(statelib.Conn).Stop`'s entry, which now decides whether a Conn has a footprint.
  - Programs: `ghost/` (CloseQuiet, CloseQuietTwice, Shutter and Stopper, StopForgets, a Tx over owned maps, CommitUnbegun, Peek, IsOn, a Close writing outside its struct, GrabLazy, the sharing programs, Grew and GrewHeld) and the guide's Lamp pair. Mutants caught: the choice off (Stopper, the Tx), the choice made before the results (the Tx's Begin), the False states off (every first call, the completeness direction), the fact on entry off (ShutBoth), not proved at calls (WrapAndShut), and the states of another parameter chosen too (GrabLazy). Not observable: F4 (a) of the review, the states of the conversion let into the method check, since the receiver of the interface method there is a value of its own; F4 (b), a method that does not list its receiver and ensures a primed state, is refused where it is declared (the primed-path check), and with an unprimed one its Ensures is not satisfiable.
- **The body of a response leaves the response alone** (F28, step 4, second commit; ruling (b) of the orchestrator, in place of an `owns` declaration, with the reviewer's four points). A shipped `notWrittenBy:` declaration names `net/http.Response`'s `Body` (`notWrittenOf`): what a Response's Body holds writes nothing the Response reaches without going through an interface value but its hidden fields, and `Response.Trailer` is hidden as `Request.Trailer` is (reading the body fills it). The claim is about responses from code that is not verified, in What a proof assumes, and `TestResponseBody` holds it of real responses over HTTP/1 and HTTP/2, with and without trailers, chunked and not, gzip-decoded by the transport, read to the end and closed early: everything the response reaches but Trailer, its request and the request's URL and headers included, is unchanged after every Read and Close; the test fails unless a Read was seen to change Trailer, and fails on a body that writes the response's ContentLength or its request's URL. Rules:
  1. Verified code does not store into such a field but new memory converted where it is stored (`newAtStore`: `resp.Body = &myBody{}`, whose footprint is what it makes), and does not store a whole struct that has one into memory that is not a local variable (`*resp = r2`, a field or an element of that type, an `append` to a slice of them); a copy into a local, `r2 := *resp`, and a response built with no Body or a new one are kept (`checkNotWritten`, refused as cannot verify with a message that names the declaration).
  2. A function that lists `p.f` for such a field of the struct p points to assumes on entry what p and the other parameters reach outside the footprint of `p.f` (`notWrittenRoot` in `enterReaches`: part 1's separation, p among the roots, its hidden cells left out).
  3. At each verified call, the argument for `p.f` must be the caller's own `p.f`, listed by it on its own parameter p that it never assigns (`ownFieldFootprint`), and every argument that holds memory, p included, outside, and the caller clean (part 1's origins). Refused, each by its own mutant: a caller that lists `resp` and not `resp.Body`, a response from a trusted call, one read from memory, and `&r2` of a copy.
  Programs (`handlers/response.go`, guide): the guide's `Status` and `Drain` (a read and a close, then `resp.StatusCode`) verify; `StoreBody`, `MakeWith`, `Overwrite`, and the guide's `ReplaceBody` are refused by rule 1, whose remedy is a body made where it is stored (`ReplaceNew`, `MakeEmpty`) or a copy into a local (`LocalCopy`); the guide's `Replace` now shows the rule for an interface field with a struct of its own, `Job`. `ifacestate.Done`, over a `Response` of the project, stays refused: a field of the project would be owned with `@Owns` (unexported) or wait for footprints listed by a path. Mutants caught: rule 1 off (`StoreBody`, `ReplaceBody`), whole stores allowed (`Overwrite`), the placement not checked (`DrainListed`, `DrainHeld`, `DrainCopy`), the separation of `p.Body` off and p not among its roots (`Status`, `Drain`).
- **Converting between interfaces by contract** (F28, step 5; ruling 6 of the orchestrator, the design agreed with the reviewer, F5 (a) to (g)). An interface value converts to an interface whose methods are not all declarations it has when, for each such method m of the target, the value's method of the name has a contract that implies m's (`convertByContract`, `impliesMethod`): a verifier whose contract is m calls the value's method with the same receiver, so the same home, footprint, and states, and the same arguments, and must meet m's Ensures and frame; the requires of the value's method is a precondition there, a wider modifies a frame error, and a method that writes behind its receiver against one that does not is refused for naming the receiver. Refused before the check (`claimsNoLess`): a state of the target the value's interface does not declare (its home row was set at no conversion), a value's method that may keep more (a method of an interface in source keeps everything, so no such value converts to `io.Writer`), vouches for less, or asserts more. A state-changing method against a method of an interface without states that lists its receiver is refused by the frame (F5 (f)). An explicit conversion `io.Writer(w)` of a listed parameter is placed as `w` at the proof at a call (`placedFootprint`), and the marks of the stale writer pass through the conversion. With it, `(net/http.ResponseWriter).Write` names its unnamed parameter `param1` (ruled by the orchestrator: an unnamed parameter of a function or method a trust entry describes is `param1`, `param2`, ... by position, as unnamed results are `result1`, `result2`; a clash with the receiver or a named result is a defect, and so is `paramN` written for a parameter that has a name, `nameUnnamed`) and ensures `0 <= result1 <= |param1|` and `result1 < |param1| -> result2 != nil`, read from source and held by the writer harness on HEAD, on a declared Content-Length exceeded, and on the recorder (a stricter ensures fails it); so a `ResponseWriter` converts to `io.Writer` and `fmt.Fprintf(w, ...)` verifies in a handler. Programs: `handlers/convert.go`, `guide/convertby.go`; the two programs of `interfaces` that the old refusal named (`DefinedFromDenier`, `OwnDeclaration`) are refused by the implication and by a missing contract. Mutants caught: the implication not checked, the claims not compared (`SinkAsWriter`, `PlainAsVouching`, `LooseAsWriter`), the states not compared (`SinkAsWriteCloser`), the placement not unwrapped (`Converted`, completeness), and the states covered by the receiver of a stateless method (`GateAsUser`).
- **The cost of F28.** On the rows of the base (ff1816f), 420.3 M units to 426.2 M and 1,012 problems left open to 1,038; 203 new rows bring 19.0 M. The rows that more than doubled: `trialfaults.FillsBoth.Fill` (25 k to 4.0 M, three problems at the limit, the same refusal), whose satisfiability check carries the footprint an `io.Closer` parameter now has; `ifacestate.TwoStates` (18 times) and `Transact` (7 times) and the programs that close two values (`ShutBoth`, `Both`, `FieldAndParam`, about 2.3 times), from the epoch each Close now starts and the facts of two states outside each other's footprints; `guide.Quiet`, `ifacestate.Make`, and `interfaces.DefinedFromDenier`, which are checked where they were refused at once; and four small trialfaults rows of `fresh` of a closer (under 15 k units each). Cold `go test ./vego/...` back to back, from a load of 3.8 and 7.8: 144 s at the base and 142 s at the end of F28.
- **Not built, from F28**, each with what stops it:
  - `Header.Add` (an append in place into the array of a key's slice, which no `modifies` names) and `Header.Values` (a result within a map's value); `mime.ParseMediaType` (shipped since F30, see Invariants kept by frame, step 5), `Request.Cookie` and `http.SetCookie`, `http.Redirect`, and `FormValue`, `ParseForm`, and the readers of the body (out of scope).
  - A struct of the project that wraps a `ResponseWriter` and is passed on as one (a status recorder): storing the writer into it is keeping it, owning it is owning what one is given, and its `Write` calls through an interface value (item 12). Message: the stale writer's.
  - A struct of another module that owns an exported field, `http.Response` its first customer: the narrower `notWrittenBy` declaration was built in its place.
  - A local `bufio.NewWriter(w)` or `log.New(w, ...)` over a `ResponseWriter`, the form handlers write: refused by the rule of the stale writer, since the entry keeps `w` in its result ("its entry keeps w in result"). It waits for following the kept writer into the result as the kinds do for literals, so that a local that never escapes is not a keep.
  - A footprint listed by a path, as `cs[0]` of a slice of closers (`variadic.CloseFirst`) and `resp.Body` of a `Response` of the project (`ifacestate.Done`), gets no separation; building it would bring GrewHeld back as an assumption about callers, as Grew is, never as a proof.
  - An entry cannot say that one method of a wrapper writes nothing of what it keeps (`io.NopCloser`'s `Close`), so `iowrap.ClosesApart` is refused.
  - A state of the home tied to a state in the struct (the second half of what real closers and transactions need, after `behind(r)`), and a method of an interface of the project that keeps nothing it is handed, which `io.Writer` would need of a writer of the project's own interface.
- The corpus is `footprints/`, each rule with a program that verifies and a defect a wrong version would let through: the growth layer keeping every allocation (`MadeKept`), regions read outside the epoch (`Peeked`), the frame check passing every write (`Unlisted`), the separation without its nil guard for maps (`NilMap`), a call on the right of `&&` treated as not run (`GuardedPeek`), the own row widened to every state (`StateLost`), a loop's footprint sites ignored (`LoopPeek`), a join dropping the epoch (`LiteralPeek`), and the range check dropped (`RangedPeek`). The nil guard for a pointer or a slice is not observable: a read at a nil pointer is reported first. `semantics.WriteChain` writes thirty times and reads through thirty epochs (152 problems, 1.3 M units).

### Buffers and builders

Built as F26 (2026-11-22 to 2026-11-23), the feature right after part 1 of footprints (decided by the orchestrator), in six reviewed steps; the design was agreed with the reviewer (F1 to F14 of the reviews) and the orchestrator (rulings D1 to D7). Verified code writes a `strings.Builder` and a `bytes.Buffer`, local, handed in, held by value in a struct of the project, or an element of a slice of them; `|b|` in a formula is the length `Len` returns; the writing and reading methods that grow or report the length ship, bounded by it; `fmt.Fprint`, `Fprintf`, and `Fprintln` write into a buffer or a `*bufio.Writer` through the writer's own `Write`; and two struct values of one type are known to be one or apart, which keeps one counter across a write through another and one `Pair` across a write of another.

Decisions:

- **The measure is `Len()`, read from the type's own fields** (D1, decided by the orchestrator). The shipped `owns` declaration gains `length`, a formula over the type's fields resolved as of the declared type in its own package (`strings.Builder`: `|buf|`; `bytes.Buffer`: `|buf| - off`), and `holds`, formulas every value satisfies (`0 <= off`, `off <= |buf|`), stated where the length is read under `ptr != nil` (`ownerLength` in `spec.go`, `addOwns`). The struct is fetched once and the formula takes its fields from that value, so the pointer enters each read once (F5). No ghost, no `cap(b)`: the capacity is refused with a message of its own, and `|x|` of anything without a length stays an error. `|x|` stays the one spelling of "how long": when `sort.Interface` and `heap.Interface` get an integer state (part 3), `|x|` of an interface value will read the state its interface declares as its length, a struct type getting its length from `owns`. The trust entries' primed-path check counts `|b'|` as reading the struct (`checkPrimed`, `lengthRead`).
- **The bounds need only `Len()`, also after `Reset` and after reads** (D2; derivation redone by the reviewer on Go 1.26.6 and 1.27.3, which have the same `grow`, `growSlice`, `tryGrowByReslice`, `Builder.grow`, `growslice`, `nextslicecap`, `bytealg_MakeNoZero`, and `roundupsize`). This corrects part 1's sentence (Footprints, Writing a `bytes.Buffer` or a `strings.Builder`) that a bound needs the capacity after a `Reset` that keeps a large array. A Builder's writes are one `append`, which moves only when the capacity is less than the new length, and then to less than twice it: `2 * (|b| + k) + 65536 <= MaxAlloc`. A Builder's `Grow(n)` allocates `2 * cap + n` when the free capacity is less than `n`, so `cap < |b| + n` and the allocation is below `3 * (|b| + n)`. A Buffer allocates only when what is written fits neither after the write end (`cap - off < Len + n`) nor, slid to the front, in half the capacity (`cap < 2 * (Len + n)`); then its `ErrTooLarge` check `2c + n > maxInt` is below `4 Len + 5n`, and growSlice allocates `max(len(buf) + n, 2(cap - off)) < 2 Len + 3n` once, rounded up to a size class or a page: the compiler makes its `append([]byte(nil), make([]byte, c)...)` one growslice (`testing.AllocsPerRun` of a `Grow(10000)` is 1, the reviewer's run). A large capacity kept by `Reset` is bounded by the slide condition, which is why the length alone suffices. The entries: Builder `2 * (|b| + k) + 65536` at most `MaxAlloc` and `MaxInt`, `Grow` `3 * (|b| + n) + 65536` at most both; Buffer `3 * (|b| + k) + 65536 <= MaxAlloc` and `5 * (|b| + k) <= MaxInt` (the simpler `3 * (|b| + k)` in place of `2|b| + 3k`, the weaker claim, chosen to read like an append). The message of an unproved bound says what is enough on the platform, as `it holds when |b| <= 10000000000000 and |s| <= 10000000000000` (`growthHint`, the largest power of ten at which the clause holds, evaluated by `groundHolds`).
- **The 32-bit edges are run, not only read** (F3, F7). `TestBuffers386` (with `VEGO_386=1`; `AGENTS.md` says when) builds `testdata/buffers386` for `GOARCH=386` and runs each case in a 4G scope: a Builder and a Buffer of capacity 720 MiB grown by one more byte, which panic; the worst Builder and Buffer inside the bounds, which return; and a Buffer read down to one byte whose growth panics at the `ErrTooLarge` check, which the Buffer's clause with 4 for 5 would allow. It found that the first Builder clause, `3 * (|b| + n) <= MaxInt`, was false: `MakeNoZero` rounds `2 * cap + n` up to a page past `MaxInt`, and `unsafe.Slice` panics. Hence the `+ 65536` in the MaxInt clauses of the Builder. `checkBufferEdges386`, a subtest of `TestShippedContracts`, evaluates every such clause and the append rule at the same cases with the 386 sizes by default, so a weakened clause fails `go test`.
- **Which methods ship** (D4). Builder `Write`, `WriteString`, `WriteByte`, `WriteRune`, `Grow`, `Len`, `String`, `Reset`; Buffer the same and `Bytes` (`|result| = |b| And within(result, b)`) and `Truncate` (`0 <= n <= |b|`). Not shipped: the reading methods (`Next` hands out a view a later write overwrites; `ReadBytes` and `ReadString` copy, and wait only for a reason to ship), `Cap`, `Available`, `AvailableBuffer` (the capacity is no part of the measure), `bytes.NewBuffer` and `NewBufferString` (they take over memory they are handed), and `ReadFrom` and `WriteTo` (dynamic types), with a message for `ReadFrom` that says what to write. Each shipped entry is tested by sequences over real buffers (`buffers_test.go`): `|b|` evaluated by the declared formula over the fields read by reflection and held against `Len()` and every `holds` after every call, reads by `Read` and `Next` included (F4); owner growth checked around each call; `Grow` below 0 and `Truncate` outside `[0, |b|]` must panic and fail the requires; `within(result, b)` of `Bytes` from the real addresses; and after a call that moved the array, the new capacity at most `L` for each requires clause `L <= MaxAlloc`, with writes past 256 KB and the worst Buffer (`worstBuffer`: a capacity above 128 KB read down to one byte and `C/2 + 1` bytes written) so that each factor of the entries, made one smaller, fails. The `+ 65536` makes this check blind below 64 KB, which the sizes chosen avoid.
- **`owns` is matched by the underlying struct** (F1). It was keyed by the named type, so `type MyB strings.Builder` escaped the copy rule, and with the Builder entries shipped, `var x MyB; (*strings.Builder)(&x).WriteString("a"); y := x; (*strings.Builder)(&y).WriteString("b")` would have been proved not to panic, which Go does. `ownsDeclOf` now finds a declaration through the package of the struct's fields, as `statesOf` does, for every caller: the copy rule, Modifies coverage, owned parts and sites, `fresh`, and `|x|` (`buffers.CopiesMyB`, `WritesMyB`, `CopiesHoldsMyB`, `HoldsNB`).
- **An owner held by value is owned by what holds it** (D7, decided by the orchestrator, for direct use). `inlineOwners` lists the fields held by value whose type owns memory or holds such a field, matched by the underlying struct, and ownedParts, ownedSites, ownedFreshSeen, and ownerGrowthSeen recurse into them at their offsets. So `Modifies p` covers what `p.out` owns, `|p.out|` reads the measure, and two such structs keep each other's lengths. A `Modifies` path that ends at an owner held by value, a field or an element (`p.out`, `bs[0]`), names what it owns too (F6); a slice listed whole does not cover what its elements own, and the message for an unlisted write names the owner's path (`list bs[0] in a // @Modifies clause, which covers what it owns; a slice listed whole covers its elements, not what they own`). A loop that writes such an owner through a pointer to a field, as `&p.out`, roots its site at the struct with the owner's offset and states at its head that what the owner owns grows only by what the loop creates (F14, `rootSite`). Converting a struct that holds an owner to an interface whose methods write behind their receiver stays refused (`ownsMemory` counts it), with a message of its own, as the orchestrator ruled: that is where rows defined at a conversion meet an owner that grows by direct calls, which ownership in source is to meet on purpose. Programs: `buffers/inline.go`, with the mutants of each recursion, of the offset, and of `ownsMemory`.
- **A buffer is not converted to `io.Writer`; it is formatted into through `prints`** (D3, decided by the orchestrator as a general rule). The contract of `(io.Writer).Write` has no bound for a buffer's `Write`, and a count of bytes on every writer would put a `Requires` on every function that writes through one. So the conversion stays refused, with both remedies in the message. The narrow way: an entry field `prints: {w: fmt.Sprintf}` claims that the function formats as the named function of its package does, calls `w.Write` once with exactly what that function returns, calls nothing else of `w`, and returns what `Write` returned; it ships on `fmt.Fprint`, `Fprintf`, and `Fprintln`, and `TestPrintsClaims` holds it against recording writers, taking the named function from the entry. Where the writer argument is a value of a concrete type whose conversion to `io.Writer` is refused and whose `Write` has a contract (`printedInto`), the call is verified as that Sprint function followed by the writer's own `Write` of a new slice of the bytes (`callPrinted`, `applyPrinted`); no interface value is made, so no row is defined at a conversion. That covers `*bytes.Buffer`, `*strings.Builder`, and `*bufio.Writer` with no rule of its own: the guide's `WriteCount` verifies. Values that convert today keep their path. The claim is narrowed (F11): an entry with `prints` may require no more than `w != nil`, list only `w`, and have no `ensures`, `keeps`, or `calls`, all of which the route would not read; a project's `plog.Checked`, requiring a short format, was verified around before (`buffers.ProjectRequires`, `ProjectModifies`). Where the formatting bound does not count everything, the text has no length bound; the writer's `Write` is called when its requires holds without one, as a `bufio.Writer`'s (F12, `ErrToBufio`), and otherwise the call is reported with what makes the length unknown. A deferred call takes the same route at each return (F13). A loop that formats into a writer this way takes its sites from the writer's own `Write` (the fix after the trial, d32bbee): it had taken them from `Fprintf`'s footprint of `w`, which refused writes that `Modifies` covered, as in the trial's `toDot`, though not unsoundly, since that footprint's epoch forgot every region.
- **Two struct values of one type are one value or lie apart** (D5 and F2; the general fact, measured before it shipped, decided by the orchestrator). Why it is true of every state Go makes without `unsafe`: a pointer to a struct points to a value of its type, a variable, a field at any depth, or an element of a slice; two values of one struct type never overlap partially, since one would have to hold the other and a struct type cannot hold itself; fields of one struct and elements of one array are disjoint; types that share regions (identical underlying structs, tags ignored) share the key, as a conversion between them needs; a struct of size zero has no identity and gets nothing; a nil pointer points to no value. It rests on the assumption that code which is not verified does not use `unsafe` to give memory two types, and needs no sentence of its own.
