Stated as a ground fact per pair, no new symbol (`statePairs` in `memory.go`): `x.ref = 0 Or x.ref != y.ref Or pa = pb Or pa + size <= pb Or pb + size <= pa` for each pair of structs of one key that the two hold. A read through a struct records its base (`fetchIn`); the fact is stated against the struct targets of call and loop layers (`target.base`), against the structs a function reads or writes that hold two leaves in one region, for stores, which no layer sees (`noteStruct`, `structsSeen`; only such types can mistake a cell for another region by region), and, for every type, against the structs a function converted (`convertedStructs`), whose footprint is a stretch blind to regions. A second fact keeps another region's cell out of a converted struct: a cell of region R lies in its stretch only at a leaf of the type in R (`leafOnly`), which keeps the slot `ts[1]` of a `[]*Counter` out of `ts[0]`'s Counter. The row keeps its byte-range definition, so `rowNewOrIn`, `within`, and `fresh(x, w)` read it as before. Programs (`distinct/`): `KeepsPairCall`, `KeepsPairInLoop`, `KeepsPairWrite`, `KeepsField`, `KeepsPairIface`, and `KeepsOtherCounter` (the orchestrator's probe) verify; `MayBeSamePair`, `MayBeSameIface`, `MayBeSameCounter`, `InnerOfPair`, `InnerOfOuter`, `TwinOfPair`, `SecondPoint`, and `AdjacentPairs` are refused. Mutants caught: no fact, the size plus one, the key check dropped, `leafOnly` saying never inside, `leafOnly` off, no record of stores. Not observable, and not tested: the fact for a size-zero struct (no cell to read) and the `ref = 0` guard (no read reaches allocation 0). Cost on the corpus: nothing measurable (409.8 M to 409.8 M units on the base rows, no row above 1.05 times).
- **Copies** (D6, ruled by the orchestrator on F38 of part 1). The sentence of "What a proof assumes" about `sync` is extended to a `strings.Builder` that is not zero, whose documentation forbids the copy; `TestBuilderCopyPanics` shows `copyCheck`'s panic. Verified code keeps the copy rule, now for named types and structs over owners too (F1).

Repairs, each of a fault of existing code that the feature ran into:

- **The capacity an append picks, on a 32-bit target** (step 1, soundness). growslice turns the rounded size into the capacity with `int(capmem / size)` and checks only `maxAlloc`, which is more than `MaxInt` on a 32-bit target. Appending 838374793 bytes of a full `[]byte` of 879605573 to itself gives a slice whose `cap` is -2147483648, and no panic (`TestBuffers386`). The rule `2 * grown * size + 65536 <= MaxAlloc` accepted it, so under `GOARCH=386` the program `@Requires |a| = 879605573 And cap(a) = |a|`, `@Ensures cap(r) >= |r|`, `r = append(a, a[:838374793]...)` verified at b4d7587 (the reviewer's run) and was false. The rule now also requires `2 * grown * size + 65536 <= size * MaxInt` (`appendFits`), implied on 64-bit targets, and its message says `or have a capacity an int cannot hold`. Every append obligation has one conjunct more, one problem more on those rows of the cost table.
- **The growth of what an owner owns is stated after the call's bound is known** (step 1). `ownerGrowth` was assumed before the call's `brk` advanced, while the type facts of what is read say every reference is below `brk`, so an owned slice that must move, as that of a zero Builder after its first write, could be nowhere: `var b strings.Builder; b.WriteString("x")` drew "contradicts what is known here". A contradiction that Z3 does not prove there would make the rest of the path vacuous, so it could have hidden a false proof; no program before this feature had an owned slice that had to move. With the order reverted, the corpus draws five such reports (the reviewer's run).
- **A receiver is named as the argument to list** (F9, F10). A write through what a method's contract places within its receiver, as `Bytes` within `b`, now says `list b`, and through a promoted method the path through the embedded fields, as `h.Buffer`.

Cost against b4d7587: base rows 394.5 M to 396 M units (SpliceChain +2.7 M from the growth stated after the bound, guide.WriteCount +1.4 M now that it verifies), every row past twice a program that verifies where it was refused (Greets, BuildsName, FormatToBuffer, FormatsIntoBuffer, WriteCount, FormatsError's try of the Write requires); new rows bring the total to about 407 M. `semantics.BufferLengthChain` writes fifteen times, not thirty as the rest of `chains.go`: a doubling rule would grow 2^15 times there, past the growth check, and thirty writes cost 11.2 M units against 3.0 M. Cold `go test ./vego/...` back to back with b4d7587 at the end of step 5: 132 s against 124 s at load 6.6 to 6.9.

Not built yet:

- **A write through a local that holds memory of no listable origin** says "list it in a // @Modifies clause" though a local cannot be listed, as `handlers`' reviewer found with `v := q["x"]; v[0] = "y"` over the map `Query` returns (F14 of the review of F28, step 3). The message is older than F28; it should say that the memory is not known to be new and nothing listed covers it, as F27 made the remedies of owners say.
- **The contents of a buffer.** The entries say how long a buffer is and nothing of what it holds; `b.String()` is a string of the right length.
- **`0 <= |b|` under a quantifier with no gatherer open.** `Forall i in [0, |bs|) : |bs[i]| >= 0` under `bs[i] != nil` is not proved (the reviewer's `Nonneg`): what `holds` gives is assumed where the length is read, and a read under a quantifier states it only into what gathers the quantifier's facts, which only a function that lists a footprint opens; with one open, the goal runs to the limit. Upper bounds, which users write, are proved.
- **A bound on what a `String` method returns, read from its contract, in formatting.** A value whose `String` ensures `|s| <= 30` still counts as text of unknown length, so formatting it into a buffer is refused.
- **`Modifies` of a slice of owners whole covering what its elements own.** It is a run of targets, as `Runs of elements in Modifies` under Structs and pointers; today each element is listed.
- **Converting an owner, or a struct that holds one by value, to an interface whose methods write behind their receiver**: the constraint on rows defined at a conversion (Footprints) binds it, and ownership in source is where it is to be built.
- **A `*bytes.Buffer` handed to a verified function that takes an `io.Writer`, `log.New(&b, ...)`, and `bufio.NewWriter(&b)`**, which need the conversion.
- **Bounded contradiction checks after buffer calls stop at their limit more often with each call**: `buffers.Two` has 18 of its 40 problems open, and `BufferLengthChain` 19 of 148 at fifteen writes (49 of 298 at thirty). When one stops it reports nothing, which is the quiet side; the fault of the growth order above was found by such a check.
- **The trial**: `toDot` stops only on bounds its contract does not state (`|w|`, the node names, and the number of edges); hex's `Dump` converts a Builder to the `io.Writer` its Dumper keeps, and the Dumper holds arrays (item 5).

### Ownership in source (footprints, second part)

Being built as F27, item 1 of the list at the top, in reviewed steps; the design was agreed with the reviewer (F1 to F16 of its review) and the orchestrator, who ruled on D2 that ownership is an invariant kept by refusing what would break it, not a taint. `// @Owns items, m` on a struct type of the project says that a value of the type owns the memory those fields refer to. The design's four claims: C1, `Modifies s` covers what s owns; C2, the growth rule G below; C3, a function that lists an owner assumes that nothing else it is given reaches what the owner owns, which follows from the invariant HI (every reference to a part that is stored in memory lies in the owned cell that holds it, and no two owned fields overlap) kept by the rules R1 to R6 of step 3; C4, the invariant of a struct reached through an owned pointer field, with `valid(x)` (step 2).

Built in step 1:

- **The declaration** (`owns.go`). On a struct type at package level without type parameters, listing fields that are unexported and hold a pointer to a struct, a slice, a map, or (since step 3b) an interface value. Unexported, so that only the declaring package stores into an owned field and every identical struct type lies in it (D1, decided by the orchestrator). Refused where it is declared, each with a message that says what to do: an exported field, a field of another kind, one that is absent or named twice, a type with type parameters, and a type that owns itself through owned pointer fields and owners held by value (`Node` with `@Owns next`, and a cycle through two types; a chain cannot leave the package and come back, since imports are acyclic). An `Owns` anywhere but on a struct type is reported.
- **Where a declaration is found.** What a type owns is needed wherever a type is looked at, the copy rule included, which has no package at hand, so the declarations are kept in a registry of the process, keyed by the type object: a package registers its own when it has collected them, and those of its imports from their facts (`ownsFact`) when it starts collecting. It is matched by the underlying struct, tags ignored, through the package of the struct's unexported fields, as shipped declarations are (F1 of F26). `ownedFieldsOf` reads it after the shipped declarations, so every piece of the machinery of shipped owners (`ownedParts`, `ownedSites`, `ownedFreshSeen`, `ownerGrowth`, `inlineOwners`, `ownsMemory`, the copy rule) covers owners of the project with no change of its own: `Modifies s` covers the array of an owned slice to its capacity, an owned map, and the struct an owned pointer points to with what it owns; a call forgets them; a loop forgets them by region; after a verified call what each owned field refers to is what it referred to, within the same stretch, or new.
- **G, checked at every store into an owned field** (`checkOwnedStore`), an assignment or a field of a composite literal, wherever the struct lies. The value must refer to nothing (a slice without capacity, a nil map or pointer); or lie within what the field refers to just before the store (the same array inside its stretch, the same map, the same pointer), unless it comes from a call that may keep what it is handed; or have been allocated since the statement began, when the expression allocates where it stands (`make`, `new`, a composite literal or its address, `append`, a conversion of a string to a slice) or comes from a trusted function whose entry lists no `keeps` and whose `modifies` can hold no reference. The identity of the owner is never compared: F1 of the design review showed two owners with one root (`ss[0].items = ss[1].items`, `t.p.items = t.q.items`), and Z3 decides the case. A call's result takes the second case only from a callee that keeps nothing (F11): the shipped `slices.Delete`, `Insert`, `Grow`, `Clip`, and `DeleteFunc` stored back into their field verify, by that rule and not by their names. Why G gives the growth that callers assume after a verified call (`ownerGrowth`): by induction over the function's stores and calls, every owned field holds nothing, memory made since the function was entered, or memory within what it held on entry. "Within what it held on entry" was considered as a fourth case and left out: the head of a loop states growth from the loop's entry, which a store of what the field held on entry of the function would break.
- **What was not supported in step 1, with messages that named the missing case:** a store of new memory held in a variable (`m := make(...); fill(m); c.m = m`), and of the result of a verified function, both of which step 3a built (R1 and R4); and converting an owner of the project to an interface whose methods write behind their receiver (step 4).
- **A refused store is verified past as if it were allowed**, as after any check, so that what it would bring in draws no second report (F18 of the review of step 1: `LocalValue`). The messages say what works today by the field's kind: `make(...)` or `&Config{...}` where it is stored, or a copy, as `slices.Clone` or `maps.Clone` makes.
- **The registry is copied on write** (F19): a speculative verifier collects the package again while its own verifier reads the registry.
- **Loops.** A store into an owned field through a variable the loop does not assign, or through owned pointer fields read from one (`t.p.items`), makes the head of the loop state that what that owner owns grows only by what the loop creates, as a call that writes an owner does (`effects.owners`); without it the frame check of the next iteration's write fails.
- **Calls that cannot be followed.** A call of a project's trusted function, a function value, or an interface method that may write an owner of the project, or that returns something that holds one, is `cannot verify`, since nothing says what such an owner owns.
- **Shipped entries.** `bytes.TrimFunc`, `TrimLeftFunc`, and `TrimRightFunc` say `within(result, s)`, as their documentation does, which the harness tests from the real addresses (F11 of the review).
- Programs: `owns/` and `ownslib/` (a Bag of another package used through its fact). Wrong versions caught: G off; the within case allowed to every call; the allocation case bounded by the entry and not the statement (`AppendOther`); every expression taken to allocate (`ThroughVerified`); the within case without the same array, or for a pointer without the same allocation (`TwoNew`, `TwoNewServers`, F17 of the review of step 1); the self-ownership check off; the check of what a trusted call returns off; and the registry not read. The loop's owner facts off breaks `Fill` and `FillInner`, which verify. Not observable: the stretch of a slice in the within case, since no expression gives a slice of the field's array that starts before it.
- Cost: no row of the base moved but the three over `bytes.TrimFunc`, whose new clause adds a few hundred units each.

Built in step 5, with the guide and the trial:

- **A new owner is placed for the proof at a call** (F40 of the review of step 5, decided by the orchestrator). An owner that the function made, or got from a call that returns it new and keeps nothing of it, held in a local that is never assigned again, and whose memory has not escaped where the call stands, is placed for part 1's proof at a call of the row of an owned interface value (`placedOwner`): by HI, what it owns is reached only through it. Each other argument must then be no part of an owner (`insideOperand`), and part 1's rules for the other arguments still apply. `fresh` of an owner of the project covers the states of the home of its owned interface value, which G lets be only new. A remedy no longer tells a user to list a local. `UseLocal`, `UseNew`, and the guide's `Jot` verify; `UseEscaped` (stored first) and `UseHandedKeeping` (handed to a function that keeps it) are refused with where it went, `UseInsideRow` (the owner's writer handed beside it) is refused, and `UseGiven` (a writer it was handed) stays refused by G. Wrong versions caught: escape ignored (`UseEscaped`, `UseHandedKeeping`), the other arguments not checked (`UseInsideRow`), and `fresh` without the states of a new home (`UseNew` and `Jot` refused, the completeness direction).
- The guide's example of the invariant of what a struct owns has a store of a second package, `guidestore` (F41).

Built in step 4:

- **Converting an owner** (`structuralFootprint`, `ownerFootprintAt`, `valueFootprintAt`). A pointer to an owner of the project, or to a struct of the project that holds a shipped owner by value (D7's refusal is lifted), converts to an interface whose methods write behind their receiver. The row defined at the conversion is the struct together with what it owns then: the stretch of an owned slice, an owned map, an owned struct and what it owns, and the row of an owned interface value with the states of what it holds. These are read in the memory of the conversion, as raw terms of each leaf's region, since a row is asked about inside the evaluation of an expression, where a recorded read would panic. A shipped owner itself is still refused, as before, by the bufio types' wrapping and the buffers' unbounded growth. An owner whose type has an invariant, C4's included, still fails its method check, since part 4 of footprints is not built. The method check (`placeFootprint`) places the receiver's struct and what it owns at the method's entry in the receiver's row. That is true at every call: by the definition, by the follow layers below, and because rows keep old cells.
- **The row follows a store** (`followStore`; `layer.follows`, read in `rowAt`). This is the constraint on ownership in source recorded under Footprints. Every store into an owned field in memory leaves a layer of the footprint region, not only one after the function converted an owner: a callee may convert an owner and hand back both, or store the conversion where the caller reaches it (F36 of the review of step 4, `StaleFromCallee`; a layer only costs where a row is read). The layer says: a row that holds the field's cell also holds the stored value's footprint, and every row keeps its cells. A call that grows an owner leaves a growth layer, as before, and so does a call that runs a function literal storing into an owned field (F3). A loop's head leaves one in any case, and the ends of an `if` meet in the join of the footprint region. Why it is sound: let R* be the footprint at the conversion together with everything added to it since. R* satisfies every fact the function has about the row (the definition, the follow layers, the growth layers), and every method's writes lie in R*. The caller proves its goal under every row the facts allow, and R* is one of them. The invariant of rows is kept, since the new layer only adds cells.
- **Growth across a call through a converted owner.** A call that writes a footprint states `ownerGrowth` of every owner the function converted, from before the call, because only verified code, and the shipped owners' methods, write an owner of the project. Without it, a direct call between two calls through the value is refused as writing what the owner owns (`GrowBetween`).
- **Refused:** an owner stored into its own owned field, or into one of what it owns. Converted there, its row would be its own struct, against C3 (`IntoItself`, the reviewer's program).
- **F4.** R6 reads the operand's own type, so `useTally(t, t.items)` with `t` converted at the call is refused (`TallyBeside`). Part 1's fourth case for a converted pointer, at the call or in a variable declared from the conversion, also counts the types of what the owner owns (F37 of the review of step 4, `ConvertedBeforeLocal`, whose interface value R6 cannot see through).
- Not built: converting an owner that owns an interface value through an owned pointer (`ConvNested`, F39): the method check does not place that value's row in the receiver's row, so it is refused at the conversion with a message that says so; one owned directly converts (`ConvDirect`). Also not built: converting an owner that owns an interface value to an interface whose method is handed memory, such as `io.Writer`. The implementation's own contract relies on C3 for the owned interface value, and part 1's method check cannot show that of a call it makes up (`madeAs`, F6 of part 1). An owner that owns only slices, maps, and pointers converts to such an interface.
- Programs (`owns/convert.go`): a `memStore` that owns its map, converted to a `Store` with `Put` (`PutThrough`, `KeptBeside`), and refused facts about a map it owned at the conversion (`StaleAtConversion`), a map stored after it (`StaleAfterStore`, the part 1 reviewer's program, read through a local), a map a direct call made (`StaleAfterCall`), a map stored in a loop (`StaleInLoop`), and a map stored on each branch (`StaleInBranch`). Also a cache (`CacheThrough`), and a `Page` holding a `strings.Builder`, converted and grown by a direct call between calls through the value (`GrowBetween`). `buffers.ConvertsPrinter` now verifies.
- Wrong versions caught: the follow layer off (`StaleAfterStore`, `StaleInBranch`; `StaleInLoop` is refused by the loop's growth layer with or without it), and gated on the function's own conversions (`StaleFromCallee`); the parts dropped from the definition at the conversion alone (`StaleAtConversionLocal`, F38, which reads the map into a local before the conversion; dropped from the method check too, every conversion of an owner is refused); the parts of the owner left out of the fourth case (`ConvertedBeforeLocal`, `TallyBeside`); the growth across a footprint call off, in the completeness direction (`GrowBetween`); and the self-ownership refusal off (`IntoItself`).
- Cost: no row of the base moved but `buffers.ConvertsPrinter`, which verifies now where it was refused at the conversion (2 problems and 1,987 units, to 11 and 43,674).

Built in step 3b:

- **C3 in the logic** (`separatePart`, `partApartFromParams`, `ownedCells` in `owns.go`; `separation` in `footprint.go`). A declared verified function that lists an owner of the project assumes, for each part of it (`target.sourcePart`, set by `ownedParts` for a part an owned field of a declaration in source gives, at any depth), that no cell of the part is reached by the other parameters, by the owner's own struct, or by the owner's other parts, closed under what memory held on entry, except through the owned cells of the owner's struct and of the structs it owns (the cut). It is part 1's separation per part, with three additions: the owner parameter and the other parts as roots beside the other parameters, the cut in `noteReached`, and, for a part that is no row, a test of its cells on entry in place of a row. It is stated where a layer whose targets hold such a part is read, as part 1's is where a footprint layer is read, and, for what a store written directly needs, as ground facts on entry: a slice parameter of the part's element type is apart from an owned slice, a map parameter of the type from an owned map, and a pointer parameter from an owned struct of its type. Why it is true: HI says that every reference to a part stored in memory lies in the cell that holds it and that no two parts overlap; E says that no parameter's value is a part of an owner that a parameter reaches. The cut is what lets two parameters point to one owner. Without it the assumption contradicts itself wherever a part is read through a layer (`CutNeeded`, whose false postcondition then verifies).
- **E and R6** (`checkOwnArguments`). At every call of a verified function, an argument that is inside an owner, or of no known origin, is refused when an owner whose parts can hold it is reached by any argument, itself included (F2): its type is an owned field's type or a pointer to an owned slice's elements, or a struct value holds such a field. Outside values, new memory, and the caller's parameters are no part, by HI and the caller's own E. `Helped` (`helper(s.items, 3)`) verifies; `Beside` (`helper2(s.items, s)`) and `SelfOwned` (a tree's children handed alone to a function whose parameter reaches the tree) are refused. Since any owner may be the argument, `ss[i].PushAll(xs)` needs nothing placed.
- **Owned interface values** (`Report`). A field of an interface type may be owned. `Modifies r` covers the footprint of `r.out` and the states of what it holds, whatever its interface declares, for an owner of the project. A shipped owner keeps part 1's parts. The separation of `r.out`'s row from `r`'s own struct is C3's owner root, which is what keeps `r.lines` across `r.out.Write(p)`. The proof at a call keeps part 1's rule for rows: the owner argument must be placed, as a listed parameter of the caller is (`KeepsBeside`). G for such a field: nothing, the same value, or a new interface value whose footprint is new: a conversion at the store, as `&Report{out: os.Stdout}`, or one a trusted call that keeps nothing returns. `ownerGrowth` after a call says the same of an owner of the project, nothing more: the same value, nil, or a new one with a new footprint (`Reopened`, whose false `r.out' = r.out` the claim of the shipped owners would prove). A writer the struct is handed is refused (`Adopt`), as F9 (d) says.
- **Repairs after the review of step 3b.** The address Go takes for a pointer method on an addressable operand, in a call, a deferred call, or a method value, has the kind of `&x`, as the address written out has (F35: `TouchFirst`, `KeepFirst`, `KeepValue`; reading the receiver as the element lets all three through). The address of a variable that lives in memory is outside: R1 never lets one into an owned field. An owned interface field takes a value converted from new memory nothing else refers to, where it is stored or into a variable first, as `w := io.Writer(&sink{}); return &Report{out: w}` (F34: `HeldWriter`, `HeldPointer`); for such a field the test of memory that existed on entry asks of the value's home and row, not of its reference.
- Wrong versions caught: the cut off (`CutNeeded` and the handlers of `ownserver`), R6 off (`Beside`, `SelfOwned`), the shipped owners' growth for an owned interface value of the project (`Reopened`); and in the completeness direction, the ground facts off (`CopyFirst`, `Keeps`, `helper2`) and the owner's struct left out of the roots (`Report.Line`).
- Cost: no row of the base moved.

Built in step 3a:

- **HI, the invariant the rest rests on** (`ownorigins.go`). Every reference to memory an owner owns (a part) that is stored in memory lies in the owned cell that holds it, and no two owned fields refer to overlapping memory. Locals may hold references to a part while a call runs. Every verified function keeps it by refusing, where it stands, what would break it; only code that is not verified is trusted with it (A1, in step 5's guide). C3 (step 3b) and C4's assumption about callers that are not verified rest on it.
- **The analysis.** A fixed point over the control-flow graph, as part 1's origins are, gives each local and parameter a kind: the parameters whose memory it may be, reached other than through an owned field; tags, memory the function made at an allocation site or got as new and not escaped from a call; inside a root (a parameter, a tag, or an owner read from memory), memory an owner owns; or unknown. A read through an owned field is inside; slicing, `&`, and a field selection of an inside value stay inside; a read of a cell that is not owned (an element, a map value, a field that is not owned, what a pointer points to) is outside, since by HI no such cell holds a reference to a part (F6). Each tag has a status, new, owned by a root, or escaped. A site has two tags, its most recent execution and every earlier one: running it again moves what held the recent tag to the earlier one, which takes the worse status of the two (F7 of the review of the design). A call is one tag for every result it returns as new (F20). The rules, each refused where it stands with a message that says what to do:
  - R1: a store into an owned field may take new memory that was not made in its own statement only when the value is made of tags that are all new (`ownFlow.made`, read by `checkOwnedStore`, which then proves the value allocated since the function was entered); its tags are owned by the owner from then on. This replaces step 1's interim refusal of a variable and step 2's interim acceptance of a verified callee's `fresh` result: `Held`, `Filled`, `Constructed`, and `Trimmed` verify; `ThroughKeeping` and `FromKeeping` (a callee that kept what it returns) and `Shares` (two results of one call) are refused.
  - R2: an inside value is never stored into memory but by R1's store back into its own cell, never captured by a function literal that may outlive the call, and handed to a callee only for a parameter the callee keeps nothing of. `StoreElsewhere`, `Alias` (F21 of the review of step 2), `AddrOfPart`, `Captured`, `LinkOwned` (a method that stores its receiver in its own field, F5), and `ToSink` (a method of an interface declared in source, which may keep what it is handed) are refused; `Sorted` (`sort.Ints`, `slices.Reverse`) and `InPlace` (a literal called where it stands, judged where it runs, F9 (c)) verify.
  - R3: a tag stored into memory, captured by such a literal, or handed to a parameter that is kept, has escaped.
  - R4, keeping (F5, F13): a function keeps a parameter when it stores, captures in a literal that may outlive the call, or hands to a kept parameter a value that refers to memory of the parameter: the parameter, a slice of it, an address in it, or an owned field read through it. A value read from its unowned cells does not count (`Remembered`), and neither does R1's store back into its own cell. Returning such a value is a flow, recorded per result. Summaries (`ownSummary`) are the greatest fixed point over the package, from "keeps nothing, returns new memory", as part 1's are, and travel in the contract fact (`OwnKeeps`, `OwnResults`). A trusted function keeps only what its entry's `keeps` lists, which "What a proof assumes" says already; a function value, a method of an interface declared in source, and a callee without a contract keep everything. A trusted result its entry places with neither `fresh` nor `within` may be any argument's memory (F14, `Peeked`), and a trusted function handed an inside value is refused where what it returns can hold, below its top level, a reference into that value's memory (F15, `Fields` with `bytes.FieldsFunc`).
  - R5: a function may return what an owner it is handed owns only when its contract says so with `within(r, s)` (`Items`; `ItemsUnsaid` is refused), and what a local owner owns only while that owner's own tag is still new, no other result holds it or what it owns, and no deferred literal runs (F9 (b) with F12's conditions: `Built` verifies, `Leak` and `Both` are refused).
- **Entries of interface methods keep only what `keeps` lists** (the orchestrator's condition on R4). That was a weaker claim before, when part 1 let an implementation keep a pointer it never wrote through. Every such entry was audited: the shipped `(io.Writer).Write` and `(io.Reader).Read` take `p`, which both interfaces document an implementation must not retain; `(io.Closer).Close`, `(context.Context).Err`, and `context.CancelFunc` take nothing; of the corpus's entries only `(foreignlib.Filler).Fill` and `(foreignlib.Copier).Copy` take memory, and their reasons now say they keep nothing (the corpus's one implementation, `foreign.scribble.Copy`, has a value receiver); the corpus's `(io.Reader).Read` covers `*os.File`, `*bytes.Reader`, and `*bufio.Reader`, which io.Reader's rule binds; the trial has none. A verified implementation is checked where it is converted, from its summary (`madeAs`): `(*Remembers).Write`, which keeps `p`, is refused as an `io.Writer`. The claim is in the table of entry fields and in "What an entry for a type claims".
- Wrong versions caught: R2's refusal of a store off (five programs), the summary's keeps ignored (`ThroughKeeping`, `LinkOwned`), a tag for each result of a call (`Shares`), an unplaced trusted result counted outside (`Peeked`), F15 off (`Fields`), R5 off (`ItemsUnsaid`, `Leak`), F12 off (`Both`), the capture by a literal ignored (`Captured`), the address of an element counted outside (`AddrOfPart`), and an element read counted inside, the completeness direction, which refuses `Remembered`, `Share`'s message, and the getters of a slice. The demotion that resets the earlier tag to new is caught by `RefillPrev` only through its message, which names the wrong owner, since the store the fixed point sees on the back edge owns the tag too; `RefillKept`, the reviewer's program, cannot tell the two apart, since the path-insensitive join puts the recent tag in `keep` at the loop's exit.
- **Repairs after the review of step 3a.** A field of a struct literal that is not owned is a store into what `&T{...}` makes, and part of the value of `T{...}`, as the elements of slice and map literals were (F29: `AliasLiteral`, `AliasValue`, `AliasHeld`, `AliasItems`; the struct case dropped is caught by all four). The results a return hands back are judged as its deferred literals leave them, which may assign them (F30: `DeferredResult`, `AliasDeferred`; caught when the deferred literals are left out). `PrevKept` (F31, the reviewer's program) tells the demotion that resets the earlier tag to new from the right one by its verdict. `ownstore.ByTag` ranges over the owned map and over each task's tags, as the trial's `GetTasksByTag` does (F32). A summary of a body with no owner fell back on part 1's bits, which would have been unsound had it been reached, since part 1's stores bit allows storing what the arguments reach; it was unreachable, since a summary runs the analysis on every body, and is gone.
- Cost: no row of the base moved.

Built in step 2:

- **The invariant of what an owner owns** (C4; `invariantSteps` in `invariants.go`). The invariants of a value at a boundary are those of its type and, through each owned pointer field and each owner held by value, those of the struct reached there, at any depth, each on condition that every pointer on the way is not nil; a type that owns itself is refused where it is declared, so the walk ends. Each clause is resolved in its type's package with the constants its fact carries, as the invariants of a parameter of another package's type always were, so package main assumes and proves the invariant of `*ownstore.Store` at `s.store` without naming `tasks`. They are assumed and proved exactly where invariants are: assumed on entry and after a verified call for what it handed back, proved on return for pointer parameters and results and at each call for each argument; between those points they may be broken. A message about one of them says where it lies: `invariant of Store may not hold for s on return, in what it owns at store: tasks != nil`. This reopens "Only at boundaries" under Struct invariants for what an owner owns, and only for that. Until step 3a, what an unverified caller is assumed to pass rests on HI, which only 3a keeps: verified code can still store a reference to what an owner owns where it outlives the call (`h.st = s.store`), and then hand back a server whose store another verified function breaks through that reference, so that the invariant a third verified function assumes is false (F21 of the review of step 2, `Alias`); 3a's R2 refuses that store. In a struct value the walk takes the field's leaves: boundaries never meet one, since the copy rule refuses a struct that holds an owner by value, but the check that a type's invariants do not contradict each other asks them of a value of the type (`Holds`, `Fine`; F22 and F27 of the review of step 2, where making that case a panic crashed every such type).
- **`valid(x)`** (`specEnv.valid`; F10 of the design review, decided by the orchestrator). One reserved word; what a boundary assumes of `x`, built from the same steps, so a loop invariant, a `Requires`, an `Ensures`, or an `Assert` can say that the invariant of a value holds without naming its fields, as `// @Invariant valid(s)` at the head of a loop that calls `s.store.Create` (`ownserver.Count`). Primed anywhere, as `valid(s')` or `valid(s.store')`, it reads the struct the path leads to on return, as a state of what a pointer points to is read; `valid(old(s))` reads it as it was on entry, inside a body (F24). Refused, each where it is written: of a type with no invariant, its own or through what it owns; of a type whose invariants cannot be used, read neither as True nor as False; in the invariant of a type, which carries the invariants of what it owns already; and in a trust entry. A Go name `valid`, as a result `valid bool`, stays a name: only the form with a call is the word.
- **A struct type identical to an owner owns what it owns.** The declaration is matched by the underlying struct, so a second type of the package with the same fields, as `type Plain struct{ store *ownstore.Store }` beside `Server`, owns `store`; a conversion between the two is allowed, so it must. The programs give `Plain` a field more. A message about such a type says why it owns and what to do (F25): `List owns items because it has the fields of Stack, which declares // @Owns items; give List a field of its own, or use Stack`.
- **Loops.** A call that writes an owner read through an owned pointer field of a variable the loop does not assign, as `s.store.Create(...)`, makes the head state the growth of that variable's owner (`ownedPathRoot`), as stores did in step 1.
- **Until step 3a, the result of a verified call whose contract says `fresh(result)` could be stored into an owned field**, as a constructor writes it, `&Server{store: ownstore.New()}`; step 3a replaced it with R1's summary that the result has not escaped.
- **What a store of a new pointer needs** (a repair of step 1). The case of memory allocated since the statement no longer reads what the stored struct owns: inside an expression, as in a composite literal, the read was not recorded in the evaluation unit and the run panicked. It is not needed: every store into an owned field of a struct made since a bound stores nothing, part of what the field held, or memory made since its own statement began, so what such a struct owns was made since the bound too.
- Programs: `ownstore/` (a task store of the trial's shape, with `Reset`, a helper handed the store under `valid(st)` and ensuring `valid(st')`) and `ownserver/` (handlers that name no field of the store; a loop with and without `valid(s)`; a server that replaces its store with one whose map is nil, on return and in a loop; `valid` of a primed path, of a type without an invariant, of a broken type, in a type's invariant, in a trust entry, and a result named `valid`). Wrong versions caught: no recursion (which breaks the handlers), the recursion assumed and not proved (`Replace`, `CountForgets`), `valid` read as True (`ReplaceInLoop`), an owner held by value read at its holder's offset (`Box.Break`, F22), and the nil guard only at the top (`Lazy.Drop`, F23).

Where a shipped owner is treated differently from an owner of the project (F8 of the design review, as one list, decided by the orchestrator): HI, C3, R1 to R6, and G apply to what `@Owns` declares in source, and what a shipped owner owns keeps part 1's rules wherever it lies, in a local, behind an owned pointer, or held by value in a struct of the project. No proof assumes a shipped owner's slices apart from anything, and verified code can neither store into nor read its owned fields, which are unexported in another module; the only part of a shipped owner that is separated is its owned interface value, from the other parameters. After a call, an owned interface value of a shipped owner is the same value (its `owns` claim), where one of the project may be nil or a new value with a new footprint. `Modifies` of a shipped owner covers the states of what its owned interface value holds only when the interface declares states; for an owner of the project always. A shipped owner itself is not converted to an interface whose methods write behind their receiver, while an owner of the project and a struct of the project that holds a shipped owner by value are.

Not built yet:

- **Owning what one is given**, as `NewReport(w)` or `NewServer(cfg)`, a transfer in which the caller gives up `w` (decided by the orchestrator: listed first). It is the constructor most users write first, as the reviewer pointed out. The message says to make the value inside or copy it, or not to declare the field owned.
- **An exported owned field**, as a struct with a `[]T` that JSON fills: an owned field must be unexported (D1), so that only its package stores into it.
- **Moves of owned memory** between fields or owners (`s.items, t.items = t.items, s.items`, `t := s.items; s.items = nil; u.items = t`), which keep HI but break G per field and R1; they need growth stated per owner and a rule for a statement that overwrites every source.
- **What the elements of an owned slice and the values of an owned map point to or own** (D4): `@Owns items` with `items []*Node` owns the array of pointers. It is runs of elements in `Modifies` (Structs and pointers, Not built yet), and what digraph's map of maps waits for. Left out of F30 (decided by the orchestrator, D-a): a frame claims no more than the layers give, so it did not need it, and the trial's getters hand out the tag arrays of the tasks they return (`GetTask`'s `Task`, `GetAllTasks`'s copies of the headers), so declaring them owned would be false there. What it costs (F7 of the review): an invariant that reads the slices held in the values of an owned map, or the elements of an owned slice, is not kept across a write into a slice of the same element type that the function was handed, as a `[]string` beside a store whose tasks hold `[]string` tags, since nothing says that slice is not one of the tags; the clause is proved again. Owning the tags would need the store to copy them on the way out (`slices.Clone`).
- **A method of an interface of the project that keeps nothing it is handed**: it has no way to say so, so it is taken to keep everything, and an inside value handed to one is refused.
- **Converting an owner that owns an interface value through an owned pointer**, or one that owns an interface value to an interface whose method is handed memory (step 4).
- **The invariant of a struct reached through a pointer field that is not owned, or in a slice or a map**, which is not covered (Struct invariants).
- **Returning what a local owner owns while something else refers to it, and a struct value that holds what an owner owns**, which only a copy can return.
- **Owners of generic types**, refused where declared, as invariants are.
- **A struct passed or returned by value that would own** (D3), as the memory server's `KnowledgeGraph`, which `loadGraph` returns by value: an owner is not copied, so a parameter, a result, or a receiver of an owner type by value is refused with the copy rule's message, which says to use a pointer.

### Variadic calls and formatting

Built on 2026-11-20 (F13): calls of variadic functions, verified and trusted, with the arguments listed or a slice passed with `...`, also deferred, through function values, through interface values, of literals in place, and handed to trusted functions under `calls`; contracts on variadic function types and methods of interfaces, and values made of variadic functions, methods, and literals; `append(s, t...)` and `append(b, str...)`; a trust-file field `formats` and one rule for what may be formatted, which `fmt.Errorf` shares; values whose `String` or `Error` method has a contract; a bound on what formatting builds; and shipped entries for `fmt.Sprintf`, `Sprint`, `Sprintln`, `Printf`, `Print`, `Println`, `Fprintf`, `Fprint`, `Fprintln`, `log.Printf`, `Print`, `Println`, the same three methods of `*log.Logger`, `path.Join`, and `path/filepath.Join`.

Decisions:

- **One binder of arguments to parameters** (`bindArgs`, `variadic.go`). Every place that paired `call.Args` with parameters by index goes through it: a plain argument, a spread, or a pack of the listed arguments of the variadic parameter. A pack has no expression of its own, so nothing roots a write at its first element. `g(f())` is still refused, as one argument of tuple type.
- **A pack is a new slice, or nil.** Its arguments are operands of the call, converted to the element type where Go converts them (a conversion site, listed by `callConversions` and confirmed by go/ssa as for `fmt.Errorf` before), and `allocSlice` makes the array after them, with length and capacity their number; no arguments give the zero slice, as the specification says. So a pack never overlaps another argument and a callee may write it without the caller's leave, but what its elements point to is the caller's: `Modifies ps[0]` and `Modifies cs[0]` reach the caller's struct and the states of the caller's interface value through the pack (`variadic.SetThrough`, `CloseThrough`). A loop's site for a pack has no root; a root at an element could not keep anything false either, since the direct target is the new array (F6 of the review).
- **A spread is the slice itself**, so the rules for slice parameters that share memory apply unchanged, and the message for a write names the slice (F10).
- **`append(s, t...)` copies as memmove does.** In place, the stretch from the end of `s` of `|t|` elements is checked against the frame, and each region of the element type gets a new row that equals the old one outside the stretch and t's elements in the memory before the write inside it; moving, the same for `t` after the copy of `s`. The read of t's elements is a recorded read, as the copy of `s` is, with a ceiling just before the append's own write in place, so it is not compared with that write. `append(s[:i], s[i+1:]...)`, `append(s, s...)`, and `append(s[:k], s...)` are proved; an element-by-element copy from the front is rejected (`variadic/append.go`).
- **`formats` is a claim in the trust file, not a list in the verifier** (O1, decided by the orchestrator). It names one parameter of type `any` or `...any` whose values the function formats, and at most one string, its format. The claim: the function formats the values as package fmt does and does nothing else with them, runs only the methods fmt runs, while the call lasts and on the calling goroutine, keeps none, and builds from them at most 4096 bytes more than fmt prints, apart from what a logger adds from its own settings. Instead of `runs`, each value is checked at each call. The check is made in the state of the call, so the claim also says that the function formats every value before it writes anything its `modifies` lists or runs any code its `runs` lists (since D1, decided by the orchestrator: `Fprint*` list `w`, and the Logger runs its writer; `TestFormatsBeforeWriting` holds it of every shipped entry that lists either, and fails on a function that writes first). `formats` with `calls` is a defect of the entry (F2), since a function it is handed may run first, and so is `formats` in a type entry. A function with `formats` made into a value is refused, since what a value is called with cannot be checked (F12). A project's own logging function is such an entry; the guide shows it.
- **What may be formatted** (`plainReason`, `formatMethod`): a type whose own method set has none of Format, GoString, Error, String and that is a basic type, a slice of plain, or a struct of plain fields; a pointer to a plain struct; an error of known origin; and a value whose Error (if it is an error) or String method has a usable contract with an empty Modifies, whose type has no Format or GoString and holds something plain (a pointer to a struct too, with the method from `*T`'s set). A type that holds itself, as `type Nest []Nest`, is not plain: such a slice can hold itself, and fmt would follow it until the stack runs out (the walk of the first version stopped at a type it had seen, which `wrapped.FormatsNested` caught). Only the value's own method set counts, since fmt reaches what it formats through an interface, where nothing is addressable. A slice passed with `...` to a `formats` parameter is refused. `TestPlainFormatting` runs every shipped `formats` function on all these shapes under every verb, index, and count of operands, and fails if fmt calls a method of a plain value, or String on an error.
- **A String or Error method is a call of the function that formats** (built with the orchestrator's agreement to step 4). Its Requires and the receiver's invariant are proved for the value as passed, in the state where the call runs: at the call, or at each return for a deferred call (F3), from values kept at the defer. fmt and the method write nothing a caller can see, so that is the state of every call fmt makes of it. A value receiver reached through a pointer needs the pointer proved not nil; a pointer receiver gets the pointer, nil included, and its Requires decides (F8). The call is an edge in the analyzer's callees (recursion through the Variant) and in `findValueCallers` and the walk of a literal used as a value (stratification), from `formattedMethods`, which reads the entry's `formats` without building the contract, since it runs for every package. In `fmt.Errorf` a value whose type is an error but not `error` is refused, since `%w` would put it in the tree of the made error, whose methods would no longer be the standard library's (F1).
- **`fmt.Errorf` no longer counts as a call through a value** (O2, decided by the orchestrator, after the reviewer checked fmt). Formatting runs nothing for a plain value, the standard library's or vouched methods for an error of known origin, which return and write nothing, and String and Error methods only through the edges above. `foreign.MakesWrapped` and `guide.Wrapping` verify now.
- **What formatting builds must be something that can be allocated** (F7, decided by the orchestrator: an obligation, not an assumption). fmt grows its buffer by append, so at each call of a `formats` function and of `fmt.Errorf` vego proves `2 * (bound + 4096) + 65536 <= MaxAlloc` (`fmtBound`, `fmtsize.go`). The bound is the format's bytes, plus for each verb its width, precision, and a wrapper, plus V + 1 times each operand under the widest verb, where each operand counts a type name, 6 bytes a byte of a string under q, x, X, or #v, 80 for an integer, 330 for a float, the width and precision on every element and field. Width and precision are read from a constant format, saturated at 10,000,009, the most fmt reads; a format that is not a constant counts as many verbs as bytes, each with the most of both. `TestBoundOnFormatting` checks each weight alone at the extremes (MaxFloat64 and the smallest float under `%f`, `%e` and large precisions, MinInt64 under `%#b`, invalid bytes under `% #x` and `%+q`, widths on slices) and the whole bound on random formats; each of ten wrong weights fails it. What the logic has no term for is assumed, in "What a proof assumes": the text of an error, what String and Error return, strings and slices below an element of a slice, what `path.Join` joins, and a logger's prefix and header (F17 confined it to these). An assumption for all of it was considered first (O6) and dropped, since the lengths are visible; the cost is that every string a function formats needs a bound, as `"x" + s` did already: `fmt.Println("starting", name)` needs `@Requires |name| <= ...`, and a function that passes its own parameter as the format needs one on it. The message names each length the facts at the call do not bound, with a bound that is enough on the platform (F16). It offers that bound as a Requires only when the Requires, read on entry, would prove it at the call (F20): after `name = other`, the message says to check the length. For a deferred call the bound is proved at each return, with what a pointer points to read there; proved at the defer, it read the struct before the function changed it (F19).
- **The text of an error** (F28, step 2; ruled by the orchestrator: A, the assumption that exists, with one repair). `err.Error()` is supported for an error whose origin formatting accepts (made by `errors.New` or `fmt.Errorf`, here or in a verified function, or vouched for), with `err != nil` proved (`errorText`); for any other origin it is refused with the formatting message. The text is a string of which one fact is known, `2 * (|s| + 4096) + 65536 <= MaxAlloc`: that it can be printed on a line of its own, which What a proof assumes already said of the text of an error (`fmt.Println(err)` verified under it) and now says of the string `Error` returns too. Verified code cannot make it false where the logic has a term: `fmt.Errorf` proves `2 * (bound + 4096) + 65536 <= MaxAlloc` of what it builds, and `errors.New` now requires the same of its text (a constant asks Z3 nothing), with the message `precondition of errors.New may not hold: 2 * (|text| + 4096) + 65536 <= MaxAlloc, so that the text of the error can be printed; it holds when |s| <= N`. No program of the corpus, the guide, or the trial gained an obligation: every `errors.New` there takes a literal (the reviewer counted 126 under `testdata/src`). The reviewer's run at the base on `GOARCH=386`: `fmt.Println(errors.New(s))` verified and returned at 1.0 and 1.4 GB and died with "fatal error: out of memory" at 2.0 GB and 2,147,400,000 bytes, not a panic, so the base had no false proof there; but on 386 the fact bounds `|s|` by 2,147,446,782, just under 2 GiB (vego's `MaxAlloc` is 2^32 - 1 there), and a string of 2,147,400,000 bytes was made, so without the requires `len(errors.New(s).Error()) > 2147446782` could have been proved false where it is true. `http.Error` requires `2 * (|error| + 1) + 65536 <= MaxAlloc`, what `Fprintln` of one string allocates, so `http.Error(w, err.Error(), code)` and a short prefix before the text verify, and twice the text does not (`handlers/errtext.go`).
- **What Sprintf returns is no longer than the bound** when the bound counts everything (no error, no String result, no strings below a slice element). Without that, a string made by Sprintf could never be formatted again. It rests on the documentation, which says they return the formatted string, and on `TestBoundOnFormatting`.
- **The same gap closed in other shipped entries** (F7): `strings.ToUpper` and `ToLower` require `6 * |s| + 65544 <= MaxAlloc`, `strings.Map` and `bytes.Map` `8 * |s| + 65544`, the two `FieldsFunc` `16 * |s| + 65568`, and `os.Remove` `|name| < MaxAlloc`, each from its source in Go 1.27.3. The guide says that an entry for a function that builds in proportion to its arguments must bound them.
- **`fmt.Fprint*(os.Stderr, ...)` and `os.Stdout`** are built since F19 (decided by the orchestrator to keep): `*os.File` carries no code (Two types are counted by what their package lets them hold), so `os.Stdin`, `os.Stdout`, and `os.Stderr` can be read where nothing in the build assigns them, with nothing known of their value, and `(*os.File).Write` ships with no `requires`, since a nil `*File` returns `os.ErrInvalid` and a closed one an error, so converting either to an `io.Writer` meets `(io.Writer).Write`'s entry; the interface is not nil, which is what `Fprintf`'s `w != nil` asks. "What a proof assumes" says that a write to standard output or standard error returns, and that a broken pipe there ends the program with `SIGPIPE`.
- **Writers and loggers.** `Fprintf`, `Fprint`, and `Fprintln` take `runs: [w]` and `requires: w != nil` (F5); the methods of `*log.Logger` take `runs: [l]` and `requires: l != nil`, so the claim of `(io.Writer).Write` covers the writer the logger holds, and stratification counts them (F4). `fmt.Print*` write to `os.Stdout` and `log.Print*` to the standard logger's output, which code that is not verified sets (`os.Stdout`, `log.SetOutput`, `slog.SetDefault`); "What a proof assumes" says that such a write returns and writes nothing verified code can see, and what that rules out (a writer that logs again deadlocks on the logger's lock) (O3, F4).
- **A logger is `ready`, and the entries of its methods require it** (F15, a soundness repair decided by the orchestrator). The entries of `Printf`, `Print`, and `Println` of `*log.Logger` required only `l != nil`, and a logger made as `&log.Logger{}`, or by `log.New(nil, ...)`, or whose output was set to nil, panics in them: `formatting.LogsToZero` verified and panicked. `log.Logger` now has the shipped state `ready`: its output is a writer that is not nil, whichever of `New` and `SetOutput` set it last, and its prefix is at most 4096 bytes, whichever of `New` and `SetPrefix` set it last. It is False in the zero value. The methods require it; `log.New` requires `out != nil` and `|prefix| <= 4096`, keeps `out` in its result, vouches for it, and ensures `fresh(result)` and `ready(result)`. A logger from code that is not verified needs `@Requires ready(l)`, which narrowed `formatting.Logs` and `guide.Inventory`; that is the Requires an unverified caller is assumed to meet, and an assumption about such callers would not cover a zero logger that verified code makes. Since the prefix is bounded, the formatting bound at a method of `*log.Logger` counts 4096 bytes more (`slackOf`), so what verified code sets is not assumed (F5 of the design review); `formatting.LoggerPrintsLong` holds the bound of `log.Println` and is refused. What stays assumed is the file name of a header and the prefix of the standard logger. Tested by a sequence harness (`wrappers_test.go`): loggers made by `New` over writers that record each call, with output and prefix changed as code that is not verified may, the methods called only when `ready` holds of the harness's ghost; the writer must run only during a method, on its goroutine, and only through `Write` (it has `WriteString`, `ReadFrom`, and `WriteTo`, whose calls fail the test), and `New` must run nothing. `fmt.Fprintf`, `Fprint`, and `Fprintln` are held to the same writers (F8 of the design review). Without `ready(l)` in the requires of `Printf`, the harness panics on a nil output.
- **Functions that do not return have no entry.** `log.Fatal*` and `log.Panic*` are left out: a trusted contract claims that its function returns.

Not built yet:

- **A verified logging function that forwards `args...`** (`@Formats args`). The Requires of a String method is proved in the caller's state, which the wrapper may change before it forwards; the way in would keep the wrapper from writing before it formats, as for `formats` in an entry. Today such a function is trusted with `formats`.
- **Nested Stringers**: a field or element whose type has String or Error. fmt calls those at depth for exported fields and elements.
- **Format and GoString methods**, a String or Error with a non-empty Modifies, and pointers to anything but a struct.
- **Functions that never return** (`log.Fatal`, `os.Exit`, `log.Panic`): an entry field saying so, under which the path ends after an exit, and the call is an obligation of unreachability for a panic.
- **`log.Default()`** needs an entry with `result != nil` and `ready(result)`, whose reason must say that the standard logger's output and prefix are set by code that is not verified, so that `ready` of it is a claim about that code. **A package-level logger**, `var logger = log.New(...)`, cannot be read: `log.New` ships since F15, but a variable of a type that can hold code, and an initialiser that calls a trusted function, wait for part 3 of package-level variables.
- **Generic variadic functions** (`slices.Concat`), with generics.

### JSON

Built as F29 (2026-11-23), item 2 of the list at the top, in four reviewed steps and a commit for the in-place decoder; the design was agreed with the reviewer (F1 to F13 of the design review) and the orchestrator (rulings D1 to D8). `json.go` encodes and `jsondecode.go` decodes.

Decisions:

- **Four builds.** Go 1.26 builds encoding/json from its v1 code and Go 1.27 from encoding/json/v2 (`GOEXPERIMENT=jsonv2` is on by default there); each can be built the other way. Every claim holds of the four or is not shipped (D6, F8): `TestJSONEntries` builds a helper (`testdata/jsonharness`) with and without `GOEXPERIMENT=nojsonv2` in every run, `VEGO_GO126=1` adds Go 1.26.6 both ways, and `TestEncoding386` (`VEGO_386=1`) runs the bounds at the 32-bit edge. In v2, `json.RawMessage` is an alias of `jsontext.Value`, and the walk takes either as the leaf.
- **New claims, not `runs`.** The probe at the base showed that an entry with `runs: [v]` verified a `MarshalJSON` that writes the caller's memory and an unbounded `Marshal`. `encodes` (Marshal, MarshalIndent, and Encode) and `decodes` (Unmarshal and Decode) name the parameter whose value encoding/json reads or decodes into; in their place each call walks the static type of the argument before its conversion to `any` (`jsonPlainReason`). Only shipped entries may list them (F13); a type entry may not.
- **The walk.** Accepted: numbers, booleans, strings, `[]byte`, slices, maps with keys of a string or integer kind and no methods, structs and pointers to them, embedded structs exported or not, by value and through a pointer (F2), and three leaves of the standard library, `time.Time`, `json.RawMessage`, and `json.Number`. Refused, each with a message that names the path: an interface value (dynamic types), a type that holds itself, a method of the value or the pointer method set that some build calls (`MarshalJSON`, `MarshalJSONTo`, `AppendText`, `MarshalText`, `UnmarshalJSON`, `UnmarshalJSONFrom`, `UnmarshalText`; D4: no method of the project runs), `IsZero` under `omitzero` but on `time.Time` (F1), tag options but `omitempty`, `omitzero`, and `string` and names v1 would not take (D7, F7), an unexported field with a tag, an embedded field with a tag, a state or an owner. The walk enters every struct through one guard with limits on depth (32), groups (256), and steps (20,000): its first version entered a struct behind a pointer without the cycle guard, and a run on the corpus took sixteen gigabytes in 23 seconds and the machine's agents with it (`TestWalkOfEncodingEnds`).
- **The bound of encoding** (D1: an obligation, never an assumption). The walk groups what the output holds by chain of slices and maps, weighed by `jsonWeights` (an escaped byte is six bytes, seven under `string`; a float at most 25; a map entry also what the builds allocate to sort the keys, 10 words, the key, and the value). At a call each length the type holds is bounded from the facts by a linear question, under the quantifiers and guards of its chain (`lengthBounds`: first every length at most n0, the largest power of ten at which a uniform bound fits; then each alone, at n0 at the full limit, lowered or raised by quick questions); `jsonFold` multiplies and adds those bounds, and `jsonFits` requires three times and a page within MaxAlloc and five times within MaxInt. A product of lengths under quantifiers ran Z3 to its limit on a map of slices and on `MarshalIndent` with parameters (F15); the orchestrator accepted the per-length form in place of the fallback. `jsonFold` is the single source (F14, F23): the call is proved against it, `TestJSONEntries` computes it from each sample's own lengths and holds every output of the four builds against it, `TestEncoding386` finds its edge with it, and the term stated of the result is the same fold over terms. Every weight made one smaller is shown to fail; mutants of the fold and of each branch of `lengthBounds` make a refused corpus program verify (F20).
- **What is known of the result** (F17, F21). `|result1|` is at most the fold over terms: what lies under no chain and no guard is the caller's own term, exact, and the rest the number for the bounds proved. `append(js, '\n')` and a loop writing rows into a buffer verify.
- **MarshalIndent** multiplies by `3 + |prefix| + depth * |indent|`, the depth of the type or 10000 under a RawMessage.
- **The in-place encoder** (D2). `json.NewEncoder(w).Encode(v)` written as one expression is verified as Marshal's walk and bound with one byte more, then w's own Write of a new slice no longer than the bound, chosen as a conversion chooses it (`concreteMethod`, F24: a promoted method refused, a value receiver through a pointer checked not nil). Nothing is kept, so a `ResponseWriter` is allowed. Its error may be Write's and is not vouched for. Any other use of an Encoder or NewEncoder is refused.
- **Decoding** (D5). `json.Unmarshal(data, &x)` decodes into `&x` for a variable, a field, or an element, or into a pointer to a struct. Every slice, map, and pointer the decoder writes must be nil, a slice of no capacity, or it appends into the array, keeps a map's entries, or decodes through the pointer. A struct is written through the pointer with a frame of the whole struct, and what json does not write keeps its value, said in the caller's terms (F25, F26); a struct whose kept field no formula compares (an unexported slice) is refused. Anything else is a result stored into the variable. The decoder writes on an error too (F3). One allocation is at most `decodeFactor` (8, or an element of a slice, or twice a map's key and value and 16) times the data, since a string grows to three times the data (F4); run at the 32-bit edge. Errors are vouched for, read in the four builds, `base64.CorruptInputError` included (D8). Nothing is said of what was decoded but its type: an invariant is checked by code after the decode.
- **The in-place decoder** (D3). `json.NewDecoder(io.LimitReader(r, n)).Decode(&x)` is verified as r's own Read of a new slice, then the decoding of Unmarshal from at most n bytes, with the bound stated on n. The decoder calls Read as many times as it needs, so a Read whose contract reads what its Modifies lists of the reader is refused (F28 of the review): a contract that means the same at every call, applied once, forgets what any number of calls write and says only what holds of each. Whether a clause reads that memory is decided by proof, as the entry-only rule decides it (F30): the clause is read over the state of a call and a state after it, and must mean the same when the reader's memory is forgotten again in either (F32: read in one state, `f.Odd' != f.Odd` passed, and a clause of the state after a call does not hold when the decoder makes none), so a Read with `@Modifies r, p` and `@Requires r != nil` verifies (`FromCycle` in the corpus), which a rule by name refuses. In v1 it calls Read until it returns bytes or an error, so a Read whose contract allows neither is refused (found after 51513c6); `(io.Reader).Read` allows it, and a shipped `progresses:` declaration says the body a server passes makes progress, read from source and held on real servers, with verified code kept from storing into `Request.Body` (ruled by the orchestrator, What a proof assumes). Of the shipped readers (F31), `(*os.File).Read` makes progress: its entry says `|b| > 0 -> (n > 0 Or err != nil)`, read from eofError and ZeroReadIsEOF on Unix and Windows and from File.read on Plan 9 and held by its harness, so a file decodes in place; `(io.Reader).Read` does not; a `*bufio.Reader` has no shipped Read, so decoding from one is refused for want of a contract, and would need its own entry, whose progress follows from that of the reader it reads from. Its error may be the reader's and is not vouched for, so a handler answers with a message of its own, or reads the body in a bounded loop and calls Unmarshal when it needs the error's text. A decoder without a limit, one held in a variable, and one from elsewhere are refused.
- **Cost.** `go test -count=1 ./vego/...` took 136.1 s at the base 5352b8d (from a `git archive` extract) and 140.7 s at 1696e94, each run alone in a scope of its own. Of the 4,732 rows of the cost table at the base, all remain and none more than doubles: 445.88 M units at the base, 445.90 M after. The table gained 185 rows, those of `jsonenc` and `jsondec` 155 of them with 2,840 problems and 38.8 M units. The costliest are `encoding/json.Unmarshal` in `jsondec` (159 problems, 13.8 M units, 28 left open), `jsonenc.All` (27, 3.3 M, 9 open), `jsonenc.OnTheType` (36, 1.7 M), and `jsondec.Enveloped` (42, 1.2 M). The check of the contract of `Read` under the in-place decoder (F30, F32) asks two quick questions for each conjunct of that contract at each call of the decoder: `FromCycle`, whose `Read` has four conjuncts, went from 29 problems and 165,411 units to 33 and 170,580 when the second question was added, so the check costs it 8 problems and about 10 k units of 170 k, and `FromFile` 4 more problems and 4.4 k units.

Not built yet:

- An encoder or a decoder held in a variable (`enc := json.NewEncoder(w); enc.SetIndent("", "  "); enc.Encode(a); enc.Encode(b)`), and with it `DisallowUnknownFields`: following a kept writer or reader into a local, as F28 lists for `bufio.NewWriter` over a `ResponseWriter`.
- Methods of the project that encoding/json runs (D4). A `MarshalJSON` would need a contract that modifies nothing, a `Requires` implied by the type alone (it runs on every element), a bound on `|result|` from its `Ensures`, and errors of known origin; an `UnmarshalJSON` would also have to keep nothing of what it is handed.
- `encodes` and `decodes` in a project's entry (F13): what bound a wrapper claims, how it names a prefix and an indent, and how a writing wrapper is checked are not written down. A verified function that forwards a value of type `any` to Marshal is refused at the call.
- A map with string keys whose values hold elements of unknown length (see Maps, Not built yet).
- Decoding into a struct with an unexported slice, a field of a type vego does not model, or an unexported field of another package's struct, which Unmarshal leaves as it was and no formula compares.
- Facts that what Unmarshal stores is new memory: the decoded references are of no known origin; that they are new follows from the nil obligation, but is not stated.
- The in-place decoder over a reader whose shipped `Read` does not say that it makes progress. `(io.Reader).Read` cannot say so of every reader. `*bufio.Reader` has no shipped `Read`, and an entry cannot say it makes progress on its own (F30, step 5, listed rather than built): read from the source of Go 1.27.3, its `Read` calls the kept reader's `Read` once, unlike `fill`, and returns 0 and nil when that does, which a reader whose `Read` returns 0 and nil shows (`ReadByte` over the same reader fails with `io.ErrNoProgress`). So the entry needs a state of the Reader, set by `NewReader` only when the reader it is given makes progress, and with it a fact of an interface value that its `Read` makes progress: a field a `progresses` declaration names, or a value converted from a type whose `Read` says so, as `*os.File`. No function of the trial waits for it. A request's body makes progress by declaration and `*os.File` by its entry.
- Pointers to anything but a struct (`*float64`, the trial's `trialSummary.Score`), arrays, interface values (item 4).

### Maps

Built: maps with integer, string, or boolean keys and values of any modelled type; reading, the two-result read, writing, `delete`, `len`, `make`, literals, nil, `range`, `Modifies`, and `m[k]`, `has(m, k)`, and `|m|` in annotations. Since F23 (2026-11-21, maps): a range walks one unknown order of the keys, a `Visited` annotation names the keys a loop has visited, `Forall k in m` and `Exists k in m` range over the keys of a map or of a visited set, a loop may write keys the map has and delete keys of the map it ranges over, `Modifies m[k]` names one entry, the number of keys is bounded by memory and by the key type, and the shipped entries of `maps` say which keys the maps have.

Decisions:

- **A map is a reference into memory of its own.** For each map type there is a region saying which keys each map has, one for each leaf of the values, and one for the number of keys. Two maps may be the same map, like two pointers.
- **A key becomes a number.** A string key becomes the number of a function that gives every sequence a number, the same for two sequences exactly when they have the same length and elements. A boolean key is 0 or 1. An integer is itself.
- **An absent key has the zero value**, so `m[k]` needs no case distinction. This, that the count is not negative, that a present key makes it positive, and that the nil map is empty are stated for each map and key that is read.
- **The number of keys is bounded by memory and by the key type** (F23, D2 decided by the orchestrator as an assumption about the runtime). At every read of a map, `noteMap` states `|m| <= MaxInt`, since `len` gives an int; `|m| * (sizeof K + sizeof V) <= MaxAlloc`; and `|m|` at most the number of values of the key type when that is smaller (2 for bool, 256 for 8 bits). The memory bound rests on the gc runtime keeping the key and the value of every entry in memory of its own in the heap (inline in a slot, or for a key or value over 128 bytes in an allocation of its full size behind a pointer in the slot), and on the heap's addresses lying in a range of 2^heapAddrBits bytes, which `maxAlloc` is (one less on 32-bit, where the map's header takes a byte too). The specification says nothing of it, so it is a line of "What a proof assumes". The reviewer read Go 1.27.3's `internal/runtime/maps`, both slot layouts, for keys and values of size zero and for the large ones kept behind a pointer, and checked the hashmap of Go before 1.24 from memory, since 1.27.3 no longer has its source. It lets `make([]T, len(m))` with T the key or value type be proved, but not an `append` per key, which needs twice the length when it moves.
- **The count is tied to the keys only in a range** (D3, decided by the orchestrator). A write adds one if the key was absent and a delete takes one off if it was present, and an entry written by a callee changes the count by what its presence did. Nothing else ties the count to the set of keys: `has(m, "a") And has(m, "b")` does not give `|m| >= 2`. A bijection for every map state would be two quantifiers in every problem that holds a map, and F11 and F12 measured quantified facts over nested structures as where Z3 loses time. A tie at ground reads only (per state, with regions of positions) was the reviewer's other option for F4 and was not taken. It would not see keys that a quantifier forces, so the check at a range is needed anyway.
- **A range over a map walks one order of the keys it has at the start** (F23). Per loop, fresh sequences `pos` (key number to position) and `order` (position to key number), with Bij: `Forall k : has0[k] -> 0 <= pos[k] < n0 And order[pos[k]] = k`, where has0 is the row of keys at the start and n0 the count. The iteration at position i gets the key `order[i]`, and while `i < n0` (F1: bind also runs at entry and after the last iteration) `has0[key]` and `pos[key] = i`. Keys of different iterations differ by congruence on `pos`, with no quantifier. Two walks of one map have orders of their own, since Go fixes none, also not between two walks (F11). Why it is true: the keys present at the start are finitely many, n0 of them, and Go produces each entry present at the start exactly once unless it is removed before it is reached; `order` is that order.
- **`Visited` names the keys a loop has visited** (F23, D1 decided by the orchestrator). `// @Visited done` directly above a range over a map. `has(done, k)` is `has0[k] And (pos[k] < i Or n0 <= i)`, and `|done|` is i. The disjunct `n0 <= i` is Bij's completeness at the exit, so after a whole run `done` is exactly the keys at the start without Z3 instantiating Bij. A ground read adds the instance of Bij at the key, a bound one Bij itself once per walk. Its rules are those of a `Snapshot`, checked in `checkVisited`: the scope is the loop, the name must name nothing, nothing in the loop declares it again, a quantifier does not bind it, a literal in the loop does not name it, an annotation above the loop that is not one of its own does not name it, one per loop, and something names it. A formula reads it only as `|done|`, `has(done, k)`, and in `Forall k in done`.
- **A state that no map can be in is reported at a range** (F4 of the design review, F23). Bij and the surjection (`Forall i in [0, n0) : has0[order[i]] And pos[order[i]] = i`), of which every fact the loop assumes is an instance, are added to a copy of the state. If that is contradictory and the state was not, the range reports `what is known here says that m has a number of keys other than |m|, which no map can`. It reports only what Z3 shows: Z3 5.4.1 gives up on these two quantifiers ("incomplete quantifiers") for many states that have a model, a map with one key among them, since a model must make the keys finite, so an open check reports nothing, as with the other contradiction checks. On the main corpus at step 1: 63 ranges probed, 4 reported, 28 found a model, 31 open (25 of them in a chain of 30 ranges).
- **Quantifiers over the keys of a map** (F23). `Forall k in m : P` is `Forall k : has(m, k) -> P` and `Exists` conjoins it. `k` has the sort of the keys, a sequence for strings (the first bound variable of sequence sort; the encoder needed no change, and TestRules has cases for it). Inside a quantifier the solver states no tie between the key of a sequence and its contents. So for each string that the body compares a bound key with (`=`, `!=`), `Key(k) = Key(t) <-> k = t` is conjoined to the guards (F12 of the review of step 2). That is true of every two strings, and it is what makes `Forall k in g : has(g', k) And (k = node Or g'[k] = g[k])` provable. Comparisons through a predicate, `has`, a length, or an index get no tie, and neither do two keys of nested quantifiers. `Z` in a domain is the integers: a `Z` that names something is refused there, and so is a bound variable named `Z` (F6, F13).
- **What a loop may change of the map it ranges over** (F23, D5 decided by the orchestrator). The specification gives a change one meaning when it adds no key: an entry not yet reached that is removed is not produced, and every other entry present at the start is produced once, with its value read when it is produced (`m[k]` in the table of range clauses). So every run of a loop that deletes but never adds is a walk of one order fixed at the start: the keys in the order produced, each key deleted before it was reached placed right after the iteration that deleted it, where it is absent and runs no body. A key added during the loop may or may not be produced, the choice varying per entry, so it has no one meaning, and a loop that adds a key in each iteration may never end. The rules, at every write and delete (`storeKey`) and every call target (`apply`) against each active range of the same map type:
  - a write needs `m != r Or has(m_now, k)`;
  - a call that may modify a map of the type needs `t.ref != r`;
  - a delete in fact mode needs `m != r Or ~has(m_now, k) Or k = key_i Or has(seen_i, k)` (the backstop of F2), and in skip mode nothing.
  Fact mode is chosen from the syntax (`deletesOtherKeys`): every delete of a map of the ranged type, in the body or a literal it runs, has as key the loop's own key variable, which the loop does not assign and which is not boxed. Then every position's key is still there, and the head assumes it. Otherwise the loop is in skip mode: a position whose key is gone runs no body (`loopShape.runs`), the invariant must hold across it, and `done` counts the positions passed, skipped ones included. The backstop makes fact mode independent of the syntax test: a delete the test missed is reported, not assumed away. Before Go 1.22 the iterations share the key variable, which still holds the current iteration's key when a delete through it runs. The active ranges end at a break, after the last position, and at a return of their frame, before its deferred calls. A literal handed to a trusted function is inlined in the same verifier, so its writes meet the rules (F3).
- **`Modifies m[k]` names one entry when the values are not references** (F23, D4 decided by the orchestrator). A path that ends at a key of a map whose values are not pointers, slices, maps, or interface values names the entry: whether the key is present, its value, and the number of keys.
