A caller keeps every other key, and the count becomes `count + ite(has) - ite(had)` (`havocEntry`); an absent key gets the zero value, the nil map no key, and where a call on one branch did not run, the entry is as it was. Several entries of one call are forgotten one after another. A path that ends in a reference keeps naming what it refers to, as a pointer field does, so the entry of a map of references is covered only by the whole map, and the message for a write of one says so (F8). A contract with `Modifies m` says what it keeps with a quantifier. A write is covered by an entry with the same map and an equal key, and a write of the whole map by no entry. A loop forgets the whole map for an entry, which is sound and keeps less.
- **`delete` on the nil map does nothing**, as in Go. In the model the removal writes that the key is absent and has the zero value, which is already true of every key of the nil map, so no rule about the nil map is ever broken.
- **Samples of maps have one key.** The search for an input on which a contract forces success tries the nil map and maps with a single key. In a value that is a struct each number is picked on its own, and everything else in it is zero. The facts of F23 hold of them.
- **The shipped entries of `maps` say which keys the maps have** (F23). Clone, Copy, and DeleteFunc gained `Forall k in ...` clauses about keys, true at every class of key their constraint admits and checked against the real functions by an evaluator that ranges over the keys of real maps (F9c); they say nothing of values, which may be structs.

Not built yet:

- **Pointer keys** (cut from F23, as the orchestrator allowed). A pointer to a struct of size other than zero would be a key as the sequence `[ref, at]`, keyed as a string is, with nil made `(0, 0)` (since `&p.f` of a nil `p` is `(0, off)` in the model). Every place a key is one term (index expressions, `delete`, literals, the key of a walk, Modifies entries, formula paths, Visited) would build it. The key of a walk would be a fresh pointer whose sequence has the walk's number, with the type facts of a pointer. A quantifier over pointer keys stays refused, since a bound sequence is no pointer in a formula. A pointer to a value of size zero has no identity, so it stays refused as a key.
- **Struct keys**, which wait for equality of whole structs in formulas, and **float and interface keys**, which wait for floats and dynamic types.
- **A bound on what the elements of a map's value hold, under a quantifier over the keys, when the facts bound the length of the keys** (measured by the reviewer of F29, step 1). With a bound on `|k|` in the facts, Z3 runs to its limit on `Forall k in m : (Forall i in [0, |m[k]|) : |m[k][i]| <= 10000000)`, even from the same fact with 1000, and also when the bound on the keys stands in a clause of its own (4.0 M units). Without a bound on the keys it proves (0.3 to 0.7 M units), and so does a map with integer keys (44 k). Its first customer is `map[string][]string`, the type of a header and of many configurations: encoding a map with string keys whose values hold a slice or a map with elements of unknown length is refused (see JSON), and a map with integer keys verifies.
- **The count tied to the keys outside a range** (D3): `has(m, "a") And has(m, "b")` does not give `|m| >= 2`.
- **An entry of a map of references named alone in `Modifies`.** `m[k]` names what the value refers to, as `p.next` does, and the field or entry itself is covered by `p` or `m`. A spelling for the entry, as `entry(m, k)`, was left out.
- **A loop that calls a function with `Modifies m[k]`** forgets the whole map, the whole-map site of an entry. A finer site would keep the other keys.
- **Adding a key and then leaving the loop** (`m[x] = v; break`, or `return`) has one meaning and is refused. A list of pending adds per active range, checked empty where an iteration goes on to the next one, would build it.
- **What memory held on entry refers only to what existed on entry.** A reference read from memory is known to exist when it is read, not on entry. The trial's `transpose` needs it to know that a set new to the function is not one of `g`'s.
- **A tie of a string key to a string compared through a predicate, `has`, a length, or an index**, or between two keys of nested quantifiers.
- **Samples of maps with more than one key**, and maps built in the input search with a symbolic set of keys.

### Struct invariants

Built as decided below, including invariants of types of other packages, which travel as analysis facts with the constants they mention. A declaration is checked once: an invariant that cannot be read, or a set of invariants no value satisfies, is an error even if nothing uses the type.

Decisions:

- **Not assumed after trusted code.** A verified function proves the invariants of what it hands back, so its callers may assume them. A trusted function proves nothing, so after it the invariant of a result, or of a struct it may have modified, has to be proved again before it is relied on. Since F30 that proof may be a frame: nothing the invariant reads changed since a point where it held (Invariants kept by frame), which is a proof and assumes nothing of the trusted function.
- **Kept between boundaries where nothing it reads changes** (F30, Invariants kept by frame): a goal that the invariants of a value hold, at a boundary or as `valid(x)`, is proved by frame where nothing they read changed since a point where they held, and a loop invariant likewise from the head of its loop. It changes how the goal is asked, not where invariants are assumed.
- **Only at boundaries, only for what is passed directly, and what that owns.** The invariant is applied to parameters, receivers, and results whose type is the struct or a pointer to it, and since ownership in source (F27, step 2) to the structs they own through owned pointer fields and owners held by value. A struct inside another struct that does not own memory, in a slice, or behind a pointer field that is not owned is not covered. The trial on real code measured what this cost (F16): in eliben's REST server, 4 of the 9 handlers call a method of the task store they reach through `ts.store`, whose invariant `tasks != nil And 0 <= nextId` was then not known, and package main cannot require it, since a formula names unexported fields only in their own package (State that a value owns, F1). Built for owned pointer fields by F27 (Ownership in source, step 2), with `valid(x)` for a loop's head. Still not covered: a struct behind a pointer field that is not owned, and the structs in a slice or a map, owned or not, whose elements are not owned.
- **Declared on the type.** `// @Invariant 0 <= Count And Count <= |Items|` in the doc comment of a struct type, naming its fields directly.
- **Shorthand for contract clauses at every boundary.** The invariant is assumed on entry for every parameter and receiver of the type, proved on return for those and for every result of the type, and proved at each call for every argument. Between those points it may be broken. It is only ever assumed where a verified caller has proved it. Like a `Requires`, it is taken on trust from callers that are not verified.
- **Always enforced, for every function, with no opt-out.** An opt-out would let the invariant be switched off function by function while the type still advertises it. A helper that must run while the invariant is broken takes the fields it needs instead of the struct, or the caller restores the invariant before calling it. Revisit only if real code cannot be restructured that way.
- **Best on a struct's own fields.** An invariant may mention what a pointer field points to, but any call that might modify that target makes the verifier forget the invariant. Ownership between structs is deferred with linked structures.
- **The boilerplate about slices a struct holds is absorbed by ownership** (F27). A struct that does not declare `@Owns` still promises `fresh(s.items) Or within(s.items', s.items)` from a method that appends to `s.items`; one that does needs nothing, since growth is checked at every store into an owned field (see Ownership in source).
- **An invariant can read a state**, as in `@Invariant ~held(mu)`, which is then assumed and proved at boundaries like any clause.

### Invariants kept by frame

Being built as F30, in reviewed steps; the design was agreed with the reviewer (F1 to F8 of its review) and the orchestrator (D-a to D-d). Measured first: Z3 fails to align two readings of one quantified formula even where nothing was written between them (the trial's `GetTasksByDueDate` ran to the limit proving `valid(ts)` on loop entry, after a `ts.Lock()` that writes nothing the invariant reads), while the question whether every value a clause reads is the same in two memories is ground once Z3 names the quantifiers' variables, and is cheap.

Built in step 1 (`frames.go`):

- **A frame states no fact.** A goal that the invariants of a value hold, at a return, at a call, or as a `valid(x)` conjunct of an `Ensures`, a callee's `Requires`, a loop invariant, or an `Assert`, is first asked as a frame: nothing the clause reads has changed since a point of the path where it held. When the frame does not hold, the clause is proved as before, and the message is the one it was. Where it holds, the goal is assumed as `check` assumes a goal it proved, so every later problem is the one it was without the feature, apart from what a changed outcome changes.
- **K, entries.** A state holds entries (`keptEntry`): a step of `invariantSteps` (a struct type's invariants, a value, the nil guards of the path to it) and a copy of the memory it held in. An entry is made only where those invariants are assumed with no condition of evaluation around them: for the parameters on entry, after a verified call for what it hands back, and for a `valid(x)` that is a conjunct of the top level of a `Requires` on entry, of a loop invariant at the head, and of a callee's `Ensures` after the call when every `valid` of the clause is such a conjunct (`keepValid`). States made by cloning pass entries on; a literal with one end passes on the end's, and a join of several ends keeps the entries of the state at the call, which every end began from. The run of a literal handed to a trusted function is a state of its own, which the caller's does not continue (`HandedBreak`). The always-fails check, `canBeMet`, the audit's candidates, and the check that a type's invariants do not contradict each other make no entry and ask no frame.
- **F, the frame lemma.** The clause is resolved once through a `frameEnv`, which implements every interface the resolver asks of an environment (`Env`, `Applier`, `Gatherer`) without embedding, reads each name in the entry's memory and in the goal's, records that the two results are the same (a pointer by its allocation and position, nil ones too), and returns the entry's. A quantifier's domain is read before its variable is bound, so its pairs are quantified over every value of the variable; the body's pairs are under the domain read in the entry's memory. The two readings gather under each quantifier exactly what O1 gathers, where O1 does (in a function that lists a footprint or an owner): what a layer of a write through a footprint, or one whose targets hold an owned part, keeps at the cells they read, the type facts, the closure, and the facts of a map, and those facts are premises of the pairs at that level only. Step 1 gathered what every layer and join keeps, in every function; no program of the corpus or the trial needed more than O1's gathering, and neither the wrong version that gathered a layer's cells as kept whatever its targets nor the one that gathered only where O1 does could be observed, since the questions they touch run to their limit either way (a layer between the head of a loop and the end of its body, `WriteByCall`), so step 3 went back to O1's, which adds nothing to guard. The goal is: the goal's guard gives the entry's, the value is the entry's, and every pair holds. When every pair is the same term, the value is, and the entry's guard is part of the goal's, it holds with no problem asked. `TestFrameEnvComplete` type-checks package `logic` and takes every type assertion and type switch on a value whose static type is `Env` or an interface related to it by embedding, whatever the value is named, and fails unless `frameEnv` implements each interface asked (F10 of the review); a method added to one of them fails to build. A value of the empty interface is left out, since every type implements it, and the test fails where the environment is converted to one.
- **What a frame asks is its own.** Names come from a namespace of their own (`frameNames`), which `v.made` never records; regions of an epoch the frame read first are made again by the function's own first read; its reads note into a scratch state; it cannot report; and every other table of the verifier must be as it was, or the run panics (`tableSizes`, by reflection over the verifier's maps and slices).
- **Which clauses.** A clause without a quantifier is framed only when every pair is the same term; otherwise it is proved again, which costs little. The question of a frame is asked, at a limit of 2 M units (half the default; D-d: the trial's `getAllTasksHandler` needs 1.2 M), only of a clause with a quantifier, and not where a region was written directly, by a store, a write, or a splice, since the entry, at a reference at which a read of the clause outside every quantifier reads that very region, as a store method writes the table of keys that `has(tasks, k)` reads at the map (`writtenAt`, F11 of the review; a store into another field of the same struct, as a counter beside the map, is not one, F15: `ServeTwice`): such a frame cannot hold, and its question ran to the limit, which took the trial's `CreateTask` from 1.36 M units to 3.36 M; it is 1.35 M now. A frame that cannot hold and that this does not see still costs its limit before the clause is proved again: on the main corpus 2 frames stop at the limit (`Scribble` and `PassTags`, defects), and none in the trial with the store's invariant. Of `valid(x)`, a clause whose quantified conjuncts a frame proves has its other conjuncts proved on their own, each read in the frame's namespace (`residue`).
- **When a frame does not hold and the clause is not proved, the message says why** (F6 of the review; the orchestrator's condition). A state logs each statement after which its memory changed, with what it is and the memory after it (`logWrite`, from `stmt` and the loops of `seq`, and before the return's goals, for what runs at the return). The report of the clause gains a sentence: `it was not kept from entry, since |tasks[k].Tags[j]| may have changed: the assignment to t.Tags[0] at defects.go:15:3 may write it`. The statements logged since the entry are taken in order, leaving out those after which memory is the entry's or what it was after the one before (F12 of the review: the call statement that made the entry was named). For each, the clause is read in the entry's memory and the memory after it, at the entry's value, so that the reading is about memory alone, with no question asked; the first after which some read is no longer the same term touched what the clause reads, and a question at the quick limit then asks whether the frame still holds there, which a write beside the clause's reads passes, at most six questions. The read named is the first whose tables of memory that statement changed, read in the memory before it and after it (a read whose only difference is an index or a reference read before it inherited the change); a statement that changed every table of a clause that reads more than one, or that started an epoch, as a write through a footprint does, is named with "what it reads", since what such a write may lose is what lies in the footprint, which no read of the clause names (F16). A read is spelled from the reads its index was made of, the variables of the formula's quantifiers, and numbers, never as a term of the logic. A call in an assignment or a declaration is named as the call (`the call to lib.Fill in the assignment to n`), a literal as `the function literal called`; a literal with one end passes on its logged writes with its entries. Where no logged statement touched what the clause reads, there is no sentence. Both kinds are counted on their own in `-vego.cost`. It says "may": nothing in it is a proof. Where the entry is from: `entry`, `the call to fix at ...`, `the head of the loop at ...`. Kinds of write named: an assignment, a call, a loop, an `if` or `switch` statement around a write, a declaration, and what runs at the return.
Built in step 2, GR (agreed with the reviewer, F13 and F14 of its review of the argument; decided in scope by the orchestrator):

- **The rule.** The growth layer of a call that writes no footprint keeps the row of a home that existed before it, at every cell, new allocations' too, when no cell of the call's struct targets lay in that row before the call (`layer.apart`, `growsOnlyThroughTargets`, `untouchedRow`, read in `rowAt`, with the separation of a listed footprint stated at each target cell as `unchanged` states it where a footprint layer is read). Before GR, every cell of an allocation the call made was unconstrained in every row, so `p.Push(5); w.Write(b)` lost `p.items'[0] = 5` across the write, while the two calls in the other order kept it.
- **The invariant of rows restated** (under Footprints, beside F18): a row of a home that existed before a call grows during it only if (W) the call writes that value's footprint, which is to say its methods run, or (S) the call stores into a cell that lay in that row before the call, where a follow layer starts. Each way a row grows, and which it is:
  1. a method of the value creates memory: by What a proof assumes, a method adds to its value's footprint only memory it creates, and only a method whose contract lists its receiver; one that does not list it writes nothing behind it, which keeping what it made would be; a function value or an interface value from code that is not verified "writes only what the Modifies of its type lists" (What a proof assumes), so it does not call a writer it captured unless its contract writes that footprint; the same holds of a value a trusted function runs under `runs`, whose writes are in the call's targets. So (W), and a call that writes no footprint at all, the only calls GR changes, runs no such method;
  2. a follow layer, a store into an owned field whose cell lies in the row: the first follow inside a callee that reaches an old row writes a cell that lay in it at the call, since a cell made during the call joins an old row only through a follow, and the callee's frame covers that cell, so a target of the call holds it. (S);
  3. a shipped owner held by value in a converted struct of the project grows: the trusted method lists the owner, a struct target whose cells lie in the row. (S);
  4. a conversion: it defines the row of a new home only;
  5. a row or stretch claim of a callee: a claim that places memory in an old row is a `within(x, w)` of an interface value, which only w's methods can make true, so it comes with w under `modifies` and the call writes that footprint (`TestWithinListsFootprint` holds this of every shipped entry);
  6. `stores`: memory joins no row, as before;
  7. a loop's head: GR does not change its growth layer (Not built, below).

  The condition is read in the model's row before the call. By the invariant of rows the model's row holds at least the true row, so no target cell in it means none in the true row, and keeping the model's row keeps it at least the true row after; that is why it may be read in the model, and why GR keeps the invariant.
- **The narrowing.** Struct targets (a struct or some of its fields) are asked cell by cell. A range target (a slice's elements, an owned slice's array) or a map target whose element or value type holds by value no struct with owned fields, shipped or of the project, and no interface value needs nothing, since no follow can start from its cells. Any other range or map target, as a `[]Pile` of owners listed whole, leaves the layer as part 1 made it; so do a call that writes a footprint or a states row, and a call of a trusted function with a contract under `calls`, whose callbacks `runHanded` runs in states of their own that the caller does not continue. A trusted function with a parameter under `runs` and nothing under `calls` gets GR: what it runs is a value whose type has a contract, and such a value writes only what that contract lists (case 1), so a footprint it writes is in the entry's `modifies` and the call writes it.
- Programs (`frames/gr.go`): `PushThenWrite` (the reviewer's gr4), `PutThenWrite` (a map `Put` may have made), and `ResetThenWrite` (an owned pointer field a call replaced) verify, refused at the base; `WriteThenPush` verifies at both. Refused, each where the new memory could lie in the row: `HandedTheWriter` (the call writes w), `IntoTheRow` (a converted owner pushed onto), `Elements` (a `[]Pile` refilled under `Modifies ps`, the reviewer's F13), and `HandedSomethingElse` (a callee handed a struct that points to the converted pile, F13). Wrong versions caught: the condition dropped (`IntoTheRow`, `HandedSomethingElse`), the narrowing's test of the element type dropped (`Elements`), and GR off (the three programs that verify). The condition read after the call is the same condition, since a target is old memory whose cells a growth layer keeps at old homes; a range target checked by its first cell does not arise, since under the narrowing no range target is checked by its cells.
- Cost: of the 101 rows that moved, units 34.6 M to 34.9 M; two more than doubled, each from the condition GR states at every cell of a footprint asked across a growth layer it changes: `footprints.MakeAndWrite` 6.0 times (40 k to 236 k units, one more problem left open) and `guide.LogToNew` 2.0 times (21 k to 42 k); the rest under 1.2 times. The trial's handlers moved by under 4%.
Built in step 5, the small things:

- **`mime.ParseMediaType`** (`trusted/std/mime.yaml`). Read from the source of Go 1.26 and 1.27, which is the same: `requires 256 * (|v| + 1) + 65536 <= MaxAlloc`, `vouches: [err]` (each of its errors is a package-level variable made by `errors.New`), `ensures (err = nil) <-> (params != nil)`. The harness of `TestShippedContracts` holds the ensures against the real function on thirteen shapes of value (a wrong version, `params = nil`, fails on a duplicate parameter). The factor was 32 at first, which counted the lowercased media type and keys but not what a parameter whose name has a star costs: an entry in a map of base names, a map of its own for each new base name, and the keys the stitching makes with `fmt.Sprintf`; distinct starred names allocate 89 bytes for each byte of a value of 4.7 KB (F20 of the review of step 5). The reason now counts each, with a map's entry measured at every count up to 300,000 (at most 178 bytes for strings to strings), at most 174 bytes for each byte of a parameter of five bytes. `TestMediaTypeBound` reads the factor from the entry and runs `testdata/mediatype` on eleven shapes from 5 bytes to 256 KB: invalid bytes in the media type (12.5 for each byte beyond 65536), distinct uppercase keys (22), continuations of one value (20), and starred names in each form (`k*0=x` 89, `k*0*=x` 77, `k*=x` 70). It fails at 32 and at 64. `TestMIME386` (with `VEGO_386=1`; `AGENTS.md` says when) evaluates the requires with the sizes of 386 to hold at 16,776,958 bytes and not one more, and runs each shape there in a 4G scope: each returns, at most 38 bytes allocated for each byte. Programs: `handlers.JSONOnly` and the guide's `JSONOnly` verify; `UnboundedMediaType` and `JSONUnbounded`, which do not bound the header, are refused at the requires. The trial's `createTaskHandler` now stops at `ParseMediaType`'s requires, since `req.Header.Get` gives no bound, and then at `json.NewDecoder(req.Body)` without a limit.
- **A `Read` entry for `*bufio.Reader` that says it makes progress** is listed, not built: its progress is that of the reader it reads from, which needs a state and a fact of interface values that no entry has (see JSON, Not built yet, the in-place decoder over a reader). No function of the trial waits for it.
- **Not built:** a loop that pushes and then answers (`PushInLoopThenWrite`): the head's growth layer is part 1's, and the fact about what the loop made is lost across the write.

Built in step 3:

- **`valid(x)` inside a clause is paired through what it is made of** (`validPaired`, F2 of the review). The value `x` leads to and the steps of `invariantSteps`, their values and nil guards, are paired reads, and each step's clauses are read through a `frameEnv` of their own over the same framer, so their pairs fall under the quantifiers around them: `Forall i in [0, |out|) : valid(out[i])` compares, under `i`, the reads of `Note`'s clauses at `out[i]`. Compared as one boolean it would be the alignment a frame spares Z3 (the wrong version: `ByLength` refused).
- **A loop invariant is preserved by frame** (`framedLoop`). A conjunct at the end of the body is framed from a head where a conjunct of the same text was assumed: the loop's own, or, after a loop nested in the body whose invariant repeats it, that loop's. The latest two are tried by identity, which asks nothing, and one question is asked, from the latest whose reading went alike (F17 of the review: asking from each made a failing invariant of a nested loop that repeats it pay three limits, 4.0 M units at the base and 12.0 M; it was 8.0 M, and is 4.1 M since the split stands for the proof, F21). A state keeps the heads of the loops its path is inside or has left (`state.loops`, the head cloned once its invariants are assumed, which every path of the body continues: K for loops). Read in its own scope, a conjunct of the same text is the same formula over what its names denote there, so pairing the two readings is the frame lemma as for one clause; a clause of other text assumed there is no entry (the wrong version that takes any head: `BreakThenInner` verifies). The entry holds the head's whole environment: its values of variables, its memory, its snapshots, and its visited sets, all read through the head state.
- **A Forall over one variable whose domain changes is asked split.** For `Forall i in D : B(i)` with D read in the goal's state as D1 and in the head's as D0, the goal is `Forall i : D1(i) -> ((D0(i) And Unch(i)) Or B1(i))`, where `Unch(i)` is the pairs of B's reads at `i` under what both readings learned there, and D0 is the head reading's guards without the frame's own fact. Why it is true: on the path of the goal, `Forall i : D0(i) -> B0(i)` held at the head (K); for an `i` in D0 whose reads are unchanged, B1(i) = B0(i) by F; any other `i` in D1 satisfies B1(i) directly. So the split goal gives the goal. It is asked at the limit of an obligation: the trial's `GetTasksByDueDate` needs 2.6 M, and at 2 M it stops. When it is not proved, its answer stands for the conjunct's own proof, which is not asked, and the report says what it would have said, with the split's limit note (F21 of the review of step 5). The conjunct's goal, `Forall i : D1(i) -> B1(i)`, implies the split goal, so asking it after the split could prove only what Z3's heuristics find in the stronger goal and not in the weaker one, and it made a loop invariant that does not hold pay two limits: the split at 3 M units (`splitEffort`, as it was until F21) and the goal at 4 M. Skipping a proof can only report more, so this is sound; no program of the corpus changed its messages, and the trial's count is the same. The orchestrator's attacks, each refused: a domain that shrinks (`Shrink` verifies, what is left is as it was), one replaced by another of the same length (`Replace`), an element moved to another index (`Swap`), a map whose key set changes (`AddKey`), a body that reads the next element (`Sorted`: the last old element reads `ys[n]`, past the head's length, which the append changed, so it is proved again with the new one), an element that comes into the domain from the capacity, in memory at the head and unchanged but said nothing of (`Reslice`; the wrong version that drops D0 verifies it), and every element written (`WriteEach`, `WriteByCall`).
- **What a frame gathers under a quantifier is what O1 gathers, where O1 does.** Step 1 gathered what every layer and join keeps, in every function. No program of the corpus or the trial needed more, and neither the wrong version that gathered a layer's cells as kept whatever its targets nor the one that gathered only where O1 does could be observed: the questions they touch, a layer between the head of a loop and the end of its body (`WriteByCall`), run to their limit either way. So step 3 went back to O1's gathering, which adds nothing new to guard (F5 of the review).
- Programs (`frames/loops.go`): `Lengths`, `Notes` (`valid(out[i])`), `Shrink`, and `ByLength` (a mutex-guarded store and a loop under `valid(out[i])`, refused at the limit at the base) verify; the attacks above are refused. Wrong versions caught: the split without D0 (`Reslice`), `valid` compared as one boolean (`ByLength`), a head of any text (`BreakThenInner`), and frames off.
- Cost: of the 83 rows that changed, 49 fell and 32 rose, the most `defects.SortWrongWay`, 1.98 times (8.2 M to 16.2 M units), which paid a split at the default limit for each of two loop invariants that do not hold before each was proved as before; the rest under 1.2 times. Since the split stands for the proof (F21 of the review of step 5), a loop invariant that does not hold pays one limit, as at the base: `SortWrongWay` is 8.2 M units again, `Swap`, `WriteEach`, `WriteByCall`, and `BreakThenInner` 4.0 M to 4.3 M each from 7.0 M to 7.3 M, and the corpus 558.0 M units from 576.4 M, with no row higher. A frame that does not hold at a boundary or of `valid(x)` (`Scribble`, `PassTags`, the guide's `DropThenAnswer`, each about 7 M) still pays its limit before the clause is proved as before: the frame's question is the frame lemma, which the clause's goal does not imply.
- **Not built:** a nested loop whose inner body appends under the outer loop's quantified invariant, as the trial's `GetTasksByTag` was written (`for _, taskTag := range task.Tags { ... append ...; break }`): the split from the inner head is not proved at 40 M units. The rewrite with `slices.Contains(task.Tags, tag)` verifies. A loop invariant `valid(ts)` beside a quantified invariant over the result made the split run to its limit; with frames from entry through the head, `valid(ts)` needs no loop invariant, and the trial drops it.
- Programs (`frames/`): `Answer`, `AnswerTwice`, `Size`, `Count` (a helper handed a map of its own), `ServeTwice` (a counter beside the map, written after the writes through `w`), `Longest`, `Tagged` (`valid(st)` at a loop's head), `AnswerAll` and `AnswerAllUnsaid` (a loop that writes through `w`, with and without `valid(s)`), and `Valid` (`valid(s')` in an `Ensures`) verify; at the base `AnswerTwice`, `Size`, `Count`, `AnswerAllUnsaid`, and `Valid` were refused at the limit. Refused: `Scribble` (a tag of a task written before the write through `w`), `Rewrite` (a loop that writes every task), `PassTags` (a helper handed a task's tags), `AddBad` (a key the invariant does not allow added), `Guarded` (F1 of the review), `AfterCall` (a store after the call whose entry restored it), `HandedBreak`, and `TwoEnds`. Wrong versions caught: the frame off (five programs refused), the guard of an assumption ignored (`Guarded`), the live memory map kept in place of a copy (`AfterCall`), the domain's pairs not quantified over its variable (`AddBad`), and `Path` not paired (every defect verifies). The entries of every end kept at a join of several ends: `TwoEnds` verifies (F9 of the review), since where no end writes what the clause reads the joined memory is the terms of the end and the identity shortcut holds of an entry made after that end's own test. Not observable, and why: the value not compared (every read is paired, so equal reads give an equal clause whatever the value), and the entry's guard not required when the identity shortcut and `len(guard) == 0` stand (an entry is made only under no condition).

### Trusted contracts for unproved code

Decision: a trusted contract covers the function's own code only. A value that can hold an error, an interface, a function, a channel, or an `unsafe.Pointer` (since package-level variables) brings code that the author of the contract has not seen, so it is passed to a trusted function only when the contract lists the parameter under `runs`, which makes the wider claim explicit and puts it in the trust report; an error of known origin is exempt. An outside audit produced a wrong proof with an honest-looking `modifies: []` entry for a function that called `err.Error()`. A parameter without a name cannot be listed, so such a function needs a wrapper.

Built: the trust file and its checks, the rule that a call needs a contract, `keeps` for functions that keep what they are given (since F15), contracts for functions and methods, contracts for methods of interfaces and function types of other modules (see Interfaces, second part), the shipped contracts with their tests, the shipped contracts for locking `sync.Mutex` and `sync.RWMutex` (see State that a value owns), the trust report (`laas -vego.trust`), `vouches`, and error variables named in trusted formulas by the path of their package. Not built yet: `http.Get` and the methods of `http.Client` that return a response (decided not to ship with state of interface values, O4): their `vouches` on the result would claim that every function value and interface value a `*http.Response` holds, `Body` with its `Read` and `Close`, `Request.Body`, `Request.GetBody`, and the request's context, meets its contract, which needs the reading of `net/http`'s source that `os.Remove` needed of `syscall`, and an entry `err = nil -> resp != nil And resp.Body != nil And ~closed(resp.Body) And fresh(resp.Body)`; shipped contracts for methods of other concrete types (the test of shipped contracts calls functions, the methods of the mutexes, of `*log.Logger`, and of the `bufio` types, each by a harness of its own, and values of the interface and function types that have entries); shipped contracts that vouch for the errors of more functions, which need the same reading of source as `os.Remove` did; checking a trusted `modifies` against the function's source, and running the shipped-contract tests on several Go versions and systems: the bounds of `filepath.Clean`, `Dir`, and `Join` rest on reading the code of Windows and Plan 9, and every `os` entry on reading the code of the systems the tests do not run on. Error variables of other packages, such as `io.EOF`, need no entry: they are read from the source of their package.

Contracts of verified functions cross package boundaries as analysis facts, together with the predicates of their package; the predicates of different packages are kept apart by a prefix.

Formulas in the file have to be quoted, because YAML gives its own meaning to `: `, `|`, `!`, `[`, and `#`.

Verified code has to call code that cannot be verified: the standard library, third-party modules, and functions of the project itself that use unsupported Go. A trusted contract states what such a function requires and ensures. Callers must still prove the `Requires`; the `Ensures` is assumed rather than proved.

Decisions:

- **One file, no in-source trust.** Contracts for unproved code live in `vego-trusted.yaml` next to `go.mod`. There is no in-source `Trusted` marker and no `Assume`, so every assumption is a diff in one conspicuous file. A function with both annotations and an entry is an error.
- **Entry format.** The key is the function's full name as Go prints it (`sort.Ints`, `(*bytes.Buffer).Len`). Fields: `reason` (mandatory free text saying why the contract is believed), `requires`, `ensures`, `modifies` (what the function may write, in the form of a `Modifies` clause; anything unlisted is assumed untouched), `runs` (parameters whose code the function may run), `vouches` (error results whose errors the author vouches for), `calls` (contracts for function parameters it calls; see Callbacks handed to trusted functions), `formats` (the values it formats as fmt does and its format; see Variadic calls and formatting), and, for a method of an interface, `receiver` (see State that a value owns). Formulas use the annotation language. Parameter names come from the real signature; there is no override.
- **Result names.** Named results keep their names. A trusted function may have unnamed results, since its signature is not ours to change: a single one is `result`; several are `result1`, `result2`, ...
- **No contract, no call.** Verified code that calls a function with neither annotations nor an entry is an error, never silent trust. When in doubt, vego takes the loudest path: agents loop on its output, so anything quiet gets exploited.
- **Checks on the file.** The function exists, every name is a parameter or result of the right type or an error variable that exists, whose package is in the build and that the caller may rely on, `modifies` is present, even if empty, and names memory a caller can see, a primed path in an `ensures` reads memory of a kind that `modifies` lists, every name under `vouches` is a result of type `error` listed once, and no contract contradicts itself.
- **Checks at each call.** After the promises of a trusted contract are assumed, a quick attempt is made to prove a contradiction; if it succeeds on a path that was not contradictory before, the call is reported. The attempt is bounded, so a contradiction that is hard to find gets through.
- **Recursion through a trusted function of the same package is found.** Its body is there to read, and since F17 the call graph walks it as it walks any body of the package: a function it names or calls is an edge, a call through an interface value or a function value reaches what the types allow, and a call into another package reaches what it is handed (see The call graph, under Function values). What is left to the assumption about trusted code is what code of another package keeps and runs later, after the call that handed it over has returned (see What a proof assumes in the guide).
- **What a trusted function is trusted with besides its contract.** It returns without panicking when its `requires` hold, which includes not calling back into its verified caller by way of another package, writes nothing a caller can see beyond its `modifies`, and keeps no pointer or slice it was given. Nothing is assumed about where a pointer it returns points.
- **`runs` also says when the code runs.** A function that keeps code it was given under `runs` and has another trusted function run it later puts the claim on an entry that received nothing and shows nothing in the report. So `runs` claimed, for every parameter whatever code it holds, that the function runs that code only before it returns and only on the goroutine that called it, keeps no value that can hold code, and calls a function value only with arguments that meet the `Requires` of its type; since F15 (2026-11-20) the third part reads "holds no value that can hold code anywhere it was not held when the call began, unless `keeps` lists the parameter", and the next decision says what `keeps` claims. A function that registers a callback cannot honestly list it. The hole is older than function values: at c7c0e2d a trusted `Keep(e error)` with `runs: [e]` could keep an error that a trusted `Flush()` with `modifies: []` then formatted, and the proof of a verified caller of both relied on `Flush` writing nothing. `Keep`'s entry was true as `runs` was worded then, and the false claim sat on `Flush`, which received nothing and shows nothing in the trust report. It was closed by the wording, which makes `Keep`'s entry the false one; `funcvalues/keep.go` holds the program. vego cannot check the claim.
- **`keeps` says where a function keeps code, and the later calls carry the claim** (F15, built on 2026-11-20, decided by the orchestrator with the reviewer). An entry maps a parameter to its destinations, results or other parameters, as in `keeps: {r: [result]}` and `keeps: {h: [reg]}` (`keeps.go`). The claim is about what the function does, for whatever code the parameter holds and, as for `runs`, only for values that meet their contracts where the type has them: while the call lasts it runs none of it unless `runs` lists the parameter too; when it returns it holds the code only in memory that the destinations reach; and it has started or registered nothing that runs the code later (no goroutine, timer, finalizer or cleanup, callback given to another package such as `context.AfterFunc`, or package-level variable). That later runs happen only inside checked calls is its consequence, not part of the claim (F10 of the review of step 1): the first wording spoke of what callers do later, which an author cannot check against the function's source, and which a copy of a destination made the entry of `Wrap` false through nothing `Wrap` did. `runs` is reworded to match: a function under `runs` holds no value that can hold code anywhere it was not held when the call began, unless `keeps` lists it, which keeps `(*log.Logger).Printf` with `runs: [l]` true. Why this is enough: code that runs the kept value later holds a reference to it, reached through a destination or a copy of one, and every such value has a type that `holdsCode` counts, so a trusted function handed one must list it under `runs` (`checkHandedOver`, by type), whose claim then covers the run, its frame, and its arguments; a call through a value always counts as one. Where the kept parameter can hold code that verified code made (`holdsCallable`), every destination must too, or the entry is a defect: that, not the types alone, keeps stratification seeing the later run for `any`, `error`, and interfaces whose contracts speak of a state. And with this, every fully contracted interface counts in stratification, states or not (F1 of the design review), since `io.NopCloser` puts a verified reader into an `io.ReadCloser`; `ifacestate.MakeSink` is refused for it, the one program of the corpus that changed. Defects of an entry: a name that is not a parameter or holds no code; no destination (`keeps: {e: []}`, the only thing `Keep(e)` could write); a destination that is not a result or another parameter, the parameter itself, holds no code, cannot hold callable code where the parameter can, is named twice, or is a parameter under which `modifies` lists nothing that can hold the kept code, since keeping writes such memory (F9 of the review of step 1: `modifies: [r.Count]` was accepted); a parameter under `calls`, since a literal handed over may assign the variables of its caller, or under `formats`; `keeps` in a type entry. A function literal, function, or method value of a type without a contract is refused at a parameter under `keeps` (`checkNotHandable`), and a trusted function with `keeps` cannot be made into a value. A destination parameter holds code, so the entry lists it under `runs` too, which makes the function count as calling through values. The trust report prints `keeps code held by: r in result`. Nothing is assumed at the later call that its own entry does not say: the kept code writes within what the wrapper hands it, since a value verified code made writes only within its type's frame and nothing behind its receiver, and one from elsewhere is covered by the type entry. `vouches` on a result or parameter that holds kept values claims they meet their contracts whenever the values the function was given meet theirs, which every value verified code passes does. The guide says what to read in the source before writing an entry, and shows `NewPrefetcher`, which keeps `r` only in its result and reads it on a goroutine, as an entry that looks true and is not. The corpus is `keeps/` and `keepslib/`: a registry that keeps handlers in itself, a box that keeps a reader, the Keep/Flush hole still refused, each defect, a handler and a reader that call back into what runs them, refused by stratification, and a reader kept in a closer that drains itself (`Drains`), which is refused by the destination check without the change to stratification and by stratification with it.
- **Shipped contracts.** Standard-library contracts are YAML files in this repository, one per package, embedded in the binary. A project entry replaces a shipped one. They state documented behaviour only, written from the documentation of the oldest supported Go version; the Go 1 compatibility promise then keeps them true on every later version, so there are no per-version variants. Each is tested against the real function on random inputs, on every supported toolchain once CI exists.
- **Shipped entries of F19** (2026-11-20, item 1 of the list until then). 126 new entries and four strengthened (`strings.Index` and `Contains`, `path.Join` and `filepath.Join`), in the files of `trusted/std`: `strings` (`IndexByte`, `LastIndexByte`, `LastIndex`, `Cut`, `TrimPrefix`, `TrimSuffix`, `TrimLeft`, `TrimRight`, `Split`, `SplitAfter`, `Fields`, `Join`, `Replace`, `ReplaceAll`, `EqualFold`, `Count`), the same shapes of `bytes` (with `Equal`, and `within` for the subslices it returns, documented for `Cut` and `TrimSpace`, read from source for `TrimPrefix` and `TrimSuffix`), `unicode/utf8` (`DecodeRuneInString`, `DecodeRune`, `AppendRune`, `RuneLen`, `ValidString`), `unicode` (`IsSpace`, `IsDigit`, `IsLetter`, `IsUpper`, `IsLower`, on ASCII and on values that are no character), `strconv` (`ParseInt`, `ParseUint`, `ParseBool`, `FormatInt`, `AppendInt`, `Quote`, `Unquote`), `sort.Strings`, sixteen functions of `math` (that they return and write nothing, all that is true of opaque floats), the value functions and methods of `time`, `os` (`Open`, `Create`, `Read`, `Write`, and `Close` of `*os.File`, `WriteFile`, `MkdirAll`, `Getenv`, `LookupEnv`, `Getwd`), `path` (`Clean`, `Dir`, `Base`, `Ext`, `IsAbs`, `Split`), `path/filepath` (`Clean`, `Dir`, `Base`, `Ext`, `Rel`, `Split`), `regexp` (`Compile`, `MatchString`, `Match`, `FindString`, `FindStringSubmatch`), and `net/http` (`PathValue`, `Header.Get` and `Set`, `NewServeMux`). Decisions, each by the orchestrator:
  - **No entry ships without a harness that tests it against the real function**, after the trial's `strconv.Unquote` with `|result1| <= |s|` was false. Each reason quotes the documentation of Go 1.26.6 (the oldest Go `laas` builds with, compared with 1.27.3) and says what the entry does not say.
  - **Documented behaviour only, and where the useful and the documented contract differ, the documented one.** Clauses read from source are a category of their own, on the terms of a vouch (the reading named in the reason and guarded by a test where one can be): `fresh` and `within` of results, the `requires` that keep int arithmetic and allocations in bounds on a 32-bit target (where `MaxAlloc` is larger than `MaxInt`, so every requires bounds both, and a buffer that grows by `append` is given twice its size and room for the rounding of `growslice`, which panics past `maxAlloc` after it rounds), the claims under `vouches`, `runs`, and `asserts`, and the bound `|result| <= |path| + 2` of `filepath.Clean` and `Dir`, where the documented "shortest" is false on Windows (`postClean` prepends `.\`); the reviewer read `path_windows.go`, `path_nonwindows.go`, `path_unix.go`, and `path_plan9.go` independently and agreed, and the harness runs the Unix code only.
  - **A length is an obligation wherever the logic has a term for it** (F1 and the decision on `Join`): `strings.Join`, `bytes.Join`, `path.Join`, and `filepath.Join` require `Forall i in [0, |elems|) : |elems| * (|elems[i]| + |sep| + 1) <= MaxInt` and the same within `MaxAlloc`, which bounds the total without a sum, and ensure `Exists i in [0, |elems|) : |result| <= |elems| * (|elems[i]| + |sep|)`, so that a caller with a bound on every element gets one on the result. "What a proof assumes" lost the elements of the two path joins; that narrowing refused `formatting.JoinsWrong` until it bounded its argument (and `JoinsAnything` holds the refusal). The one place the old assumption remains is formatting a `[]string`, or any slice whose elements hold strings or slices: the formatting bound is one linear sum over the operands (`fmtsize.go`), and a bound by the elements would make it a quantified obligation, which is not a small change.
  - **The harness itself is checked** (the orchestrator's demand, with F3, F4, F5, F11, F12, and F14 of the review). The evaluator (`evaluator_test.go`) ranges each quantifier over exactly the integers its guards allow and panics on one it cannot bound, divides as Go does, holds integers past int64, and fails a sample that reads outside a sequence (past the end of a substring the logic has the parent's byte, so a wrong interval would pass); `TestEvaluator` holds it against Go. Every argument a harness hands over is snapshotted with a print of all the memory it reaches, unexported fields included, and compared after the call unless `modifies` lists it (a parameter under `runs` with the code it holds opaque, and what a callback writes taken out); `fresh` and `within` are decided from the real memory, against everything the arguments, the other results, and every earlier result reach; and every condition of an ensures (of an implication, a conjunction of them, both sides of an iff) must hold in some sample, pooled over the implementations of a type entry. For every entry, every condition of every ensures is met by some sample, and the unchanged check covers what its modifies leaves out; stronger and false variants were made by hand, and shown to fail, for the entries and clauses the reviews name, which no test repeats (entries with only a `modifies`, as those of `math`, have no clause to make stronger).
  - **Shipped entries that say less than their function does**, each waiting for the logic or for a reading the documentation does not give: `strings.Index`, `LastIndex`, `Cut`, `Contains`, and their `bytes` forms do not say that the instance is the first or last, nor that `-1` or false means none, which needs a Forall over an Exists that every call would carry (quantifier alternation, measured as too costly to put in every call); `sort.Strings` does not say the order (the logic has no order of strings) nor that each element is there as often as before (a count); the `unicode` predicates say what they report on ASCII only (a model of Unicode's tables); `EqualFold` only that equal strings fold equal (case folding); `strconv.Unquote` gives no length (the documentation gives none; the 3x bound read from source stays a project entry in the trial); `strconv.Quote` bounds its result by `10 * |s| + 2`, the longest escape the documentation allows, where 1.26.6 writes at most `4 * |s| + 2`; the `math` functions say only that they return (floats are opaque); `Header.Get` and `Set` not which key (canonical forms of keys); the `os` functions nothing of files (the world is not modelled); since F21, the generic entries say only what can be read at every type argument they serve: `slices.Clone`, `Grow`, `Clip`, `Delete`, `DeleteFunc`, `Insert`, `Reverse`, and `Compact` nothing of the elements (equality of structs, or clauses limited to some type arguments), and `Delete`, `DeleteFunc`, and `Compact` not that they zero what they drop (the zero value of a type parameter); `slices.Sort` not the order and `cmp.Compare` not which of -1 and 1 (strings have no order); `ContainsFunc` and `IndexFunc` not which element (applications of a struct or string argument); the `maps` entries nothing of what a map holds (a quantifier over string or unbounded keys); `maps.Clone` not that a nil map gives nil, which only the source says.
  - **Not shipped, each with what it waits for.** `os.ReadFile` and `os.ReadDir`: `ReadFile("/dev/zero")` grows until the program fails, and a sparse file larger than `MaxAlloc` makes its first `make` panic; `ReadDir` collects every entry of a directory, past `MaxAlloc` on a 32-bit target. It is the `ReadString` decision: an assumption about what the input contains is not made for a shipped entry. They wait for an entry that may end the program (with item 8, functions that never return), and `ReadDir` and `os.Stat` also for entries of the methods of `io/fs.DirEntry` and `io/fs.FileInfo`. The guide shows a bounded loop of `Read` and a project entry for `ReadFile` whose claim about the files is the project's, which `TestReadFileEntry` checks against the real function on regular files. `flag.Parse`: `CommandLine` is `ExitOnError`, so a bad flag or `-h` ends the program (item 8), and it runs `flag.Usage` and the `Set` of every `Value` registered before, code kept in package-level variables (item 3) and run later (item 11). `sort.SearchStrings`: sortedness of strings cannot be required (string order), and for an unsorted slice the documentation says nothing. `bytes.Split` and `Fields`: their elements lie in `s`, which `within` under a quantifier cannot say. The methods of `bytes.Buffer` and `strings.Builder` that grow: shipped since F26, bounded by the length of the buffer read from its fields (Buffers and builders). `(*regexp.Regexp).FindStringIndex`: its result is called `loc` in Go 1.26 and `m` in Go 1.27, and an entry names a result by its name; it waits for an entry that names a result by position, or for the oldest supported Go to be 1.27. `regexp.MustCompile`: it panics on a pattern that does not compile, which only a `requires` that the pattern compiles could rule out. `http.Error` and `(net/http.ResponseWriter).Header` ship since F28 (Footprints, The writer of a handler). Not shipped since then, each listed there: `Header.Add` and `Header.Values`, `mime.ParseMediaType`, the cookie functions, `http.Redirect`, and the readers of a request's body.
  - **`(*os.File).Close` has no state.** A second `Close` returns an error and does not panic, and so do `Read` and `Write` of a closed file, so no `requires` needs `~closed`; a state would only let an ensures say that a second `Close` fails.
  - **`*os.File` carries no code** since F7 of the review (see Two types are counted by what their package lets them hold), so its entries need no `runs`, `Write` has `modifies: []` (the harness compares the file field by field after the call), and an `*os.File` converts to `io.Writer` and `io.Reader`: `fmt.Fprintf(f, ...)` after `os.Create` verifies, and so do `os.Stdout` and `os.Stderr` where nothing in the build assigns them. `bufio.NewWriter(f)` stays refused, as recorded under Shipped wrappers of bufio.
  - **Calls that wait on the system are assumed to return**, a new bullet of "What a proof assumes" beside the one for `(io.Reader).Read`: opening a FIFO, reading a pipe or a terminal, writing a pipe nobody reads, and a file system that does not answer; and a write to standard output or standard error through a broken pipe ends the program with `SIGPIPE`.
  - Messages changed with it: the assignment that leaves an error variable's origin unknown is named (F8), the argument to list when a write goes through a result the callee places `within` it (F13), and the `Modifies` path stops before a field the package cannot name (F17).
  - The corpus is `stdentries/` (text, values, files, web, paths: each group verifying and refused), `guide/files.go`, `heldcode/`, `unsafecycle/`, and `gospellings/`.
- **Generic functions under entries** (F21, 2026-11-20, decided by the orchestrator with the reviewer; `generics.go`). A call of a generic function is checked against a contract of its own instantiation. `calleeAt` makes, for the call, a function object with the instantiated signature (`types.NewFunc`), cached per package by the generic function and its type arguments under `types.Identical`, and `trustedContract` builds the contract of that object from the entry written under the generic name, so every check of an entry runs at each instantiation: resolution of every clause, `modifies` and primed paths, `runs`, `calls`, `keeps`, `asserts`, `vouches`, the contradiction check, the always-fails check, the order rule over the instantiated signature, recursion, and the cost table. A contract usable at one instantiation is never taken as usable at another. Nothing new is translated to SMT-LIB. Decisions:
  - **One entry for every type argument** (A, not B). A clause means at an instantiation what it means of its type arguments; one that has no meaning there (a struct compared as a whole, an order of strings, anything of a float, a pointer to a value of size zero compared with another) refuses the call there, and is never dropped. The message names the clause and the type arguments and says what to call instead: the function of the same name with `Func` where it has an entry, a loop for a function of a slice or a map, otherwise to write out what it does. A defect of the entry at every type argument (a name that is nothing, a form the resolver refuses, `old` in a requires, a field of a type parameter) says to fix the entry instead; defects are marked where they are raised (`typeDefect`, `entryDefect`, `logic.FormError` and `logic.SortError`), not by their text. Not built (B): a clause limited to some type arguments, which would give `Sort` and `Compare` their order on integers and the functions of any element type their element claims on integers and strings without refusing structs.
  - **Checked once for the entry**: a clause, a `modifies` path, or a `calls` requires that names a field, a state, or the address of a value whose type in the generic signature is a type parameter is a defect (`throughTypeParam`, a state only for a name that some type declares as one, so that an application is read as one).
  - **Type arguments refused at the call**, each with its own message (`typeArgProblem`): one that is not strictly comparable where the constraint is `comparable`; one that can hold code (an entry would have to say what it runs, keeps, and vouches for at those instantiations only); one that vego does not model; one that holds a state (these functions copy their elements); and one whose values reach a method, through `T`, `*T`, or what they hold at any depth, unless the entry claims under `asserts`, keyed by the type parameter, that the function runs none. Generic code reaches those methods through its constraint, through `any(x).(I)`, and by reflection, so a type argument is code handed to the call: without the claim, the call graph gives the call the methods of the package that such a value reaches (`handedCode`), and a cycle through them is reported. The claim, `asserts: {E: []}` and only the empty list: the function runs no method of a value of type `E` or `*E`, or of anything such a value holds, directly or through what it calls (`fmt` and `reflect` included), neither while the call lasts nor later through anything it returns, keeps, or registers. It rests on reading the source, and is in What a proof assumes.
  - **A generic function of the project with an entry** is called like one of another package: the call graph walks its body as that of any trusted function of the package, and a cycle back into the caller is reported with `Back is generic and cannot be verified yet, so break the cycle`. A generic function with annotations stays refused where it is declared, and a call of one says that generic functions cannot be verified yet. A call inside generic code whose type arguments mention its own type parameters gets no instantiation.
  - **Instance identity** (F2, F4, F14 of the reviews). An object made by `types.NewFunc` is its own `Origin`, so `originOf` maps an instance to its generic function, and maps keyed by functions of the package are read through `byFunc`, which panics on an instance. A parameter whose type mentions no type parameter is one object in the generic signature and every instantiation, so `runs`, `calls`, `keeps`, and what `vouches` says of writes are `byParam` maps of one contract, read only through `get`, which panics on a parameter of a type-parameter type: no contract is built of a generic function, and `trustedContract` panics on one, so such a read is a site that bound a call by the generic signature.
  - **Callbacks of a type parameter's type** run through `calls` unchanged at the instantiated type, with struct and string parameters. Applications keep their rule (one integer argument), so an entry that applies `f` is refused at a type argument that is not an integer; the shipped entries apply nothing.
  - **`elemsize(A)`**, a new reserved word: the size in bytes of an element of the slice `A` on the target, 0 for values of size zero. The allocation bounds of `Clone`, `Grow`, and `Insert` use it, with `|s| + n <= MaxInt` beside it, which is the bound for elements of size zero.
  - **Shipped**, each reason quoting Go 1.26.6 and carrying the claim for every type parameter: `slices.Contains`, `Index`, `Equal` (equality, refused at structs, floats, and pointers to values of size zero), `ContainsFunc`, `IndexFunc`, `Clone`, `Grow`, `Clip`, `Delete`, `DeleteFunc`, `Insert`, `Compact` (lengths, capacities, nilness, `fresh` and `within`), `Reverse` and `Sort` (what they modify only), `BinarySearch`, `Max`, `Min` (order, refused at strings and floats), `maps.Clone`, `Copy`, `DeleteFunc` (counts), and `cmp.Compare` (range and equality, refused at floats). The documentation of `slices.Grow` says the result keeps the nilness of `s`, which is false for a nil `s` and an `n` above 0, so the entry says only what is true. The harness (`shipped_generic_test.go`) runs every generic entry at one list of classes, wherever the constraint admits them: int, uint8, a named int with methods, bool, string, a pointer to a struct and to a struct of size zero, a struct and a struct of size zero, float64, and a named slice or map type, with keys of four kinds for maps. Each class has a harness or the refusal the entry must draw, and the methods of the types with them fail the test when called.
  - **Not shipped**: `slices.SortFunc` and `BinarySearchFunc` (see Callbacks handed to trusted functions, A requires of the entry on the callback's result), `maps.Keys` and `Values` (iterators, item 10).
  - **Cost.** `go test ./vego/...` took 140.5 s at 5e65ae2 and 146.4 s at the end of F21, each run alone on the machine of Speed; the cost table gained 200 rows, 845 problems, 27.9 M units, and 34 problems left open, and no row that was there changed.
- **Not shipped.** Contracts for third-party modules, which carry no such promise.
- **Trust report.** `laas -vego.trust ./...` prints the project entries, the shipped entries in use, and unused entries.
- **`vouches` claims what verified code needs of an error, and no more.** An entry lists error results whose errors verified code may format, wrap, search with `errors.Is`, compare with any error, and hand to another trusted function. The claim, for every non-nil error returned there, every error in its tree, and every value these hold: every method returns without panicking and writes nothing a caller can see, whatever it is passed (so an `Is` method calls no method of its target); `==` on these errors cannot panic and is identity, so `errors.Is` answers the same for errors, and for targets, that `==` reports equal; and what `errors.Is` reports does not change after the call. A two-tier design, in which a vouched error would not be exempt from `runs`, was considered and dropped: it doubled the origin machinery for one rare use. The claim is stated in the guide in words to check against source, and in "What a proof assumes".
- **Names in a trusted contract are paths.** A trusted contract means the same in every package that calls it, so its names cannot depend on what the declaring package imports, which changes between Go versions and systems (`strconv` imports a package named `strconv`). A bare name is an error variable of the declaring package; `io.EOF` names one by a path that is an identifier; any other path is quoted, `"io/fs".ErrNotExist`, `"example.com".ErrRoot`, which parses as a field of a string literal (`/` is division). A quoted identifier is refused, so each name has one spelling, and so is a quoted path outside a trusted contract. The package must be in the build of the caller.
- **A shipped vouch may rest on reading source.** Documentation rarely says which types the errors of a function have, and what their methods do. `strconv.Atoi`'s claim follows from its documentation (a `*NumError` whose `Err` is one of two sentinels); `os.Remove`'s rests on its documented `*PathError` and on reading what package `syscall` puts in it. `shipped_test.go` walks the tree of every vouched error the harnesses produce and requires each dynamic type to be in a list of types whose methods were read, so a new type in a later Go fails the test until someone reads it.
- **Shipped wrappers of bufio** (F15). `bufio.NewScanner` with `Scan`, `Text`, and `Err`; `bufio.NewReader` with `ReadByte`; `bufio.NewWriter` with `Write`, `WriteByte`, and `Flush` (`trusted/std/bufio.yaml`). Each type has the shipped state `ready` (F4 of the design review): for a Scanner, made by `NewScanner` with a reader that is not nil and neither `Split` nor `Buffer` called since; for a Reader and a Writer, the last of the constructor, the constructor with a size, and `Reset` that set its reader or writer set one that is not nil, `Reset` to itself setting nothing. The zero values never return or panic, as do a Reader reset to nil and a Scanner with a split function that panics, so no entry with only `x != nil` would be true. The methods require `ready`, and those with `modifies [x]` restate it. `Text` ensures `|result| <= 65536`, the bound of a ready Scanner's buffer, which lets a line be formatted. `NewReader` and `NewWriter` return the argument's own Reader or Writer when it is large enough (F2), so they claim no `fresh`; that the reused one is ready follows from the entry of `(io.Reader).Read` or `(io.Writer).Write`, which covers it and every reader or writer it reaches, since one that reads or writes through nil panics. So `bw := bufio.NewWriter(w)` followed by a write is refused: the write is to memory that may have existed on entry, which an unverified caller holding that Writer would see. The message names the function that returned it and says to take it as a parameter (`returnedBy`, decided by the orchestrator); local construction waits for footprints, or for dynamic types, since the result is new exactly when the argument is not already a large enough Writer. The tests (`wrappers_test.go`) run each type by sequences over kept readers and writers: the constructors run nothing, are called with an argument of their own type so that a claim of `fresh` fails (it does, on `NewReader`), `Reset`, `Split`, and `Buffer` are called as code that is not verified may, `Text` is checked at a final token of 65536 and 65537 bytes (a bound of 65535 fails), what `Write` was given stays as it was after later calls, and every Read or Write of a kept value must come during a method, on its goroutine, with a slice that is not empty for Read, and never through `WriteString`, `ReadFrom`, or `WriteTo`. The bufio entries cost the corpus little but for the contradiction checks after `NewScanner`, which stop at the limit as those after a call returning a struct of many sequences do (`buffered.CountLines`, 80 problems, 2 s). The guide's former project entry for `Scan`, which claimed the split function from what callers pass, is replaced by the shipped one; `guide.CountLines` now requires `ready(s)`.
- **Shipped wrappers of io** (F15). `io.NopCloser`, `io.LimitReader`, `io.TeeReader`, `io.MultiReader`, and `io.MultiWriter` keep what they are given in their result and vouch for it: its `Read`, `Write`, or `Close` meets the entry of its interface whenever what it keeps meets theirs. Each requires its arguments, or every element of the slice, not nil, since a nil reader or writer panics at the first call. `NopCloser` ensures `fresh(result)` and `~closed(result)`: its result is a new value whose `Close` does nothing, so closing it closes nothing of `r`; two `NopCloser`s of one reader are `==` in Go, which has no identity for a struct value, and vego gives each conversion its own reference, as for any struct converted to an interface. `MultiReader` and `MultiWriter` copy the slice; the harness puts values that fail when called into a spread slice after the call (F7 (d)), and it fails if the call is moved before. None claims what its result's `WriteTo`, `ReadFrom`, or `WriteString` would do: verified code calls only the methods of the interfaces. Tested by sequences (`checkIOEntries`) over kept readers and writers and over wrappers made before, with each call checked against the entry of `Read`, `Write`, or `Close` and the kept values run only during a call, only through `Read` and `Write`, and never closed (`keptReader` has a `Close` that fails the test). `~closed` replaced by `closed` fails it. Stratification counts `io.ReadCloser` since F1, so a verified reader whose `Read` hands a `NopCloser` of its own type to a trusted function that runs it is refused (`iowrap.drain`). The corpus is `iowrap/`. Since the second commit of step 4, `MultiReader` and `MultiWriter` also say `fresh(result, readers)` and `fresh(result, writers)`. The check of the entry itself does not read that clause as a claim about the footprint, since the slice's length is unknown there: it reads it as saying only that what the result holds is new. Nothing false follows, because the finite form is read where a call writes each argument out, and the check for a contradiction after that call takes it, so a contradictory entry is reported there (`keepslib.Joined`, `iowrap.JoinsContradiction`); a call that hands the slice whole gets the weaker reading, and the write through what it returns is refused (see Footprints, D1 and the entries).
- **Errors that pass through a wrapper.** The error of `Err` may be one the reader returned, so the shipped entries vouch for nothing and `fmt.Errorf("scan: %w", sc.Err())` is refused (`scanerrs`, a module with no trust file). The guide shows the way that works today: a project entry for `(io.Reader).Read` that vouches for the errors of the readers callers pass, and one for `(*bufio.Scanner).Err` that vouches for what it returns, naming bufio's errors and resting on the first.
- **An `unsafe.Pointer` handed to another package reaches what an `any` reaches** (F19, a soundness repair of the recursion rule, approved by the orchestrator, from F16 of the review). `codeTargets` gave nothing for a raw `unsafe.Pointer`, while `holdsCode` counted it as anything, so a cycle through one was not found: a trusted `t` that handed `lib.Run` the address of a package-level `func()` holding a verified `F` let `F` verify, and `F` never returned (stack overflow). The memory has one type on both sides, so the assumption that unverified code respects the type system does not cover it. Since F19 it reaches what a value that can hold anything reaches (`anythingTargets`: every exported method and every function value the package lets out), and `writingCode` names it, so an entry with `formats` that runs a parameter holding one is a defect. The two types `heldAs` exempts are walked as it says before. Corpus: `unsafecycle` (the reviewer's program, the cycle of `F` reported; an unexported verified function that hands over the address of plain data still verifies; a `formats` entry refused). A `uintptr` carries no code, so a round trip through one, `lib.RunAt(uintptr(unsafe.Pointer(&hook)))` converted back in `lib`, hides the cycle; that conversion breaks the rules of package `unsafe` (`go vet` reports it), and "What a proof assumes" now says that code that is not verified uses `unsafe.Pointer` only in the patterns its documentation allows (F18 of the review). It cost the corpus one program: `unsafeuse.CallListed`, exported and handing a holder of an `unsafe.Pointer` to a function that runs what it holds, is now reported as a call that may not end, which it may; no cost of `testdata/costs.txt` and nothing of the guide changed.
- **Two types are counted by what their package lets them hold** (F19, decided by the orchestrator, from the review of its design, F7). An `unsafe.Pointer` counts as anything at all, so `*os.File`, whose only one is the `v` of `sync/atomic.Pointer[os.dirInfo]` (and on Windows also `runtime.Pinner`'s `refs`), counted as carrying code: every entry of its methods had to list `runs: [f]`, which made each a function that calls through values, and every conversion of an `*os.File` to `io.Writer` was refused (`fmt.Fprintf(f, ...)` after `os.Create`). `heldAs` (`calls.go`) walks `sync/atomic.Pointer[T]` as a `*T`, since its one field is unexported and package `sync/atomic` only loads, stores, swaps, and compares it as a `*T`, so anything else in it needs `unsafe`, which code that is not verified is assumed not to use to give memory another type; and `runtime.Pinner` as holding nothing, since it holds the pointers it pins and nothing reads them as values. They are matched by package path and name, not by shape; `atomic.Value` and the other types of `sync/atomic` are walked as declared. Every rule that asks whether a value can carry code calls it first: `holdsCode`, `heldCode`, `foreignCode`, `holdsCallable`, `callableIn`, `holdsContractedValue`, `dataType`, `codeless`, `codeTargets`, `concreteMethods`, `staticTargets`, and `noteEntriesIn` (`plainReason` and `flat` ask whether fmt follows a pointer and whether a value holds a reference, which is not about code). The reviewer walked `*os.File` on every GOOS and GOARCH of Go 1.26.6 and 1.27.3 and found nothing else. `TestHeldAs` holds the six walkers that answer yes or no to an atomic pointer of a plain struct, of a struct with a function, and of one with an `unsafe.Pointer`, `atomic.Value`, `runtime.Pinner`, and `*os.File`; it fails with `atomic.Pointer` counted as holding nothing and with `Pinner` not matched. `heldcode` hands a trusted function a struct with an `atomic.Pointer` of a plain struct, which verifies, and one of a struct with a function, which is refused. A consequence: `os.Stdout` and `os.Stderr` can be read where nothing in the build assigns them, and nothing is known of their value.
- **`asserts` says what a function finds on what it is handed** (F17, built on 2026-11-20, decided by the orchestrator). Without a claim, the call graph counts every exported method and every function value of the package as reachable from an interface value handed to another package, since the function may assert the value to another type or use reflection. An entry maps a parameter that can hold code, the receiver of a method included, to the types the function may assert it to, as in `asserts: {r: [io.WriterTo]}`, or `r: []` for none (`asserts.go`). The claim: while the call lasts, and when what it returns or keeps is called later through the entries of the methods of what it returns, it calls nothing the parameter holds, or that a call of it returns, at any depth, but the methods of the interfaces and the function values of the function types that the parameter's type and the listed types reach through fields, elements, and pointers, and it uses no reflection to call what the parameter holds. The listed types are qualified names resolved among what the function's package imports at any depth, or an interface written by its methods with predeclared types only, as `"interface{ Timeout() bool }"`. Defects, reported where the entry is used: a key that is not a parameter or holds no code, a type that does not resolve or holds no code, a parameter under `formats`, which says this already, and a parameter that `runs` and `keeps` do not list, since then the function may run nothing it holds. A type entry may claim it too. The claim narrows only the test of held code that the call graph and stratification share. It changes nothing that is known of the call's outcome; a proof that a cycle the claim rules out cannot happen rests on the claim, which is in What a proof assumes in the guide. The trust report prints it. Shipped, each read from the source of Go 1.27 with a comment that says what was read: the `bufio` and `io` wrappers, `log`, the `fmt` functions that write to a writer, and `os.IsExist`, `IsNotExist`, `IsPermission` (`err: []`, since they unwrap only `*PathError`, `*LinkError`, and `*SyscallError` and compare with `syscall.Errno`), and `IsTimeout` (`err: ["interface{ Timeout() bool }"]`), each with `runs: [err]` and `ensures: "err = nil -> ~result"`. The harness of `wrappers_test.go` gives the readers and writers the methods of the other optional interfaces of `io` and `fmt` and errors that record their calls, and fails on any call of them; `shipped_test.go` tests the `os` entries by a harness of their own and checks that every listed type resolves. The harness catches only the interfaces it has methods of, so the claim rests on reading the source, as `keeps` does.

Not built yet, of trusted functions that keep a value:

- **ReadString and ReadBytes** (F3 of the design review, decided by the orchestrator). They do not return from a reader that never yields the delimiter: they grow until a fatal out-of-memory error (a reader of `x` for ever reached 2975 MB in 2 s), and every entry claims its function returns. An assumption about what the input contains is not made for a shipped entry. A project that knows its input can write its own entry. `Scanner`, bounded at 64 KB, is the line reader. The trial met it once (digraph.parse), whose loop until EOF has no `Variant` either; the decision stands.
- **`os.ReadFile` and `os.ReadDir`, and the line between two kinds of assumption about the world** (F19, decided by the orchestrator, after the reviewer's question whether the file system, as the world, may not be assumed of as a `Read` from a pipe is). The file system is the world, and assumptions about the world are allowed. The line is between two kinds. That an I/O call returns at all is an assumption no program with I/O can do without, and it is made (a FIFO, a pipe nobody reads). That the input is finite, small enough to hold in memory, or contains a delimiter is an assumption a careful program does not make, because reading without a bound is itself the defect, and the bounded form is available and verifies. A shipped entry makes the first kind and not the second. A project that knows what its paths name writes the entry and takes the claim on. So `ReadFile` and `ReadDir` do not ship, for the reason `ReadString` and `ReadBytes` do not.
- **`(*bufio.Scanner).Bytes`, `ReadLine`, and `ReadSlice`**, which return a slice of the buffer that the next call overwrites: no entry can name that memory. They wait for footprints.
- **`(*bufio.Writer).WriteString`, `io.Copy`, and `io.WriteString`** (F8 of the design review): `WriteString` type-asserts the kept writer to `io.StringWriter` when its buffer is empty and the string is longer than the free space, and calls that method, which no entry covers; a writer whose `Write` meets its entry and whose `WriteString` panics makes the entry false.
