They have a package of their own: in `lockgroup` their exported writers of `rw` were what `HitNotify`'s value may run, which 49bb396 put there and whose test failed.
  - F21: a boundary function whose `Requires` names a state that a lock clause it assumes contradicts is told why (`lockContradiction`): "code outside the package's verified code can call Bump (it is exported), so it assumes ~held(mu), a lock clause of the invariant of mu, hits, on entry, which its Requires contradicts; keep a function that runs under the lock unexported, and do not use it as a value". `lockgroupbad` has one of each kind: exported (`Bump`), used as a value (`bumpValue`), and a method with an exported name (`helper.Run`).
  - Wrong versions caught: every function a boundary (`bump` unsatisfiable, `Hit` refused before it); the invariant assumed after a trusted call (every `Lock` reported as contradicting what is known); a group's kind-3 variable not covered by its target (`LeaveLocked` accepted); the escape rule without the closed address (the group of `lmu` and `log` refused).
- **Built in step 5** (`initcalls.go`): initialisers that call functions, and F1.
  - In a package with annotations every initialiser that `checkValueInitialisers` does not check as a variable holding code is run as code, in `InitOrder` (`runInitialiser`, a verifier with `inInitialiser`). A call of a verified function there whose `Requires` may not hold is reported where it stands, not captured: "precondition of set may not hold in an initialiser, where a verified function relies on it for what it writes: k >= 0". That is F1's repair: `var _ = set(-1)` broke `n >= 0`, and `Get`, proved `>= 0`, returned -1. Everything else such a run finds (an index, an overflow, a write its empty frame does not cover, a trusted call's requires) is kept quiet, as at the base, and gives the variable no facts.
  - An initialiser that holds a function literal is not run: it gives nothing, and a call in the literal of a verified function that has a `Requires` is reported, since nothing checks it ("set is called in a function literal in the initialiser of a package-level variable, where its Requires is not checked; call it from a function with a contract of its own").
  - **The facts** (`initialiserFacts`): the path's facts (`st.hyps`) with every constant but those of fixed variables renamed into `init <key> ` (`transport`), and each leaf of the variable equal to the renamed value, but a sequence, which has no equality in the logic; only what is connected to the leaves by a constant they name is kept, and nothing that names a sequence of the run (memory, a string it made). They are kept only for a fixed variable whose initialiser part 1 does not read completely (`knownComplete`), and asserted where a function reads it (`fixedValue`). A run that captured anything or reported a call outside its `Requires` gives none.
  - Programs: `initcalls`. `third` (`|table| = 3` from `buildTable`'s `Ensures`), `readSix` (`five() + 1`), `readCfg` (`Config{n: five()}`), `readDoubled` (`double(later)`, with `later` declared after it and initialised first), `Get`, and `pokeListed` (`box` is not nil, from `newBox`) verify; `fourth` (past the length), `readTooMuch` (its initialiser `double(5000)` is reported, and it gives nothing), and `poke` (`box` is fresh where `newBox` ran, not in `poke`) are refused, and the initialisers `double(5000)`, `short[0] + pick(short)`, and `set(-1)` are reported.
  - Wrong versions caught: no renaming (`poke` accepted, `pokeListed` unsatisfiable: the run's bound became the reader's); the facts of a run that reported a call outside its `Requires` kept (`readTooMuch` accepted); the reports captured (the three initialisers unreported, and the reviewer's `p3` `go test` fails).
  - Cost: rows that read a variable whose initialiser part 1 does not read completely moved, by up to 1.34 times (`globals.ProbeCfg`); the runs of initialisers are rows of their own.
- **F22 of the review of step 5.** F1's report left out a call through a value and of a method of an interface: the reviewer's `var setter Setter = set` and `var _ = setter(-1)` broke `n >= 0`, and `Get`, proved `>= 0`, returned -1. What a value runs may be a verified function of the package checked against the contract of its type, whose frame is proved only under that contract's `Requires`. Such a call in an initialiser is now reported as a verified callee is, and so is one in a function literal of an initialiser (`valueContract`, `methodContract`). The run of the initialiser could not reach the call before: calling through a value there is hidden init code, which made reading `setter` "not known to have its value yet"; while an initialiser runs (`initRunning`) that refusal does not apply, since Go initialises every variable it reads first. Program: `initvalues` (`setter(-1)`, `sets.Set(-1)`, and `setter(-2)` in a literal reported; `setter(2)` quiet). Wrong version: the base of step 5, which leaves them out.
- **Built as step 5b: what an initialiser shows, published.** The facts of the run of the initialiser of an exported fixed variable are published in `initialisersFact.Facts`, when they are small enough (`maxPublishedFacts`, 4096 nodes as trees, since a fact is encoded as a tree). Their constants are the variable's fixed constants and the run's renamed ones, the same names in every package. A reader of another package rebuilds them (`rebuiltTerm`: nil, True, and False as the logic's own values, and every node with its size) and asserts them where it reads the variable (`publishedInitFacts`), which holds since an importer's code runs only after the package is initialised (V5), and only while the variable is fixed in the build. Programs: `initcallslib` and `initcallsclient` (`Third` reads `Table[2]`, `IsWord` calls `Word.MatchString`, `ReadSeven` knows `Seven = 7`; `Fourth` and `NotEight` are refused). Wrong version caught: nothing read from the fact (the three fail).
- **Built in step 6**: the initialiser-only mark (D4 of F31). An entry may say `initialiserOnly: true`: its claims hold only for a call that the initialiser of a package-level variable makes itself, outside every function, where a panic stops the program, or the plugin that holds the package, before any code reads the variable. In a plugin, `plugin.Open` may recover the panic, and then nothing of the plugin can be reached without the `*Plugin` it did not return, unless an earlier initialiser handed code of the package out, which `hiddenInitCode` refuses (F5 of the design review). A call of such an entry anywhere else is refused (`initialiserOnlyWhy`), in a verified function, an `init` with a contract included (it runs after every initialiser, and a deferred `recover` there could catch the panic), and in a function literal of an initialiser, which is reported. Shipped: `regexp.MustCompile` and `MustCompilePOSIX`, with `ensures result != nil And fresh(result)`; the harness runs them on patterns that compile, and `TestMustCompilePanics` holds that they do not return where `Compile` and `CompilePOSIX` fail. `template.Must` has no `text/template` entries to stand on, and is not shipped. Programs: `mustcompile`. `isWord` (`var word = regexp.MustCompile(...)` and `word.MatchString(s)`) and `isKept` (an operand of a verified call) verify; `compileHere`, the literal of `lazy`, and `init` are refused.
- **Built in step 7** (`loggers.go`): a logger in a variable. `var logger = log.New(w, prefix, flags)` of type `*log.Logger` can be read when nothing assigns it, it is not exported, its initialiser is a call of `log.New` whose run verifies (`loggerInitialiser`, recorded by `runInitialiser`), and every use of it in the package is a comparison with nil or the receiver of a call, not in a `go` statement, of `(*log.Logger).Printf`, `Print`, or `Println` under their shipped entries (`loggerUses`; a project entry that replaces one is no allowed use, F6 of the design review). Then `ready` holds of the Logger outside those calls: `log.New` ensures it, each of the three ensures `ready(l')`, and nothing else writes the Logger, whose fields are unexported in another module and which the copy rule keeps from being copied or overwritten. So a call of one of them assumes `ready` of its receiver (`loggerCall`), and `logger != nil` comes from the facts of step 5.
  - **Who lists it.** When the initialiser's writer is inert (a concrete type whose methods `io.Writer` takes write nothing behind their receiver, as `os.Stderr`, or an inert fixed interface variable), a call of one of the three drops its receiver's targets (the Logger and what it owns), and nothing need be listed; listing it is reported as not needed. That is O-b: the writer's footprint is empty and stays so, its output never changes by the rule on uses, and of the Logger's cells only `ready` is something a formula can read, which the call restores. Otherwise the function lists the logger, which covers the Logger and the footprint and states of its writer, and so do its callers, by the variable in the package. A write through it forgets what is not apart from that footprint; a separation as F40 gives an interface variable is not built.
  - **Hidden init code.** `hiddenInitCode` now reads a trusted callee's entry (`trustedRuns`): an operand that holds code is no hidden init code when its parameter is under `keeps` and under nothing that runs it (`runs`, `calls`, `formats`, `prints`, `encodes`), as `log.New` keeps its writer, since `keeps` claims that the function runs the kept code during the call only if `runs` lists it. A later call that runs it hands what keeps it, and is scanned as such. Without it `log.New(&tally, ...)` made every initialised variable of the package unreadable. As first built (27c921e) it skipped every parameter not under one of those, which is the reading F15 rejected: an entry claims nothing of code a parameter carries unless `runs` lists it (F23 of the review of step 7). The reviewer's program, `hiddenlook`, hands `hooker{}` to a trusted `Look(x any)` that calls its `Init` during initialisation, before `table` is initialised; `third`, which reads `table[2]`, verified at 27c921e, and its `go test` panics with `index out of range [2] with length 0`. It is refused again.
  - **A logger whose initialiser does not verify** is reported at its reads with the reason, as F42 has it for a variable that holds code ("broken cannot be relied on: its initialiser does not verify, at log.go:98:14: precondition of New may not hold: out != nil").
  - Programs: `loggervar`. `Notify` and `notifyTwice` (over `os.Stderr`, nothing listed) and `count` (over a `*Tally` whose `Write` writes it, `Modifies counted`) verify; refused: `notifyListed` (lists an inert logger), `countUnlisted`, `notifyRenamed` (an unverified `SetPrefix`), `notifyExported`, `notifyBroken` (`log.New(nil, ...)`), and `echo`, F6's deadlock probe: `Echo`'s `Write` logs through the same logger, and its conversion is refused, since an implementation cannot call through an interface value yet (item 12), so the reads of `echoed` are refused with that reason rather than as a call that may not end. `globalsclient.Quiet`, the hole of F14 of part 1, is refused for its writer, whose `Write` has no contract. The guide's `Notify` verifies, and the guide gains "A logger in a variable".
  - Not built: an exported logger, a logger of another package, `log.Default()`, and the separation of a logger's writer from what a function's parameters reach.
- **Built in step 8: two of the three suggestions of F31's coder.**
  - **S1** (`keepsvars.go`): a call of a verified function of the package that never assigns an assigned variable it lists leaves its storage as it was, as if the callee ensured `v' = v`; the caller forgets only what the variable refers to (`keepUnassigned`, which drops the storage target, in `var ` regions, from what the call writes). `mayAssign` follows the call graph from the callee and counts, in each body reached (F7 of the design review): an assignment of the variable or of a part of it, in the body or a literal there, deferred or not; a call through a value or of a method of an interface whose contract lists it or a state that covers it, while the graph's edges follow what of the package the call may run; a trusted call whose entry lists it; and, for an exported variable, any call into another package. Built for kind 2 and interface variables, whose storage is reached only by name (V1); a struct variable whose address is taken may be written through any pointer that reaches it. The guide's `addTwo` verifies now without `routes' = routes`, and F19's message is shown with `addAfterNew`, whose callee assigns `routes`. Programs: `keepsvars` (`bumpTwice` and `printTwice`, refused at the base, verify; `afterValue`, `afterDeferred`, and `afterHelper` are refused). Wrong version caught: calls through values not counted (`afterValue` accepted).
  - **S3**: the initialisers of a package with no annotated function but a contract on a type of its own (`funcTypes`, `ifaceMethods`) are checked too. Before, a value such a package made was taken on the assumption about code that is not verified, and the program shows it false: `typeonlylib.Now`, a `Clock` whose literal returns -1 against `@Ensures t > 0`, let `typeonlyclient.Positive` verify; it is refused now with the initialiser's reason, and `PositiveGood` verifies.
  - **Speculation.** Since step 5 every initialiser of a package with annotations runs as code, and each speculative verifier ran them again, which asked Z3 the same questions twice in one run (`TestAnswerCache` caught it, at times, as an answer from the cache in a run with an empty one). A speculative verifier now waits for what the verification found of the initialisers (`initResults`, `takeInits`) and asks nothing of them; F41's checks of variables that hold code went the same way.
  - **S2 is not built** (O-c): the separation of a writer variable for callers that cover it through the state of a package would change what every function that lists a state assumes, one separation for every interface variable of every covered package, with a proof at each call; nothing here or in the trial needs it.
- **The trial and the time at the end of F32.** `measure.sh` at 44038e1: 75 of 152, as before, in 45 s. No function of the trial uses what F32 builds: `laascmd.main` stops at the bound of `os.Args[1:]` and at `os.Exit` (item 8), and hands `&excludedPackages` to `flag.Var`, which keeps it; `memory.main` at `flag.Parse`; `digraph`'s `main` at `flag.Usage = usage` (item 11); `tasks.main` at `HandleFunc` (item 11). `go test -count=1 ./vego/...` cold from `git archive` extracts of 292f589 and 44038e1, one at a time: base 176 s and feature 204 s (load 6.6 and 10.4, the reviewer's runs beside); base 175 s and feature 159 s (load 8.2 and 6.7); feature 160 s and base 158 s (load 6.1 and 7.3, nothing else beside). So 2 s over the base under the same load. Of the 5,300 rows of the base's cost table only `guide.Notify` (429 to 35,453, verified now where it was refused at once) and `globals.Count` (46 to 810, the same) more than doubled.
- **What a reader knows of a fixed variable whose initialiser calls functions** is the facts of the initialiser's path that read no memory: those whose constants are the variable's leaves, other fixed constants, literals, and the results of the calls in it, renamed to names of their own. In every run that reads the variable, its initialiser completed, and the real values satisfy them together. A path that forks gives nothing.

Not built yet:

- **A changed variable of type `error`** (F8 of the design review, a narrowing). Its origin would have to be the origin of every store into it in the build, published as a reliance, and a comparison with it would not be a sentinel comparison, so `errors.Is` against one would have an unknown target. A variable `lastErr` set from a parameter and formatted later is the program.
- **A named condition of a package's state that importers can require** (F12, decided by the orchestrator). A `Requires` of an exported function that reads unexported state is refused. What would lift it is a condition named by the package, that its functions establish and importers can require, as in `Init` before `Use`.
- **A helper that runs inside a group while a clause about its data is broken: `@Inside v`** (designed during F32 and not built, by the orchestrator's ruling; the lock clause serves the helper that only needs the lock). The program that needs it is a helper that leaves a clause about the group's data broken for its caller to restore.
  - **Meaning.** A function declared `// @Inside v`, naming one variable of a group, neither assumes the group's invariant on entry nor proves it on return. What it needs goes in its `Requires`, and what it leaves goes in its `Ensures`.
  - **Where it may stand.** It is refused at the annotation unless the function is unexported, is never made into a value, a method value, or a literal that leaves, is not let out, and is called only by verified functions of the package. A test, an unverified function, or an initialiser that calls it refuses it, with the caller named.
  - **At a call.** The invariant is not proved before the call, and is not assumed after it. The caller proves it again before it returns and before any call that may reach a function that assumes it, by frame where nothing changed since it held. A caller may be `@Inside` the same group too.
  - **Why V3 stays true.** An `@Inside` function runs only while the outermost verified caller of the chain runs. That caller assumed the invariant, restores it, and proves it before every call out.
  - **The yardstick.** `Hit` calls `bump`, both verify. The defects: `bump` called without what its `Requires` asks; `Bump` exported; `bump` stored in a variable; a caller that calls out of the package after `bump` and before restoring.
  - **The mutants.** `@Inside` that assumes on entry; the invariant assumed after the call; the check of callers off.
  - **Structs.** The same word would fit a parameter whose type has an invariant, `@Inside s`, under the same placement rules. Struct invariants record the same wish ("no opt-out"), and nothing in the lock clause stands in the way of either.
- **Code of the package kept and run later by another package**: `flag.Usage = usage`, a hook stored into another package's variable, or handed to a parameter that keeps it (D3, F6). It waits for item 11.
- **Part 4 of F31** is built as F32 (see "Built as F32" above). What it left out:
  - **A struct variable of another package whose address is taken**, read or named directly (O-d). Another package covers what a function of its package changes through the state of the package; a direct read, `&lib.V`, a value method on it, and `Modifies lib.V` are refused with that remedy.
  - **A verified callee as a sink of a closed address** (O-a): `load(&cfg)` with a verified `load` leaves the address open, so a write through a footprint forgets `cfg` and it cannot be in a group. A summary that a verified function neither converts nor keeps a pointer parameter, nor hands it to code that may, would admit it.
  - **`@Inside v`**, below.
  - **An exported logger, a logger of another package, a logger used in other ways** (`SetPrefix`, `SetOutput`, a copy), **and the separation of a logger's writer** from what a function's parameters reach, as F40 gives an interface variable.
  - **`template.Must`**, for want of `text/template` entries.
  - **A group whose initialiser makes its memory by a call** whose `Ensures` says `fresh(result)`: F37's "made where it stands" still refuses it, as `var cache = newCache()` in a group.
  - **A `*bufio.Writer` in a package-level variable**, as `var out = bufio.NewWriter(os.Stdout)`: it holds code, and is not one of the loggers that step 7 admits.
  - **S2 of F31's coder**, the separation of a writer variable for callers that cover it through the state of a package (O-c).
  - **Facts of what an initialiser shows of memory**: a reader knows only the facts of the run that read no memory, as `|t| = 3`, not the elements.
  - **An initialiser with a function literal** is not run: it gives no facts, and a call in the literal of a verified function with a `Requires` is reported.
  - **What `TestIsolatedHooks` does not do**: read the bodies of the isolated shipped functions for a call through a value read from a variable that an exported function writes (the reviewer's suggestion in F11); the pinned list of hooks stands for it.
- **`log.Default()`**: see Variadic calls and formatting.
- `flag` variables wait for pointers to anything but a struct (`flag.Int`) and for part 3 (`flag.StringVar(&s, ...)`); `var buf [64]byte` waits for arrays.

### Dynamic types and typed errors (interfaces, fourth part)

F33, item 4 of the list at the top, in progress. The design was agreed with the reviewer (F1 to F12 of its review) and the orchestrator, who cut the feature to typed errors (part 1, steps S1 to S6); assertions and type switches on other interfaces (part 2) and optional interfaces with `io.WriteString` and `io.Copy` (part 3) are features of their own. This section is written step by step as the feature is built.

The model (`dyntypes.go`):

- **An error is a Go error value, up to `==` where `==` is identity.** Equality of errors in the logic was "the same error"; it is now made precise without changing it: for a dynamic type on which `==` is identity (`identityEq`), two errors that hold equal values of it are one error, and for any other dynamic type an error stands for one conversion, which its copies share. Every fact stated before F33 stays true under this reading: made errors are new pointers, the errors of a sample are distinct inputs, and nothing says that two converted errors differ.
- **What an error holds is read from tables that nothing writes, at `ErrRef(e)`**, as the home of an interface value is read: `dyntype` gives the tag of the dynamic type, and `held T i.path` one leaf of the value of type T it holds. `ErrRef` is the one new function of the translation to SMT-LIB, `(declare-fun error-ref (Err) Int)`, declared only in a problem that reads it, so a problem without typed errors is the text it was. It is a function of the error and nothing more; nothing says it is injective. Guarded in `TestTranslation` (a function of the error, not a constant, not injective, two references tell two errors apart, a table read follows the error) and in `TestSortsAreChecked`; the random problems of `TestProveIsSound` read it at the references they build, evaluated by a model function that maps several errors to one reference. Mutants caught: `error-ref` written as a constant, as a fresh constant at each use, and declared injective.
- **Tags follow `types.Identical`.** The tag of a type is the number whose base-256 digits are the bytes of its canonical name (`canonicalName`): a defined type by the quoted path of its package and its name with its type arguments, an alias by what it stands for, `byte` as `uint8`, and the rest written out, with the package of each unexported field or method name and each struct tag, which `types.Identical` compares. A type declared inside a function, or one that holds one, has no canonical name, and a conversion of a value of it gives an error whose tag is unknown, which can only let a path be explored that cannot happen. `TestCanonicalNames` checks that two names are equal exactly when the types are identical over aliases, `byte` and `uint8`, parameter names, struct tags, unexported names of two packages, type arguments, channel directions, and interfaces.
- **A conversion of a value of a concrete type to `error`** (`typedError`) gives an error that is not nil, whose tag is the type's and whose held leaves are the value's. For an `identityEq` type whose value is one integer or a pointer, the error is a term of the value, `mkerr T` read at the value (two reads for a pointer), so two conversions of equal values are one error with no fact needed: `return 0, ErrSyntax` and `err == ErrSyntax` meet. For any other type it is a new error with the same facts. Such an error is never one of the made errors, whose facts (new, different from sentinels and opaque variables) stay theirs.
- **A nil pointer has position zero** (`goLeaves`, F2 of the design review): Go's `==` on two nil pointers of one type is true whatever position the logic gave them. The allocation of a pointer is read twice there, so one that is not a name or a number is named first, which keeps a chain of conversions from doubling (`semantics.HopChain`; the chain in which a conversion is handed what another made comes with assertions, S3).
- **One predicate decides where `==` on errors is equality in the logic: `identityEq`** (F1 of the design review and its condition). Integers, strings, booleans, pointers to values that do not have size zero, and arrays and structs of these; not floats (NaN), and not pointers to values of size zero, which Go may or may not give one address. It replaces the rule for opaque variables (`identityComparable`, which admitted every pointer), a narrowing that refuses `err == ErrZ` for `var ErrZ error = &E{}` with `E` of size zero; no program of the corpus, the guide, or the trial had one. Code may compare two errors when one is nil, of known origin, an opaque variable whose initialiser has an `identityEq` type (all as before), a value of an `identityEq` type that Go converts beside an error (`err == ErrSyntax`, a case of a switch on an error), or is proved nil or to hold a value of an `identityEq` type met in a conversion of the function (`holdsIdentityEq`). For two errors that code compares, the function states, for each such type met, that they are one error when they hold equal leaves of it (`sameTyped`).
- **A made error holds a type of package `errors` or `fmt`** (`noteMadeTag`): `*errors.errorString`, `*fmt.wrapError`, or `*fmt.wrapErrors`, stated of every made error and sentinel met, in a function that meets a conversion, so that `errors.New("x") == ErrSyntax` is false. `TestMadeErrorTypes` checks the names against go/types, and `TestErrorsAsModelled` the type of every error the running `errors.New` and `fmt.Errorf` return.
- **The methods of a type converted to `error` are checked at the conversion** (`checkedMethods`; a change from the design, which converted every value and left the methods to the origin). An error can reach code that is not verified, which may run any method of what it holds at any time: a trusted function that runs an error claims its contract whatever code arrives, so a method that writes made `values.BoxedImplicitly` verify and return 1 where its contract says 0. So every method of the type's method set must have a usable contract that modifies nothing and has no `Requires`, must make no call through a value, must name no variable of a group with an invariant, and must be declared on the type itself, not promoted; the type has no invariant; nothing the type holds through exported fields has methods, which reflection reaches; and a value receiver reached through the converted pointer has the pointer proved not nil at the conversion, where it is fixed. Otherwise the conversion is refused with a message that names the method (`guide.CheckBare`). A `Requires` about the receiver value, proved at the conversion, comes with the typed origin (S4).
- **What a converted error holds is counted** (F13 of the review of S1). `checkedMethods` asked only whether what a type reaches through exported fields has methods, and `any` has none: `HeldAny{X: c}` handed to a trusted function under `runs` that bumps what `X` holds verified and returned 1 where its contract said 0, and so did a field that is not exported. A conversion to `error` is now refused when the value holds, in any field at any depth, exported or not, through pointers, slices, arrays, maps, and channels, an `any`, an interface with a method that has no contract, a function type without a contract, or an `unsafe.Pointer` (`uncheckedHeld`). An `error` field and an interface whose methods all have contracts stay, since each value of them is checked where verified code makes it. The step after S5 that gives `runs` its new meaning may revisit it.
- **Every condition of `checkedMethods` has a refused program, and a message with its own remedy** (F14 and F15 of the review of S1): `typederrors/unchecked.go` has `ThroughAny`, `ThroughHidden`, `NilRuns` (whose run-time failure, a panic in `consume`, `TestRefusedWouldFail` shows, as it shows `ThroughAny` returning 1), `HookError`, `HitsError`, `PromotedError`, `NilValueReceiver`, `ReachedError`, `InvariantError`, and `ModifiesError`; each of the ten conditions turned off alone is caught by them.
- **No trusted contract for `errors.As` or `errors.AsType` can be true** (the orchestrator's condition): the message for a call of either without an entry says so and what to write instead (`noEntryCan`), where it advised adding an entry. No other function of the standard library that verified code calls in a form of its own leads to that advice: the routes of `encoding/json` have messages of their own.
- **Programs and claims.** `typederrors/` holds what S1 builds in both directions, and `typederrors/run_test.go` runs every equality the verifier proves against the real conversions and `==`; `TestCorpusClaims` runs it on the toolchain of the test, and with `VEGO_GO126=1` on Go 1.26.6 (GOPATH mode does not switch toolchains, so the test finds that toolchain's `go` and runs it). Mutants caught by the corpus: one table of canonical terms for every type, floats taken as `identityEq`, pointers to values of size zero taken as `identityEq`, no normalisation of nil pointers, the methods not checked, no `sameTyped`, no `noteMadeTag` where an error is made and where a conversion meets one, no tag fact, no held fact, a converted error not known to be non-nil, and `holdsIdentityEq` true without a conversion.

Built in S2, formulas (`dyntypes_spec.go`, the resolver's `Typer`):

- **`holds(x, T)`, `x.(T)`, and `T(v)`.** `holds(err, T)` is what `_, ok := err.(T)` gives: not nil, and the tag of T. `err.(T)` is a step of a path (`Step.Assert`) that reads the held leaves at `ErrRef(err)` and goes on through them, as in `err.(*PE).Line`; a read notes what T guarantees of the leaves and that a nil pointer is held at position zero (`heldRead`, F8's condition), so `err.(InvalidByteError) = 300` on an interface method is refused where it is declared. `T(v)` converts a constant in T's range, or a path whose Go type fits T's range, and is refused otherwise (F10): a formula does not wrap. A string or boolean type converts without a range. Types are named as in Go, `T`, `*T`, `pkg.T`, and in a trust file `"path".T`; nothing else is type syntax. `as(err, T)` is reserved and refused until S5.
- **An error beside a value of a concrete type is Go's `==` of the error and the conversion** (`typedCompare`, `TypedEquals`), whether the value is a constant (`err = ErrSyntax`), a conversion (`err = InvalidByteError(src[k])`), or a path (`err = p`). For a type whose errors are terms of their values the formula is equality with that term, and the term's facts are noted where the formula builds it (`noteCanonical`, F8): in the state the formula is read in, or, when the value mentions a variable a quantifier binds, as one fact for every value of the type. For another `identityEq` type it is not nil, the tag, and the held leaf. A type on which `==` is not identity, and a struct, are refused. `is(err, C)` takes the term as its target, and a target of a type without one is refused. An untyped constant beside an error is still refused.
- **Nil beside a pointer is a nil pointer only as written** (found in S2): an error result that an outcome chose to be nil, beside a pointer of an error type, was read as `p = nil`, while Go's `error(p)` is never nil. `typederrorsbad.TypedNilNotNil` is refused.
- **A name that is both a type and a predicate** is reported when a formula could convert to the type (an integer, string, or boolean type); a struct type leaves its name to the predicate, as `guide.LastZero`'s `zeros` does.
- **Contracts read in other packages** carry the keys of the types they name and of the types of their constants (`contractFact.Types`), and the values of constants of other packages (`s2.ErrSyntax`), which a formula may now name.
- **An error variable initialised with a constant of a defined type** (`var ErrX error = Error(2)`) is the term of that constant wherever it is read, in code and in formulas (`noteTypedVariable`, F12).
- **The always-fails check** takes the tables as part of every input, as a fixed variable (`isDynTable`), which a function that compares an input with `ErrRange` needed (`PassRange`, reported as requiring success for no input before), and offers each error a contract names as a term of a constant to its samples, as it offers error variables.
- Mutants caught: no facts of terms in formulas, no quantified fact, no type facts of held reads, the range of a constant or of a path's type not checked, `holds` without "not nil", `!=` not negated, the tables taken as produced, no term offered to samples, nil read as a nil pointer, the typed variable not tied, and the tag dropped from the form for strings. Programs: `typederrors/formulas.go`, `typederrorsclient/` (imported contracts and a trust entry naming `*"typederrors".PE`), `typederrorsbad/` (every refusal), with their claims run by `TestCorpusClaims`.
- **Fixes of the review of S2.** A string type converts only a string literal or a path of type string or `[]byte` (F16): Go encodes a `[]rune` as UTF-8, so `SErr(rs)` read as the runes' numbers made a trusted entry `err = SErr(rs)` prove `Wrap([]rune{233}) != SErr("é")`, which is false (`typederrorsbad.Accent`, refused at the entry). `err.(*PE) != nil` crashed `spell`, which did not know the step of an assertion (F17; `typederrors.HeldNotNil`). The facts S2 states are guarded by verdicts (F18): the trust entries `typederrorsbad.two` (`err = Error(1) And err = Error(2)`), `big` (`holds(err, B) And err.(B) = 300` for a byte type), and `wide` (`err = InvalidByteError(n)` for an int `n`) are each refused only by their fact or check, and `NilValueReceiver` has a contract that is otherwise met, so the nil value-receiver check alone refuses it.
- Not built from S2: a sample for a contract that reads what an input holds only through `holds` and `x.(T)` (`@Requires holds(in, Error)`), which the always-fails check reports as finding no simple input; and an outcome of `canBeMet` for a contract on a type that says only `holds(err, T) And err.(T) = v` for a type whose errors are not terms of their values, which is not proved to exist.

Invariants, stated before the code that relies on them:

- **TI (tags).** The tag of a non-nil error or interface value is the tag of its dynamic type, or unknown. No fact gives a value a known tag but a conversion, `noteMadeTag`, and (S2 on) a contract or an assertion whose obligation is proved, or assumed of code that is not verified.
- **HV (held values).** The leaves read under the tag of a value are the value converted, with nil pointers at position zero, and they never change, since Go copies the value into the interface.
- **AC (assertions, S3).** An assertion never yields a value with a contract it was not checked against: a concrete target's methods are called under their own contracts, and an interface target is accepted only when none of its methods has a contract, or every contracted one is a declaration in the static type's method set or is implied by the static type's method of the name.
- **TO (typed origin, S4).** A typed error of known origin holds a value whose every method, and every method of what it holds through exported fields, writes nothing a caller sees, asks nothing but of the receiver value, which was proved where it was converted, runs no code through values, and is reached by the call graph from every place that may run it. S1 holds the first half of this for every conversion.

Not built yet, found on the way:

- **A trusted function under `runs` handed an `any` that holds a value of the project whose methods write** (found in S1, at the base too): `var x any = a` for a `failing` slice with a writing `Error`, handed to a trusted `consumeAny(x any)` with `runs: [x]` and `modifies: []`, verifies, and the program returns 1 where the contract says 0. The base's rule says that `runs` claims the contract whatever code arrives, which puts it on the entry; but no author can make such an entry true of code verified code made. Converting to `any` a value whose methods are not checked as `checkedMethods` checks them is the same hole as the one S1 closes for errors, and part 2 counts every interface.

### Errors

Built: errors with an identity. Code compares them with `==`, `!=`, and `switch`, calls `errors.Is`, and reads the error variables of its own package and of every package it is built with. Contracts say which error is returned, with `err = ErrEmpty` and `is(err, ErrEmpty)`. Also built: non-nil results for `errors.New` and `fmt.Errorf`, the trees of what `%w` wraps, the always-fails check with its exception, every error variable that is never assigned (made, alias, or opaque), and errors of trusted calls that the contract vouches for (see Trusted contracts). An error inside a struct result is not looked at by the always-fails check.

Decisions:

- **Identity is equality.** Two errors are equal in the logic when they are the same error, which is what Go's `==` reports when one side is a pointer. Code may compare two errors only when one of them is `nil`, a sentinel, of known origin (made, or vouched, whose claim says `==` is identity), or an opaque variable whose initialiser has a pointer, integer, string, or boolean type or an array or struct of these. Two errors of which nothing is known may hold the same uncomparable type, and are refused. Floats are excluded since `==` is not identity on them (NaN, and 0 against -0), and so are channels, to keep the stated rule short. A formula may compare any two errors, and there `=` is identity.
- **A contract of a type in source can vouch for its errors** (F18, decided by the orchestrator, from the trial's memory server: `fmt.Errorf("failed to read from store: %w", err)` after `k.s.Read()`). `// @Vouches err` in the doc comment of a method of an interface or of a function type declared in source lists error results (`addVouches`), with the claim of `vouches` in the trust file. It joins the contract on its terms: checked of every value verified code makes of the type (`madeAs` and the literal check, which already read `T.vouches` and now name the contract in source, `vouchingContract`), and assumed of every other value, which "What a proof assumes" says in the bullets on interface values and function values from code that is not verified. It is not a new kind of in-source trust: the contract it adds to is already assumed of values from unverified code. `vouchedThroughValue` no longer requires a trust entry. The listed results travel in `contractFact.Vouches`, so a package that calls through a value of the type or makes one reads them; `vouchsrcclient.MakeRelay` is refused only through the fact. Not in the trust report, since no contract in source is (decided by the orchestrator). Refused: a lone `Vouches` with no other clause (F3 (a) of the design review), one in the doc comment of a function (a verified function's errors are judged from its body), in a body, on a struct, and a name that is not an error result or is listed twice (`vouchsrcbad`).
- **Every call makes a different error.** An error made in a body differs from every sentinel, from every other error made in the function, and from every opaque variable met, which held its value since initialisation: the facts are stated pairwise as each appears. It is not known to differ from a parameter or the result of a call. Stating that needs an allocation fact on every error read, which was left out until a contract needs it. An opaque variable is not known to differ from a sentinel, since `var ErrF = id(ErrMine)` holds one.
- **A variable that is never assigned holds one error.** findSentinels classifies every package-level variable of type `error` that its package never changes (since package-level variables, `findChanges` decides that for every variable, see there): made (an initialiser of `errors.New` or `fmt.Errorf`, the sentinel as before), alias, or opaque, and publishes all of them in `sentinelsFact`, with what changes the others (`Changed`), so the write and reliance machinery covers every kind. An alias is a variable whose initialiser names another error variable, or calls a parameterless, non-generic function of the same package whose whole body is `return V` (`fs.ErrNotExist = errNotExist()`); it is that variable's constant, so `os.ErrNotExist`, `fs.ErrNotExist`, and `oserror.ErrNotExist` are one sentinel. The form was kept that narrow on purpose: no longer bodies, no chains of calls, no functions of other packages. An alias whose target cannot be relied on is opaque: it copied one value at its initialisation, and its proofs do not rely on the target. An opaque variable is a constant with nothing assumed but what the static type of its initialiser gives: not nil when that type is concrete (a nil pointer included), and comparable as above. Its methods are unknown, so it is not formatted, not the first argument of `errors.Is`, and not handed to a trusted function. Settledness covers every kind; in `hiddenInitCode` only made sentinels and aliases of them, or of variables of other packages, count as holding no code of the package, and anything else goes through its initialiser.
- **Known origin has two kinds, and every conclusion needs only what both give.** Made: errors.New or fmt.Errorf made it from plain values and made errors, so its methods and those of its tree are the standard library's. Vouched: a trusted contract vouches for it, it holds such an error in its tree, or a verified function returned it. Formatting, the first argument of `errors.Is`, the safe side of `==`, handing to a trusted function without `runs`, and `KnownErrors` rest only on methods and `==`, which both kinds give. What needs a made error (not nil, differs from other errors, what `errors.Is` finds) is never drawn from known origin; it is noted where errors.New and fmt.Errorf are called and for sentinels.
- **Sentinels come from source, for every package.** `laas` analyses every package a package is built with, the standard library included, so `findSentinels` runs on `io` as on the package itself. Each package publishes its sentinels, what each one's error rests on, and its tree in a fact about the package. A fact about a variable reaches only the direct importers of its package, which left `io.EOF` unknown in a package that imports `io` only through another. Trust entries for variables were considered and not built: a fact read from source is stronger than a claim and covers third-party modules too. Settledness is a matter of the declaring package only. Go initialises a package before any code of its importers runs, so an importer reads a sentinel that is unsettled in its own package as a sentinel.
- **A write anywhere in the build is seen.** Every package publishes its writes to the error variables of other packages, assignment and `&` both, and the proofs that rely on sentinels publish which keys they rely on, with every sentinel that went into them. A package refuses a sentinel that it, or a package it is built with, writes, also through what went into it. And every package reports each write in its build that some proof in its build relies on, so running `laas` on a root package alone reports a write in one dependency to a sentinel that another dependency relies on. What remains assumed is code outside the build and writes through `go:linkname`, assembly, `unsafe`, or `reflect`.
- **`errors.Is` needs a known origin in its first argument.** It runs the `Is` and `Unwrap` methods of the tree it searches, and calls nothing on the target, so the target may be any error. Its result is `is(x, t)`.
- **Trees by ground instances.** For each error whose tree is known, which are the errors made in the body and the homemade sentinels, and each target met outside a quantifier, the verifier assumes `is(e, t) <-> (e = t Or (w != nil And is(w, t)))`, or `is(e, t) <-> e = t` when nothing is wrapped. For every `is` term it also assumes that `is(nil, t)` is `t = nil` and `is(x, nil)` is `x = nil`, which is `errors.Is`'s first test and calls no method. A quantified axiom was not used: Z3 then answers `unknown` where the search for inputs needs `sat`. A target bound by a quantifier gets no instance, which can only keep something from being proved. The facts stay in the facts of the path, so the dependence check's renamed copies keep them.
- **The format is read, or the tree is not known.** `formatWraps` follows fmt's `doPrintf` for the formats it can read without doubt: flags, a width and a precision of at most six digits each (fmt stops reading a format at a number past a million), a verb that is any rune, `%` taking no operand, at most one `%w`. Anything else, such as a format that is not a constant, `[n]`, `*`, or several `%w`, gives an error of known origin whose tree is not known, so `errors.Is` on it is an unknown boolean. Nothing that verified before this became `cannot verify`. A call without an error operand wraps nothing whatever its format. The rules hold from Go 1.13 on. `TestFormatWraps` checks them against the running `fmt.Errorf` and `errors.Is` on random formats drawn from the forms fmt reads in more than one way, and `TestErrorsAsModelled` checks what the model takes from the implementation and not from documentation: every result is a new pointer, none has an `Is` method, and `Unwrap` returns what the tree says.
- **Inputs of the always-fails check.** Each error of a sample is `nil`, a sample error of its own, the error of an earlier leaf, or an error variable the formulas mention and tie to an error of the input: two constants that hold errors are tied when one formula mentions both, transitively. Offering every variable the formulas mention made the shapes pass `maxShapes` once `strconv.Atoi`'s contract named `ErrSyntax` and `ErrRange`, and a function with two error parameters lost its excuse; a variable tied only to what calls returned cannot be needed by a fact about the input. The sample errors are known to be non-nil and pairwise different, which separate `errors.New` values are. Error variables count as inputs, and the facts about them alone are part of what is known of every input.
- **Contracts name sentinels by their spelling where they were written.** A contract of this package resolves `ErrEmpty` and `io.EOF` in the file of the function. An imported contract carries the key of each spelling (`contractFact.Errors`), so an importer's own `ErrGone` cannot capture it. A struct invariant may not name an error variable yet, and a predicate names only its parameters.
- **Only an error of known origin can be formatted** (since variadic calls, one case of the rule for formatting; see Variadic calls and formatting). `fmt.Errorf` runs the `Error` method of an error it formats and, with verbs such as `%#v`, methods of the values the error contains. An error passed in from outside could use them to write memory the function reasons about. An error may therefore be formatted only if it was made by `errors.New` or `fmt.Errorf`, here or in a verified function that returns nothing else, is a package-level variable of any package holding such an error, or is one that a trusted contract vouches for. That is decided where the error is used, from the assignments on the paths that reach the use (since F20; see the next bullet). Errors from parameters and from trusted functions that do not vouch for them can be returned and compared with `nil` or a sentinel, but not formatted or searched with `errors.Is`. The same rule decides the first argument of `errors.Is`.
- **Origin is decided along the path** (F20, 2026-11-20; `origins.go`, `originpath`, `originpathclient`). Go reuses `err` for every call, so the rule over every assignment in the function refused `f, err := os.Open(n); if err != nil { return fmt.Errorf("...: %w", err) }; err = decode(f)` at the `%w`, once the shipped `os` entries vouched for their errors. Now a local error variable is followed along the paths, tracked, when it is not in `unknown` (no `&x`, no range, no assignment the collection does not understand, and since F20 not the variable of a type-switch clause, which had no sources and so counted as known) and every assignment to it is in its home, the function or literal that declares it, or in a literal deferred where it is written there. A use of a tracked variable in its home's own code is judged by the definitions that reach it: reaching definitions over `golang.org/x/tools/go/cfg`, with `mayReturn` always true, the union at every join, and a fixed point at every loop head, so an origin survives a loop exactly when every assignment in it keeps one and no invariant has to say so. On entry a parameter of the declared function is unknown, one of a literal used in place holds one of the arguments of its calls, each judged where it is written, one of any other literal is unknown, and a named result is nil; a `var` without a value is a definition of nil, which kills what an earlier iteration assigned. Everything else is decided as before, from every assignment in the function: a variable that a literal assigns, which may run between any two statements, and every use inside a literal, which may run after any assignment. A literal deferred where it is written runs only when its frame returns, after every use in the frame's own code on that path, and so does everything made inside it, which is why its assignments are left out of the paths (F1 of the design review); what the function publishes for a result such a literal assigns is still judged by every assignment (`late` in `returnsKnown`). Why a flow over the text and not the state of the executor: `KnownErrors` is a least fixed point over the package before anything is verified, the state is rewritten in many places that would each have to keep an origin current, and `forget` would lose every origin a loop touches. The origin at a use is computed from the position of its syntax and never kept in a state, so no consumer can read a stale one: formatting, `%w`, the first argument of `errors.Is`, `comparableError`, the hand-overs to trusted functions and to values, which read it at the argument (a deferred call's at the defer statement), and `KnownErrors` and the conversion checks of a value of a type that vouches, which read the rule of publishing: each `return` is judged by the origins where it stands, a bare return by the definitions of each named result that reach it. A recovered panic, which go/cfg does not model, is the only other way back to a caller; `recover` is `cannot verify` and in a function that verifies nothing panics, and the reviewer accepted that against judging named results by every assignment in a frame with a `defer`, which would have taken `KnownErrors` from functions that publish it (decided with the orchestrator). Nothing that verified before can stop: the definitions that reach a use are some of all assignments, plus entry definitions, and the only unknown one is a parameter's, unknown before too. That holds only if no answer outlives a round of the fixed point of `KnownErrors`: a definition `err = g()` judged before `g` qualifies is unknown then and known later, and a cache that kept the first answer made a caller of a function declared after it, and what such a function publishes, stop verifying (F5 of the review of step 1; `CallsLater`, `Passes`, `originpathclient.WrapsPasses`). The cache is cleared whenever a function joins `knownReturns`. Loud paths (F4 of the design review): a use in a body's own code that no node of the graph holds, and a use in a live block that no definition reaches, are internal errors that panic, and so is a tracked variable that a range assigns, since go/cfg puts the key and value once before the loop; a use in a block go/cfg finds unreachable is decided from every assignment. A search for an unknown definition counts one being decided as known, caches what it finds unknown, and caches what it finds known only when nothing was being decided (`CachedCycle`). The message names a definition that reaches the use without a known origin, one that brings the error itself before one that copies another variable, then the first by position; it says whether it came around a loop or along a branch, follows a copy through at most four variables to the assignment that brought the unknown error (F6), and chooses the remedy for where that assignment is: a variable of its own for its error when a variable on the way also holds errors of known origin, keeping it out of the variable that is used when only that one does, and nothing when no variable can help. Thirteen mutations of the analysis were each caught by the corpus or by `TestReachingPositions`, three of them by a panic of the analysis (no back edges, one predecessor instead of the union, type-switch variables known); the thirteenth keeps the cache across the rounds of `findKnownReturns` (`WrapsPasses`).
- **A contract must not allow a function to always fail.** For every verified function that returns an error, vego checks whether an implementation that always returns a non-nil error and zero values would satisfy the postcondition. If it provably would, that is an error, not an opt-in audit finding. The fix is to state when the function succeeds, as in `err = nil <-> 0 <= i < |A|`.
- **Exception: success that hangs on the outside.** A function cannot promise success when a call nothing predicts decides it: a trusted function whose contract leaves open what comes back, or a verified function that is itself excused. The call must decide between failure and success: some return of an error and some return without one must each depend on what the call produced, and each must be reachable by some input. Reaching the call is not enough, and a function that fails whatever the call does is not excused.
- **Reaching the call is judged generously.** Only the conditions on the function's own inputs must be satisfiable by a concrete input. When the path to the call depends on values the verifier does not know, such as the result of an earlier call, the path counts as possible. A call that is in fact dead behind such an unknown can therefore excuse a weak contract. So can a call that in fact always succeeds when seeing that takes facts about memory more than one pointer away from its arguments: what the types guarantee is supplied for the arguments, for the elements of their slices, and for the objects their pointers refer to, but not further. Both make the check miss a weak contract; neither makes a proof wrong.
- **A sample says nothing of the homes of values made after entry** (F15, found with `io.NopCloser`). A sample of the always-fails check gives each interface value of the input with states a home of its own and every other reference one home that exists, which is how the samples meet the facts about parameters. A call whose contract says that an interface result is new, `fresh(result)`, then contradicted the sample (its home is past the bound before the call), so `canReach` found no input that reaches the path after the call, the call was no source of failure, and `rc := io.NopCloser(r); _, err = rc.Read(p); return err` was reported as requiring success for no input. That dated from states of interface values (`ifacestatelib.Fetch` has the shape). The facts that read the home of a reference that is not the input's are now left out where `canReach` applies a sample (`readsLaterHome`); the path itself is still refuted with them. That can only make a path more likely to be reached, the generous side of the bullet above, and the acceptance of a sample still proves the facts about the input. `keeps.ReadsClosing` verifies, and `keeps.AlwaysFails` and `FailsAfterRead`, which fail whatever the read does, are still reported (F16 of the review). A first fix gave the sample a table of homes that leaves references past its own open, a free constant or a home far past every reference: correct, and 35 times slower. Every function that reads through a value a trusted call returned (`TeeReader`, `LimitReader`, `MultiReader`, `NopCloser`) took about 7 s, with 6 questions of the check at the limit, against 0.2 s now. Z3 solves those questions at once when the table of homes is one value everywhere and the facts hold a quantifier over a written row; a table with two values makes it run to the limit, whatever the second value is (F17 of the review). Measured on one such problem with the pinned Z3: 4,686 units with a uniform table, more than 4 M with any other.
- **Depending on a call is decided by proof.** Whether a returned error is nil, or the condition of a branch that was a real choice, depends on a call when it cannot be proved to stay as it is once everything the call produced is replaced by other values the call could have produced. What a call produced spreads to what a later call returns that was handed any of it, and to the unknowns that loops put in place of variables. It is not followed through memory that a later call reads or that a loop writes; a function that depends on the call only in that way is reported and has to be restructured.
- **The check asks only what is read** (decided with speed, by the orchestrator). What `noteFailureSource` and `tieFailures` find (the calls whose failure nothing predicts, and whether the function fails because of one) is read only by the report of a contract that may always fail, and only for a function that returns an error. So they ask nothing unless such a function is being verified (`tracksFailure`); in a function that returns no error, in an audit, in the check of a value made from code, and in every other task they return at once. One thing is still done there: whether a trusted contract, or the contract of a type, forces success for some input is settled for each callee with an error result (`trustForces`), since the trust report reads it for its note `[may fail unpredictably]`. Skipping questions changes the comments of later problem texts, which carry numbers the process hands out, and never their answers: Z3 reads no comment. Together with the next bullet, it brought the main corpus run from 22,309 problems asked and 369 M units counted at 35dca07 to 20,263 and 226 M at the end of speed (the same packages; `manywrites` came later).
- **Whether no state forces success is asked only for a contract that is reported.** When the search finds no input, `forcesSuccess` leaves that further question, the costliest of the check (a quantified question that ran to the limit 31 times on the corpus at 35dca07), as a function that `reportAlwaysFailing` calls for a contract it reports, with the facts and options built when the function was verified, and counts against the function. Its answer only chooses between "it requires success for no input" and "no simple input was found". `trustForces` never asks it, since it reads only whether an input was found. Of the 201 times the corpus asked it at 35dca07, 53 were for contracts that are reported.
- **The always-fails check waits for the function's own faults** (F17, decided by the orchestrator). The trial's `searchNodes`, which cannot be verified for other reasons, asked 1,897 problems, 1,459 of them questions of the check at a limit. A function that drew a report inside its declaration or its doc comment is not searched for an input on which it always fails, and its failures are not tied, until the fault is fixed: the report would add nothing a reader can act on before then. A caller whose excuse depends on such a function asks it then, so an excuse still rests on an answer (`errors.go`, `outcome.pending`; `trialfaults/alwaysfails.go`). `searchNodes` now asks 85. The guide says when the check runs.
- **The questions come in the same order on every run.** A loop records what an iteration leaves in each variable it forgets in the order of the variables' names; it took them in the order of a Go map, so `tieFailures` asked the same questions in another order on another run, and once 56 questions against 58. The answers never differed. The guard of the corpus costs (see The prover) fails when an order changes what a function asks, as this one did; an order that asks the same questions in another sequence changes no count and no answer.
- **A sentinel is trusted only when nothing can run before it is set.** `var ErrX = errors.New("x")` is non-nil for a verified function unless code of the package can run during the initialisation of its variables in a way Go's ordering does not see. Code of a package reaches another package only by being handed over, so every hand-over in code that runs during initialisation costs the package its sentinels: an argument, receiver, or type argument of another package's function, an assignment to another package's variable or to anything but a plain variable, a send, a built-in that passes its argument on, and a call or range that cannot be resolved. A value whose type has no room for code is not a hand-over.

- **The outside world is unknown, not modelled.** A call whose outcome depends on something that is not an argument (a file, the network, the clock) returns some value satisfying its contract, fresh on every call. Proofs must hold for every such value. vego does not model the state of the world.

Not built yet:

- **Origins through memory and across literals** (left out of F20). An error read from a struct field, a slice element, a map, or through a pointer has no known origin, and neither has a variable whose address is taken or that a `range` assigns. A use inside a function literal, and a variable that a literal other than a deferred one assigns, are decided from every assignment in the function; a literal used in place could be judged at each of its calls, with the definitions that reach the call, as the executor inlines it. The parameter of an in-place literal holds what any of its calls gives it. The call graph's `knownAtCall` reads no variable, only nil, a call of `errors.New` or `fmt.Errorf`, and a sentinel at the argument, so an error variable of known origin handed to another package still counts as one that may hold any type; it could read the origin at the argument.
- **More of an opaque variable.** Its methods could be read from source like a sentinel's, so that `context.DeadlineExceeded`, whose type has only `Error`, `Timeout`, and `Temporary`, could be formatted and searched. That needs a reading of every method of the type and of what it holds, which is the vouches claim made by the verifier instead of an author. Not planned until a use appears.
- **`errors.Join` and several `%w` in one `fmt.Errorf`.** They are rare in the code this is for. The model extends to them: the tree has several branches, each guarded by its error not being nil, and since Go 1.20 both behave the same way. Until then several `%w` give a tree that is not known.
- **`errors.Unwrap`.**
- **A new error known to differ from a parameter or the result of a call.** It needs an allocation number for every error made, and a fact on every error read that its number lies below the allocation frontier.
- **Struct invariants that name an error variable**, which needs the keys of their spellings to travel with the invariant as they do with a contract.

### Integers

Built: every integer type, overflow obligations for arithmetic, negation, division, and conversion, and the named limits (`MaxInt` and friends) in annotations. Shifts and bitwise operators are expressed by arithmetic (`vego/bits.go`): a shift is a multiplication or division by a power of two, `x & y` is an unknown with the facts that hold of any two integers in two's complement plus exact ones for constant masks, and the other operators follow from it by identities such as `x | y = x + y - (x & y)`. Not built yet: bit operators in annotations, so that a function can say which bit it set; more about `x & y` than its bounds when neither operand is a constant mask (the XOR swap and `x&(x-1) == 0` are not proved); and automatic range facts for slice elements inside quantified annotations.

Decisions:

- **Overflow is an error for every integer type, including `int`.** Go wraps silently; verified code may not. Each arithmetic operation carries a proof obligation that its result fits the type, so program values always equal their mathematical values. The same goes for conversions to a narrower type, negation of the minimum value, and `MinInt / -1`. Code that wraps on purpose (hashes, checksums) cannot be verified and belongs in the trusted file.
- **Annotations keep mathematical integers.** `|A| + 1` in a contract never overflows.
- **A shift to the left must not lose bits**, for the same reason, and a shift count that may be negative is an error since Go panics on one.
- **Bits are facts about integers.** Everything about `&`, `|`, `^` and shifts is told to Z3 as facts about unknown integers, each of which is true of all integers in two's complement and tested against Go's operators in `bits_test.go`. Z3's bit-vector theory is not used: values stay mathematical integers throughout, and mixing them with bit-vectors would need a conversion at every operator. It is the way to the XOR swap and `x&(x-1) == 0` if those are wanted.

How the solver supports this: Z3's integers are unbounded, so bounds like 2^63 - 1 need nothing special; its nonlinear arithmetic covers `i*w + j < h*w` from `i < h` and `j < w`, and deeper facts are left to an `Assert`; built-in facts such as `|A| <= MaxInt` come from the verifier.

### Floats

Built as decided.

Decision: floats are opaque. The verifier tracks a `float32` or `float64` through the code but knows nothing about its value: arithmetic yields an unknown float, a comparison an unknown boolean, and a conversion to an integer an unknown integer of that type. Modelling floats as real numbers is ruled out as unsound. Exact IEEE reasoning is possible now that the prover is Z3, which has a floating-point theory, and is not built.

Consequences, accepted because the first target is services, CLIs, infrastructure, and data structures, where floats are incidental:

- No fact about a float value can be stated or proved.
- An integer computed from a float needs an explicit range check before it is used as an index.
- A loop controlled by a float cannot be proved to terminate, so its function goes in the trusted file.

Possible later: facts that are true of real floats, such as small integers converting exactly, added when a concrete need appears.

### Strings

Built: the basic tier and approximate ranging. A concatenation must be proved not to exceed `MaxAlloc`, the largest allocation of the platform, for the same reason as an `append`.

Decisions:

- **Basic tier.** A string is an immutable sequence of bytes: length, indexing, equality, literals, concatenation, substrings `s[i:j]`, and the copying conversions `[]byte(s)` and `string(b)`. Substrings are safe here, unlike sub-slices, because strings cannot be written to.
- **Unicode, approximately.** In `for i, r := range s`, `r` is some valid character and the next index is one to four bytes further. Exact UTF-8 decoding is not modelled.
- **Contents through the shipped entries** (F19). Contracts about content are stated with quantifiers over bytes: where `Index` and `Cut` find their instance, what `TrimPrefix` keeps, that `IndexByte` finds no earlier byte. Left until real code needs it: string ordering, and a term for a substring in formulas.
- **`string(x)` of an integer is its UTF-8 encoding, exactly** (F18, from the trial's digraph.split): a new string with facts by range, one to four bytes with their values, and the three bytes of U+FFFD for a negative number, one above 0x10FFFF, or a surrogate, as the runtime gives for every integer type (it checks `int64(rune(v)) != v` and does not truncate; `string(int64(1<<32+65))` is U+FFFD). The integer is named once (`runeString`, `utf8Facts`). `TestRuneString` asks Z3, for every boundary and random values of each integer type, uint64 above MaxInt64 included, that the facts allow Go's bytes and nothing else; it fails on a boundary moved by one, a wrong lead byte, and a surrogate left out. `semantics.RuneChain` repeats it thirty times.
- **Kept opaque.** Formatting, parsing, and pattern matching. `fmt.Sprintf` returns some string, of which only a bound on its length is known (see Variadic calls and formatting); `strconv.Atoi` returns some number when `err = nil`.

## Later

### Runtime checks for trusted contracts (gradual mode)

A trusted contract may be wrong in practice. The remedy is to check it while the program runs under `go test`: evaluate the `Requires` before the call and the `Ensures` after it, and fail loudly on a violation. The same mechanism covers the other direction, where unproved code calls a verified function and nobody has checked the `Requires`. This is the paper's gradual mode, applied where it pays off most.

Planned mechanism: `go build -toolexec`. The Go toolchain lets a program wrap each compiler invocation and rewrite source files before they are compiled, which is how compile-time OpenTelemetry instrumentation works. vego would use it to inject the checks during test builds only, so:

- the source tree is never modified and no generated files are checked in;
- checks can be added at call sites of external functions, which have no source in the project to edit;
- production builds carry no overhead.

Things to settle when this is built: which formulas are executable (bounded quantifiers are, quantifiers over all integers are not), the cost of checks inside loops, and how a violation is reported so it points at the contract and not at the injected code.

This is deliberately deferred. Nothing else on the roadmap depends on it.

### Effects: marking which functions touch the outside world

Worth investigating; nothing is decided. An effect system records, per function, whether it may interact with the outside world. Functions without the mark are guaranteed to depend only on their arguments.

How it could combine with the current design:

- Trusted entries would declare the effect, for example `effects: [io]` on `os.ReadFile`. The verifier would infer it for verified functions from what they call.
- The always-fails rule already has this shape: a contract may leave failure open only if the function calls something whose contract does. An explicit effect would state that directly instead of deriving it from contract shape, and would catch a trusted entry for a pure function that merely has a weak contract.
- Pure functions could be given stronger reasoning: the same arguments give the same result, and they could be called inside annotations.
- A contract could forbid effects, for example to prove that a function does no I/O.

A further step, deliberately not planned, is modelling the world's state as an invisible input so that contracts can say when I/O succeeds and relate one call to another.

### Other items from the paper

- Multiset reasoning, so a sort can promise that its output is a permutation of its input.
- The `Preserves`, `Immutable`, `Cost`, and `Library` annotations.
- Mutation-based candidates for the specification audit.
- Counterexamples in diagnostics.
